the commission's proposal for a regulation on the establishment of a european regional development fund is a very good one. ten years ago, when the maastricht treaty was being negotiated, the climate seemed to me more favourable to the regional package than it is today. there is even a counter-movement within the european convention. i am very concerned about the draft constitutional treaty submitted by valéry giscard d'estaing. it contains no reference to any sub-national government and is in many ways a step backwards. all this, ladies and gentlemen, is happening against the background of enlargement. many of the states that will be joining are smaller than the existing regions and nations with legislative powers. the applicant countries will soon be fully involved in european integration. it is only fair that nations such as flanders, wallonia, the basque country, catalonia, wales, scotland and galicia should be able to do the same. mr napolitano's report comes at an opportune time, a few weeks before the debate on this subject in the convention on 6 and 7 february. i fully support the strengthened version of the napolitano report as mr maccormick has put it. commissioner barnier and his colleague méndez de vigo both endorsed the proposal to give constitutional regions direct access to the court of justice. they are, as i understand it, in favour of controlled access under the authority of the member state concerned. colleagues, this trick is too transparent. in practice, for example, it means that the three historical nations, the basque country, catalonia and galicia, are more or less denied access to the court of justice by the centralist powers in madrid. as a representative of flanders, one of the historical regions which has achieved quasi-autonomy without violence, i would ask you not to follow the path of mr méndez de vigo.
mr president, i am very pleased that mrs swiebel's report gives an account of the human rights situation in the european union and in the member states themselves. i consider this to be a cornerstone of the work of this parliament because it allows us to take a salutary step back, which is so rare here, and to envisage a clear, coherent policy, of precise actions, to achieve the ends that we so often claim. i will not be insulting you by quoting the various conventions and treaties we must respect, nor by reiterating our desire to anchor the charter of fundamental rights in the european conscience in an unconditional way, to affirm the freedoms and rights of european citizens. but every year when we report on human rights, when we deplore violations, when we adopt resolutions, we are talking only about third countries. so how can we be credible and enforce these rights in our relations with these countries, if we ourselves do not respect them? some of my colleagues will be irritated at the idea of naming certain countries in this report. let's not cover our faces either. as democrats, we are representatives of our citizens; we are not protectors of the policies of our national governments. we must say in this chamber that the defence of fundamental rights and freedoms in the european union is not just litany; it touches all the acts of life, of all human beings, wherever they are, outside of any free zone. the defence of human rights is a duty and a right which cannot be opposed by the principle of non-interference in the internal affairs of states. the report is therefore useful for the purpose of establishing the facts. it is up to us to act if we are not satisfied with this reality, so that these fundamental rights are a daily reality in our own member states. i would like to conclude on two points: firstly, i am very happy that economic and social rights are being discussed at european level and i think that the convention should be inspired by this. secondly, i would ask our member states to do what is necessary to ensure that clear statistics, broken down by sex, are drawn up in all areas and given to our next rapporteurs.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. but it is clear that in such a european market, where more players are coming into play, there must be a good safety regime. security is good at the moment. the railway is a safe means of transport, it must remain at least that good, and wherever possible we must ensure that the level of safety is even higher. the directive we are now drawing up is a first step towards that european approach to security. there are still too many different rules and regulations at the moment, which in many cases make the railway market in europe too difficult. we must therefore develop a uniform procedure for the various member states in this directive. first, there is structure. who's responsible for what? the railway companies are of course responsible for what they do and as a parliament we add the infrastructure managers to that. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of the road is not compromised. i want everyone to know what's going on. however, as far as the operational aspect is concerned, i believe that the know-how, knowledge and experience of the existing railway companies should be able to be used by those safety authorities. secondly, we must have a common approach to security, a common security objective. we in parliament believe that once this objective has been set, it should be re-submitted to the council and parliament and not just assessed by a committee of national experts. we must not only have a common objective but also a common method of working and i am a little more stringent than you in discouraging new national rules. we must be as strict as possible in this respect and the commission must have the last word. we must also - and i am a little more ambitious than the commission in this - set a european end-goal and say: the final objective is a single european regulation. we may not be ready for that tomorrow, but we should at least say where we are going. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of the railway network is not compromised. as far as staff is concerned, i think it is important to have a european driving licence for train drivers. we have to work on that. communication is also important, and i have added that to my report. when you cross language boundaries, you also have to make sure that you solve language problems. the way that is done is up to the agency. this is an important point, however. thirdly, we must work towards a common way of investigating accidents, of investigating incidents, an independent investigative body and criteria to be respected in those investigations. i think we have struck a balance between the most european approach possible and the most independent supervisors possible, but the theoretical model must also be workable in practice. the knowledge that is available in the old monopolies cannot be simply discarded, but must be used to create a workable system. i am convinced that we are in a first phase here, that this is a first step. we will have to talk about safety a few more times in the next 10, 15, 20 years, we will have to change and adjust guidelines. the structure we are setting up now will also have to prove its worth in practice and we must ensure that we are flexible enough to admit our mistakes and possibly do something about them. i also think we have struck a good balance in terms of the involvement of the staff concerned. it is important that the people who work on the railway are involved in the matters that concern them. safety is also a human endeavour and the people responsible for it must be involved in decision-making. i think that is also clearly stated in the directive. we are therefore taking an important step towards a european railway world with mrs ainardi's report on technical barriers and mr savary's report on the railway agency, which will be very important in the future because it will have to do a great deal of the work. the report by mr jarzembowski is also an important step forward. we need open, dynamic, commercial railway companies that think european and not just national or local. the culture of national monopoly is still very much alive and too often hinders the proper functioning of the european railway. we need - and mr jarzembowski and the commissioner have said this - a dynamic railway industry. if you look at how the transportation economy is developing, you can't do without railway companies. it is a shock to my heart to hear people in the railway industry say that if we do not adapt quickly we will have no freight traffic on the railways, we will be priced out of the market, not by the prices themselves but by the poor way of working. the railway is a sustainable means of transport, so let's take care of it. i think that with our reports and with your proposals, commissioner, we are taking an important step forward, because we must take a step forward towards a european transport market.
- mr president, i would first like to thank all the colleagues and rapporteurs. the aim of all reforms must be to achieve a sustainable transport policy in europe. liberalisation can sometimes be a means to this end, but never an end in itself. the extremely bad situation in maritime transport should indeed be a warning to us. the transport of goods in europe has become a problem because of the enormous increase in the number of lorries on our roads. i myself see an early liberalisation of rail freight transport as a means of reducing the number of lorries on the road, provided that this liberalisation is adequately accompanied. we are not in favour of liberalisation as an end in itself, and therefore we do not support liberalisation of passenger transport. passenger transport by rail is a public service and must remain so. the commission has also proposed that the commission should be given the task of drawing up a report on the implementation of the directive. the ill effects of liberalization are already being felt by the traveller. international lines outside the high-speed network are being cut. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's transport policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. the problem is that the brussels-strasbourg rail link is not very good, as i was able to see yesterday. the european commission has not done enough research on the effects of liberalisation on rail transport. we believe that an evaluation is essential before any further steps towards liberalization are taken. until we have a clear picture of the consequences of this liberalization in terms of working conditions, safety, the environment, the effective improvement of the supply to consumers and the piece is a railway bureau to guide the whole harmonization process and to get the savary report on track. the times when the container is being transported by rail at a speed of 18 km per hour from the north sea port to northern italy must be forgotten as soon as possible. the sterckx, savary and ainardi reports emphasize cooperation and consultation between different companies. of course, these companies could have done this themselves, but apparently they only take action when competition is threatened. europe should have organised this internal rail market much earlier. that railroad office should have been here 13 years ago. the free movement of goods was not organized in a sustainable way in the 20th century. the 21st century must urgently change that. commission. - (es) mr president, first of all, as commissioner for transport, i would like to thank you for allowing me to present this proposal on social matters in the road transport sector, which is part of the commission's policy to increase road safety and the quality of road transport. there are social aspects which have consequences for the safety of the road transport sector, and i understand that we must deal with them. the proposal includes measures to promote the effective and uniform interpretation and application of the current rules, as announced in the white paper, which will further level the playing field in the road transport sector. this proposal repeals and replaces the 1985 regulation. it aims to simplify, clarify and update legislation that was adopted 17 years ago. this amendment promotes the effective application of the rules and, in particular, the introduction of the digital tachograph. in order to simplify these rules, the degree of flexibility of the existing regulation has had to be reduced, complex rules on compensation for reduced rest periods have been eliminated and the duration of the daily rest has been increased by one hour to 12 hours. in order to update the rules, exemptions and exceptions have been minimised and limited to small and light vehicles, those used on short distances and those providing a public service. one of the main factors motivating this review has been the increase over the last 17 years in the number of services offered by private companies. in order to ensure more effective enforcement of the rules, the commission wants to introduce some new aspects into the rules, including the reinforcement of the rules on the liability of employers and explicit provisions to facilitate the imposition of penalties in one member state on drivers who infringe community rules in another member state of the european union. in addition, an explicit provision is introduced for all member states to adopt vehicle impoundment as one of the penalties for breach of the law. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the transport of dangerous goods is not subject to unreasonable and unlawful delays. finally, in order to encourage the constant clarification of the rules, a fruitful exchange of experience and practice in respecting them and the active participation of all the member states in the application of the regulation, the commission proposes the creation of an advisory committee composed of national experts from each of the member states of the union. this committee will work together with the existing sector dialogue committee at european level. in short, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, this is our proposal, the main purpose of which is to improve compliance with the rules currently in force, to clarify their content, to increase responsibility and, from there, to improve road safety.
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. the commission has already made a number of proposals. the initial situation for parliament's consideration of the report was a difficult one, because the commission had prepared its proposal for this regulation without waiting for the outcome of the conciliation committee on the working time directive, nor the council decision on the digital tachograph. the two documents - as mrs palacio has said - are of course closely linked in content to the present report. i would like to thank mr andersson, who drew up the opinion in the committee on employment and social affairs. we had some discussions in advance. the commission has also proposed that the commission should be given the task of drawing up a report on the implementation of the programme. i would also like to thank the commission staff, mr darmis from the committee's administration, the many european and national institutions which are affected by this regulation and have actively participated in the debate, as well as the shadow rapporteurs from the other groups. i believe that this cooperation has enabled us to adopt some important compromise proposals together in the committee. this regulation must take account of and integrate three aspects. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve road safety, to improve the social protection of workers and to ensure fair conditions of competition on the roads of the european union. i believe that a compromise has been found between the groups and that these three premises have been integrated, despite the fact that some members have asked for the regulation to be rejected. what are the main premises on which the committee has been able to reach agreement by a majority? firstly, the regulation applies to all drivers of vehicles for the carriage of goods and passengers, whether they are employed or selfemployed. secondly, the regulation also applies to vehicles from third countries when they are on the territory of the european union. thirdly, the commission is instructed to bring this regulation into the negotiations with the aetr contracting parties. fourthly, the concept of the flexible week, which the commission has recently introduced, has not been confirmed, but it remains the basis of the calendar week, since this is more practical and easier to control. fifthly, the committee has defined the term 'management period' in order to achieve greater legal certainty. sixthly - and this is a very important point, and i hope it will remain so - all courier and express services which have contributed to a sharp increase in accidents in the transport sector will be covered by the regulation. seventhly, the daily rest period has been increased from the proposed 11 hours to 12, but with the possibility of flexibility by a limited division into two periods of nine plus three hours. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the directive is implemented in a uniform manner. the number of checks to ensure compliance with the regulation has been doubled. ten: transport companies were obliged to take appropriate measures to obtain information on the total working time of drivers, even if they were employed by several employers. the commission has already taken the necessary steps to ensure that the relations between shippers, freight forwarders, main contractors and subcontractors are regulated by the adoption of binding contracts which make it possible to verify compliance with this regulation. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that is why a number of amendments have been tabled in the plenary, and i would like to see them adopted. this applies in particular to the decidedly excessive driving time of 56 hours per week, but with a limit of no more than 90 hours in two weeks. we propose a maximum of 45 hours of driving per week, which is still considerably above the weekly hours in almost all other sectors of employment. secondly, i would like, as do many of my colleagues, to see a further reduction in the exceptions to this regulation. i know that this regulation does not meet all the wishes of all those concerned, but i am convinced that the new rules will increase road safety, improve social conditions for those concerned and enable undertakings to perform their task of providing transport services in a high quality manner.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for his work and also my colleagues for the many coordination meetings. with 300 modifications, the work was not easy. the main question raised in this context was the question of the need for a new directive. the priorities have been different, and colleagues in the group of the european people's party (christian democrats) and the european democrats will be speaking on this today. the epp group is, however, in principle in favour of a change. the aim was to achieve a synthesis between road safety on the one hand and flexibility in this profession, which is already in great demand throughout europe today, perhaps because of the unfavourable conditions but also because of the high demand. this was a challenge for us. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the first is the controls. all colleagues agree that we can adopt the best directives here, but if there are no effective controls in the member states, this will not help. less than one percent, as is the case in some countries, is not enough. the two per cent mentioned here as a minimum should be respected. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it cannot be, as the frenchman says, that the principle is: "celui qui triche gagne le plus d'argent", that is, the one who does not follow these guidelines earns the most money. that is precisely what we want to prevent: unfair competition at the expense of safety and other things. secondly, there was unanimity on the scope of the directive, namely that we are including third countries, that is to say, we want to create a european transport area and everyone who uses that area must abide by the same rules. we know that companies move to third countries for various reasons, not only economic but also other reasons. that is why it is important for us to create this european transport area over time. we wanted to build flexibility. exceptions have been discussed. i do not want to quote them all, of course. but we continue to insist that public transport and taxi drivers are excluded, and of course we support exceptions in certain areas, such as farmers who do seasonal work, as well as breakdown services. the concept of security must not lead us to restrict the people who are responsible for security in and of themselves by means of the directive. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. some say they should be in, others think that the many accidents are not a matter of rest and driving times, but rather a matter of speed. we also introduced flexibility in the breaks. we wanted not just the 24 to 45 hour weekends, but the 36 hours. we also want to take account of the countries outside central europe, because tomorrow, with enlargement, we must take into account that the centre is moving and that it will not be just a question of driving and resting times, whether or not you are efficient in the transport business. the 12 hours of sleep are not in question here either, with the associated flexibility of nine plus three hours. this also applies to the daily breaks and short breaks. we wanted a certain flexibility, which would take account of the work rhythm of the individual drivers and the needs of the profession. safety was the other thing that needed to be synthesized. rest breaks, daytime rest, weekend rest are safety issues, and overtired drivers involved in accidents are not unknown. but this aspect should not be exaggerated. according to certain statistics, the risk is very high. overall, it is assumed that realistic statistics show that it affects about 15% of serious road accidents. the problem is not that we are condemning the road in all its aspects. the commission and parliament's initiative here will, i believe, improve security. as far as violations are concerned, we also believe that closure is the best course of action. this is not contrary to the legislation in the various countries and only one thing is required, namely that the carriage be stopped until the fault is corrected. in short, i would say that this directive enables us to intervene in this area in an economically positive way by introducing stricter rules and new requirements. this will not change the fact that road transport will continue to be one of the most important elements in transport in general. i'm going to go back to the discussions we had earlier. this does not mean that if we want to achieve a shift, it must be achieved only by road transport policies, but it must also be accompanied by clear efforts in other transport sectors. thank you for your attention.

commission. – let me emphasise that kosovo's institutional set-up, including the distribution of responsibilities, has been defined by the united nations security council and must be respected by all investment. there is still a long way to go but we should not forget from which level we started. furthermore, the commission has succeeded in letting kosovo benefit from the various instruments of the european union's stabilisation and association process, even without a decision on its future status, to ensure that it does not fall behind in the region. unmik is also currently consulting with various international financial organisations on how to give kosovo access to their funds in the absence of a decision on its final status. in the framework of the stability pact, unmik is exploring ways of embedding kosovo in the process of regional trade liberalisation and has already signed the memorandum of understanding on the regional electricity market in south-east europe. in short, we have gone a very long way to find creative ways to improve kosovo's economic situation and we will continue to do so. our efforts and those of the united nations qualify the imaginative policy that in writing. - citizenship is inseparable from participation in the political life of a state. it is a sign of belonging to a particular group, not only in the strictly legal sense but also in a very practical sense. voting at the local level is one way to participate. third country nationals can already participate in the life of the city in the european union in a number of ways. the right to vote at local level is an additional right, but it also entails responsibilities. thus, the granting of the right to vote in local elections to third-country nationals who can prove a residence of at least five years must involve a voluntary commitment on their part. it must confirm adherence to democratic values and human rights as enshrined, inter alia, in national constitutions and laws, as well as in the european convention on human and citizen's rights of 1950, protecting in particular the freedom of philosophical and religious belief, or prohibiting any form of discrimination based on religion, sex or race. citizenship must be defined by a mode of civic behaviour as well as by active and daily participation in the life of society.

- madam president, i come from a former colonial country, belgium, whose emotional ties with the democratic republic of congo have never been severed. it was with despair that i saw it plunge into war and with relief that i welcomed the pretoria accords, a promise of peace and democratization in this martyred region. the natural resources of the congo are exceptional. in 1997, the value of the resources in the north was estimated at $25 billion. the ores of zinc, lead, iron, tungsten, cadmium, cobalt, germanium are unique, and germanium has semiconducting properties that are used today in the world's most powerful computer chip. not to mention radium, lithium, niobium, which give superconducting alloys at high temperatures. and i pass the diamond, the silver, the platinum, and so on. it is probably the country with the largest natural resources in the world. that is why, in addition to the neighbouring countries concerned, the predators of the west - it must be said - have rushed upon it under all kinds of pretexts, exterminated its population and waged a western war through african intermediaries. it's a real holocaust. the figures are 4.5 million congolese victims since 1998, 2.5 million displaced persons, 16 million deaths in suspense, thousands of people deported to rwanda and uganda, and i prefer not to mention the details of the massacres that have taken place, details which are intolerable. that is to say, i am in favour of this resolution. and if it turns out that my country, as suggested by a panel of experts, harbours these predators that fuel the war, belgium will not fail in its responsibilities. it will conduct the necessary investigations and ensure that they are followed up. it will not add to the burden of past colonization the complicity of the present. but there is a condition for this: last week's un resolution on the same subject stresses that the panel of experts is not a judicial authority and that, therefore, the results it presents cannot be considered as established facts and therefore cannot automatically lead to sanctions. the rights of the defence must be preserved.
- mr president, as a belgian, i find this resolution particularly interesting because the congo was a belgian colony. but other countries in old and new europe are also guilty of plundering africa's natural resources. i can't believe this is still going on. it is not the old mother countries anymore, but european companies are mentioned in the un report, rightly or wrongly. in any case, europe must take action because we have a collective historical responsibility. i just got back from the world social forum in porto alegre, brazil. i heard the africans there too. we expect a lot from europe, including a kind of wiedergutmachung. the belgian senate has set up a committee of inquiry into the illegal trade in raw materials in the great lakes region. the main demand from europe is that the commission proposes a directive on the control of european companies operating outside the union and that this directive is quickly transposed into law by each member state. on the other hand, a control mechanism must be set up for the extraction of raw materials in the congo. the united nations has a more urgent mission here than in iraq.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the spread of the disease. since september, aid agencies have been warning of a full-scale disaster. it is significant that even this parliament is not paying attention to this until five months later. nevertheless, i welcome this resolution. in addition to the immediate causes of drought and war, the deeper causes were also considered: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. two concerns. of course, the rich countries must be more generous and more rapid in providing the means to alleviate the distress. but this food aid must not be used as a political tool by regimes like zimbabwe to evade responsibility and perpetuate their power. secondly, the famine must not be used to impose genetically modified food. the european union rightly places high demands on the authorization of gmos. in africa, the uncontrolled dumping of gmos can have far-reaching consequences, especially for the environment, agriculture and food safety. even grinding genetically modified grain is not a waterproof solution. by the time the food arrived in zimbabwe, the stores had been looted. the claim that there is not enough unadulterated food available is not true. india has 65 million tons of grain immediately available. during the cold war, food aid was already a political and commercial weapon. we cannot allow it to be used today to create a market for western biotech companies that will bind the farmers in the developing countries to them forever.

in writing. - this report and this proposal for a directive are an important step towards the realisation of a fundamental element of european citizenship, namely the effective freedom of movement of persons within the european union. i would like to make one criticism, however, on one aspect which has unfortunately not been taken into account. the court held that the principle of equal treatment for men and women was not infringed by the fact that the right to equal treatment for women was not conferred on them by the treaty. these people will continue to be at a disadvantage compared with nationals when crossing an internal border of the european union, as they will then have to carry a passport or identity card issued by the state of which they are nationals, which implies additional administrative procedures and costs. it is regrettable that the opportunity to put an end to this injustice has not been seized, either by providing for a system of european identity cards or by providing for the permanent residence card to be considered equivalent to an identity card when crossing the internal borders of the european union.
- (fr) mr president, madam commissioner, i note that there are many dutch speakers here, but that is perhaps no coincidence, because in the low countries there is a hit, a schlager, and i quote the text: "with my 30 tons of diesel, i'm tearing through the brenner pass, far from home but in my coffin". the people in austria and the alpine region are not mentioned. whether they were in their prime or not is absolutely irrelevant to this song, but it is relevant to this report. this report is a symbolic dossier for european transport policy. the interest goes beyond austria and the whole alpine region. it is a lever of sustainable development in european transport policy. the greens/efa group therefore calls, in amendments nos 19, 20 and 21, for a rapid, lasting and comprehensive solution to the ecological, economic, social and safety problems caused by traffic through the alps for decades and known throughout the european union. the end of the austrian eco-points system can only be accepted if there is a different kind of protection for the whole alpine region and not just for austria, based on the alpine convention. there must be no time vacuum in this protection. there must also be a limit on the number of lorries crossing the alps. without a clear numerical objective, it all makes little sense. we therefore call for a permanent limit of 8% growth above the 1991 figure. we cannot approve this report unless a solution is found for the whole alpine region in the medium term.
mr president, commissioner, the white paper has opened up a stimulating debate, capable of a strategic vision, which allows us to look at the objective of creating those conditions for mobility, of things and of people, which are essential to truly integrate the enlarged europe, to pursue its territorial cohesion, all while avoiding any waste of land and reducing any undesirable effects on the environment, on the safety and health of citizens. it is a goal of enormous proportions, and the horizon of 2010 seems far away, but, given the timing of the necessary structural and infrastructural adjustments, we can say that we are already far behind. in this respect, the fact that the transport council is holding back progress on the strategic dossier - and on the others - is worrying, while we can only welcome the commission's emphasis on all these issues. it is in this direction, with this aim, that i think it is worthwhile to continue to investigate even those concepts which seem still theoretical, not yet refined, such as that of decoupling, of separation or of the attempt to reduce the transport impact of development, which nobody certainly wants to limit, because this implies exploring collateral policies in the area of territorial organization and in the area of the optimization of existing infrastructures through the use of new technologies. equally important is the contribution to balancing the choice of modes by internationalising external costs and by exploiting differences between tariffs and internal costs, including for the cross-use of more compatible modes of transport. these are hot, difficult issues, and there is a risk of getting bogged down in them, but they are crucial, decisive for the issues we are facing. i hope that, as has been said, the commission's communication on the methodology for charging external costs will be published soon, which will also allow us to provide for a fairer allocation of transport costs than is currently the case, by replacing the general tariffs we are currently paying with appropriate and specific tariffs.

- mr president, on the eve of a possible conflict in iraq, which we hope can be avoided, a debate on the use of anti-personnel mines and depleted uranium munitions is particularly timely. in fact, in previous military conflicts, such as the gulf war in 1991 and in kosovo, soldiers and civilians who had been exposed to depleted uranium munitions later suffered serious health problems, the so-called gulf or balkan syndrome. they suffered from memory loss, chronic pain and fatigue, paralysis, or an increased risk of cancer. united nations statistics published in the prestigious medical journal "the british medical journal" show that the number of cancer cases in southern iraq increased sevenfold between 1989 and 1994. even the u.s. army environmental policy institute has acknowledged the potentially serious medical consequences of using depleted uranium. weapons and ammunition targeting civilians are in violation of the geneva convention and the convention on conventional weapons. the latter is very clear. it provides for a prohibition or restriction on the use of certain conventional weapons which are considered to cause excessive suffering or which do not distinguish between military and civilian targets. according to leaked pentagon data, the first wave of attacks in iraq would have fired some 3,000 guided bombs. the chances of the us using depleted uranium munitions again are high. as a european parliament we must continue to warn of the possible dangers of using this type of ammunition. there is an urgent need for independent scientific research into the consequences of their use, not only for health but also for the environment. pending its results, a moratorium on the use of depleted uranium should be imposed. the european union must take the lead here, as it did in the quest for a global ban on anti-personnel mines, as enshrined in the ottawa treaty. and my questions, mr president, concern that treaty, its ratification and its application. i would like to ask the council specifically what it intends to do to ensure that as many member states and candidate countries as possible sign this convention. you will understand that i would also have liked to hear from the president-in-office of the council what greece is actually waiting for in order to sign this important treaty. finally, with a view to hopefully avoiding a possible international conflict, i would urge a clear statement from the council guaranteeing that european member states will not use weapons which are prohibited or considered illegal under international law in present or future armed conflicts.
mr president, depleted uranium has been used extensively in the manufacture of shells and anti-tank munitions which were dumped in large quantities during the gulf war on iraqi territory, but also in bosnia and kosovo. on impact, the uranium is volatilized into fine particles which are liable to be inhaled by humans or dispersed into the environment, where they may, by the action of wind, be suspended and eventually inhaled as well. these particles are emitters of alpha radioactivity, which is particularly aggressive to living tissue. the particles of depleted uranium that are deposited in the body can seriously damage the health not only of military personnel but also of civilians. it seems clear to me that depleted uranium weapons and munitions are directly covered by the 1980 un convention on weapons of mass destruction. the un subcommission on human rights made this statement several years ago. however, no restrictive or prohibitive measures have been taken to date. thus, scientific uncertainties about measurable health effects are used as an argument for doing nothing. the precautionary principle would require an additional protocol to the 1980 convention to explicitly prohibit such weapons. that is what my group is asking for. last week we were in iraq and in the hospital in basra we saw with horror and sadness children with cancer and birth defects. the figures released by the university of basra for the region are frightening. between 1990 and 2001, the rate of congenital malformations has been steadily increasing, in this case sevenfold. the parents' exposure to depleted uranium is the logical factor scientists have put into question. we cannot allow situations like this to happen again, which are the consequences of real war crimes. however, in afghanistan, contrary to official denials, even more powerful weapons, coated with depleted uranium and even natural uranium, were used. these are remote-controlled bombs and cruise missiles. i think it is up to the european union to take the initiative to stop the current escalation and to set the stage for a definitive ban on weapons which, like cluster bombs and antipersonnel mines, are morally unacceptable.

commission. – mr president, the european steel industry has been through a difficult period over the last 20 years. there was a need for an overall restructuring of the sector. nowadays the european steel industry is very competitive. in order to maintain their global position, however, companies need to engage in a continuous process of adapting their structures. the industry is still striving for efficiency, but even if remaining overcapacities are closed down, from an industrial point of view, we cannot talk about a real crisis on the scale that we had in the 1980s, but rather about a social crisis due to new closures. restructuring is often a synonym for job losses which can have tragic consequences on the social fabric of any region, but in particular on those regions facing a low job creation potential. the commission is acutely aware of the need for citizens and workers to feel secure in taxing times. we have observed again in recent weeks extremely different experiences and approaches on how to deal with large-scale operations of corporate restructuring. in some cases, these operations are prepared well in advance, fully involving workers' representatives, with due care being taken to search for less damaging solutions, and seeking actively and in good time to prepare workers likely to be affected to face the challenge of sudden unemployment. in other cases these operations were launched and implemented with no care or concern at all for those affected. on every occasion in the past, the commission has always highlighted the following basic principles. firstly, decisions on corporate restructuring remain a prerogative, as well as a responsibility, of management. this means that when contemplating actions likely to have serious social consequences, companies should always actively search and choose the less damaging solutions. secondly, when dismissals prove to be inevitable, the utmost must be done to improve the employability of those concerned. this requires anticipation and adequate management of those processes. thirdly, nothing can justify the absence of timely and effective information and consultation with workers' representatives. these basic assertions attract a wide consensus in europe. it is, thankfully, increasingly rare to see companies embarking on damaging restructuring operations, ignoring these fundamental concerns, but it does sometimes happen. the way in which metaleurop conducted the closure of the pas de calais plant is the worst possible example of disregard for the interests, the concerns and the rights of workers, as well as for the health and safety of the surrounding population. it is, of course, fair and necessary to underline other approaches: at the other extreme, arcelor is also facing a restructuring process with unavoidable pain for its workers, but is tackling it differently. the commission does not wish to interfere in the economic logic behind the management's intentions, it is the management's role to do that in close consultation with workers' representatives. arcelor took a number of positive and productive steps, well before the recent announcement measures had already commenced, to anticipate the problems. again recently, the management reaffirmed its intention to enter into effective consultations with workers' representatives at all levels. the consultation process encompasses: the economic reasoning behind the projected closures; taking independent expert advice on the whole affair; taking all possible measures to avoid the closures or limit their social impact; and seeking to clean the local environment and re-industrialise the closed plants. this extensive programme is serious and is considered to be so by all stakeholders. it is the result of a different kind of industrial relations which have led, for instance, to an agreement on transnational information and consultation of workers that is widely recognised as being very advanced and effective. the three seats on the board for workers' representatives, although there is currently no legal obligation for this, also illustrate that same positive approach. this in fact anticipates the european company statute. we have built over recent years in the european union a wide range of instruments that meet these concerns. the directives on collective redundancies, transfers of undertakings, information and consultation and the european works councils directive are some examples. the most recent texts clearly promote upstream worker involvement, notably on strategic issues and on the foreseeable evolution of employment within companies. this is the only way to help the european workforce face the challenges of change successfully in that it allows for the timely development of the employability and adaptability of workers. the two cases i mentioned before can only confirm those assertions, one positively and one negatively. it is up to national courts and other authorities to ensure compliance with community directives. the commission will, of course, fulfil its ultimate role of control of the application of community law if breaches are not effectively sanctioned. those aspects will constitute some of the main elements of the forthcoming revision of the european works councils directive. more importantly, the commission very much hopes that the european social partners identify and find the means of developing good practices of corporate restructuring throughout the eu, as they decided to do when responding positively to last year's consultation on this issue. those good practices go well beyond the information and consultation of workers. they cover actions with a view to anticipating the market and technological developments, investing in people on a permanent basis, developing employability, seeking alternatives to closures and redundancies, redeploying, whenever possible, workers affected by restructuring operations and so on - precisely those good practices which i hope will help arcelor's workers overcome the present difficulties, and which are dramatically absent in the metaleurop environment. it is also useful to recall in this context other initiatives of the commission relating to corporate social responsibility, social dialogue and the european monitoring centre on change. besides those policy aspects, our immediate concern today is the recent cases of restructuring. the commission would like to state clearly that, above all, we share your regret and concern on the possible social consequences for those who may be affected. i sincerely hope that every effort is made to prevent or attenuate them through dialogue between all parties concerned.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i must say to the commissioner that i found his speech extremely disappointing. he expressed a set of general considerations on a situation with which we are well acquainted. these considerations are concerned with business restructuring in a number of sectors, but they do not specifically concern the problems we are experiencing today in several regions. i must say that i was very surprised to hear the commissioner condemn the behaviour of a company which everyone considers to have been particularly scandalous, namely metaleurope, and at the same time praise another company which has been in the headlines in recent months, arcelor. the decision by arcelor to phase out its hot-rolled steel plants on the mainland was, of course, met with dismay in the regions concerned. it was also greeted with anger, particularly in the liège region, as it abruptly broke the commitment made by usinor before its merger into the new arcelor group to invest in the liège blast furnaces in order to ensure the maintenance of hot-water production lines. this commitment was matched by a reciprocal commitment by the workers to make productivity efforts to ensure the competitiveness of the site. the workers' commitments were kept but those of usinor are now being denounced and the workers feel deceived. that is why all the forces in the region, but also the walloon government and the belgian federal government, are now calling arcelor to account. it seems to me important that the european commission should be part of this same perspective. the resolution we have tabled together has as its primary objective to ensure that arcelor meets its commitments. arcelor must keep its commitments, especially since you have indicated - and this is an important element of your statement - that the steel industry was not in a crisis situation, that companies in general were competitive companies and that there was therefore no reason for arcelor, except to pursue a pure and simple financial logic, to want to go back on the commitments that were made. it is not a question of first imagining conversion processes with cockerill-sambre first and usinor later. the liège region has already suffered and paid a heavy price from the steel crisis. what is needed today to ensure a conversion is time. what we do not accept at the level of the liège region is that commitments are broken which shorten the deadlines we had set ourselves and, therefore, we clearly ask the commission to remind arcelor of its commitments. we will also have to ask ourselves about the behaviour of arcelor, as soon as we learn that this company intends to acquire a number of sites in a candidate country which will be part of europe tomorrow. this raises a number of questions at european level. mr president, i should like to begin by reminding the honourable commission spokesman that the commission is the heir to the high authority of the european coal and steel community, which was not created to organise the burial of industrial activity in europe, nor to catalogue the various ways of burying industrial activity in our regions, nor to play the social red cross with workers and regions that are suffering cruelly. that is to say, we, like mr hansenne, believe more than ever that a statement from the commission was necessary, but we also believe that it is largely inadequate. this is particularly true of me, because i have provided the commission with evidence of arcelor's willingness to intervene in poland, on continental sites, when it is being said in three european regions that continental sites are by their nature destined to disappear. i shall say no more, mr president, to allow other members to speak.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should also like to stress the first point which seems to me to be essential: the commission's statement is an unworthy statement. the commission's responsibility today is indeed to embody the role of the public authorities within the framework of the powers it has inherited from the high authority of the ecsc. in addition, it should be recalled at the outset that the commission must not be a spectator, but a public actor, in the management of steel at the level of the european union. the resolution before us has four main themes which we consider to be essential. i would like the commission to answer four specific questions in relation to these four themes. firstly, in view of the fact that european firmness is necessary for a fair distribution of steel production worldwide, will the commission ensure that the polish government is in no way able to intervene financially in industrial processes to support any arcelor group project in poland? secondly, does the commission consider that it still has full competence to guarantee that the european union chooses and maintains a steel industry, or is it simply waiting for this industry to disappear? in other words, is the commission still fully aware that it still has a responsibility under article 3 of the ecsc treaty, or has it forgotten that article? thirdly, as regards the elements of regional conversion and social support, is the commission prepared to use all the european instruments now in a programme negotiated with the competent authority, namely the walloon regional authority in belgium, so that any aid is concentrated on social support and conversion, while ensuring that no state aid is paid in other regions? fourthly, what steps does the commission intend to take to ensure that what already exists today in terms of european social law is respected before any decision is taken by any group? this includes compliance with a number of collective agreements. in the same vein, is the commission prepared to invoke article 5 of the ecsc treaty on the subject of company law, which states that the commission is responsible for ensuring that social activity is maintained when general production at european level is not in danger? mr president, europe must now, through the commission, show commercial firmness vis-à-vis the rest of the world and make the industrial choice of social voluntarism and useful conversion. finally, the commission must exercise its role as a public authority so that social and business law at european level can become a reality. i am glad to hear the commission's reply and i hope that the speech which follows the speeches of the members will not be of the general character agreed upon in the first speech of the commissioner.
- mr president, commissioner, the steel crisis and, in particular, the recent decisions of arcelor are not a belgian regional problem, but a european problem. it is not necessary to live at the foot of a blast furnace to feel the full human impact of the decisions that have been taken, of the way in which they have been taken, and to be aware of their economic impact which will go far beyond the liège region and will even cross borders. but this is not the only element that makes it possible to highlight the european, not just the regional, aspect of the kind of problem that is being raised here. this is not a problem unique to the steel industry. it was a spirit of enterprise in which financial profitability was given priority over real qualifications, proximity and, above all, respect for the commitments made in favour of the sustainable development of europe's regions. therefore, if we respond to this kind of development with relatively soft statements, we confirm our inability, on the one hand, to enforce a basic social right and, on the other hand, to control or even prohibit the use of public funds to create cheap jobs at the expense of necessarily more expensive quality jobs. the action we can take today, beyond promises and declarations, lies in respecting the right to control the use of public funds. but the events of the day also show that, in my view, we do not have enough resources. the question is: what are our ambitions for the europe of tomorrow? as we approach enlargement, it is vital that we define the social rules that the market will never define itself, if we are to avoid unfair and suicidal competition between the regions of europe. we must not forget that the financial rules which today help to empty some parts of europe to the benefit of others will empty europe tomorrow to the benefit of other parts of the world. the debate is therefore about the social future, but also about the economic future of europe. it is therefore urgent that national governments and parliaments, as well as the commission, should have effective instruments to manage both an economic and a social europe. the question of law enforcement and legislation to better manage the future is not a question of capacity but a question of political will. it is above all a question of overcoming the fatalism of liberalisation and certain regional and national egoisms, entrusting europe with what is best managed at its level, in order to have a strong economy but also a quality of work worthy of the social model we talk about all the time.

- the european union must indeed take the lead in the world trade organisation negotiations on the liberalisation of trade in services and the achievement of a general gats agreement. these negotiations are certainly more than crucial for europe. firstly, services are becoming an increasingly important part of world trade, of which the european union, as we know, represents 24%. in addition, the european union is today the world's largest importer and exporter of services. another reason for making these gats negotiations work is, of course, unfortunately, i would say, the continuing slowdown in economic growth. we must therefore concentrate more than ever on trade in services, and this is true again and particularly for the european union, where services account for more than two thirds of total gross domestic product. however, we must not only negotiate in our own interests, but also in the interests of other countries and other continents. liberalisation also means that we open our borders more widely to services from third countries and thus contribute to their further development.

in writing. - at first reading, parliament had tabled a series of amendments aimed at the recognition of crude vegetable oil (hvb or pure plant oil-ppo) as a biofuel. these various amendments were not included in the common position adopted by the council, and i therefore vote in favour of amendment no 7 by the committee responsible. the recognition of crude vegetable oil as a biofuel is appropriate in view of the many environmental benefits it provides, but also because decentralised production of oil on agricultural holdings would allow the farmers concerned to derive added value from their production. the main criticism of the use of hvb is the need to adapt diesel engines. this criticism can, however, be in favour of the hvb, since it is a whole economic sector that could thus be set up, with the economic and social consequences that are linked to it. in addition, engine adaptations are also necessary for other alternative fuels which benefit from the tax exemption: hydrogen, liquefied natural gas, electricity, etc.

the rapporteur. - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, although we are not dealing with legislative proposals in this debate, their importance cannot be underestimated. the green paper on consumer protection - and i shall confine myself to this in my first five minutes, and i shall deal with mr whitehead's question in the two minutes i have left - is not only of legal and economic importance, but also of political importance. it can help to dispel the myth that the single market is only for business and that consumers do not really benefit from it. a good consumer law is a key instrument for bringing the european union closer to the citizen, and it is our common task to make it work. another misunderstanding i would like to dispel is the alleged and perpetuated contradiction between the completion of the internal market and the establishment of a high level of consumer protection. both are objectives of the treaty and can be mutually supportive. it is our duty to do so, but i regret to say that the division of tasks between the committees of parliament does not always help us in this respect. i think this is a matter which we will have to look at again in the next legislature. thirdly, there is another entrenched notion which i want to get rid of, namely the belief held by many that consumer law and fair trade rules are completely separate from each other from a competitive point of view. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that will lead to greater justice, especially for smes, greater legal certainty and greater legal stability, which is what we all want. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of consumers in respect of the sale of consumer goods is a very good one. but let us not be overconfident. it is not that everything that consumers keep on their own home market is an obstacle to the internal market that can be removed. there are also natural limits to market integration - they are listed in my report - and we must accept them. so the key is to identify the real obstacles and focus on them, and we won't miss our target. the new approach, if it comes, should, according to the legal affairs committee, aim at a high level of consumer protection - an objective of the treaty - sufficient flexibility, simplicity and transparency of legislation and also legislation of high legal quality. we therefore support the suggestion in the green paper to ensure that consumers have efficient and affordable dispute settlement, because that is part of it. we are not opposed to the idea of a framework directive either, commissioner, but as responsible legislators we would like to make sure in advance that such a framework directive, with all that entails, actually leads to more simplicity, more legal certainty and also to a more effective consumer policy. we therefore ask you to present us in advance with the full picture, that is to say both the framework directive and the accompanying proposals for directives. for reasons of legal certainty, we prefer a head clause based on a prohibition of unfair trading practices. of course, that prohibition must be well defined. we recognize the usefulness of the instrument of maximum harmonization, but, like mr whitehead in his report to the committee on the environment, we ask for caution and to work on a case-by-case basis, otherwise we risk going too far. in any case, we in the legal affairs committee are also convinced that maximum harmonization is not possible until a high level of consumer protection has been achieved, unless harmonization is aimed at this. a high level of consumer protection is also, in our view, a prerequisite for the full application of the principles of mutual recognition and the application of the principle of the country of origin. commissioner, we are in favour of a legal basis for a european code of conduct, provided that the conditions set out in paragraph 17 of my report are met. but since no one benefits from false feelings of legal certainty, we oppose any bureaucratic approval mechanism that can only give a rebuttable presumption of legality. the logic of the members of the legal affairs committee is that commitments entered into in codes should be enforceable. ladies and gentlemen, you will find that the patrie report of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection differs on many of these points from what we have said in the legal affairs committee. we in the legal affairs committee have tried to concentrate on the legal aspects of the matter. we made choices based on well-considered legal considerations. i would therefore ask the environment committee and the other members of this house to look at this again before voting and to ask for support for these points from the legal affairs committee. i would also like to thank the members for their very constructive cooperation and to say to mr whitehead that i will answer his specific question in the two minutes i have left.
the committee on legal affairs and the internal market has adopted a report on the proposal for a directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the protection of employees in the event of the insolvency of their employer. - mr president, i can actually be brief here and thank mr whitehead. in the legal affairs committee we have done our best to concentrate on the legal aspects of the strategic document on consumer policy and apparently mr whitehead and his colleagues in the environment committee have respected or appreciated that, because i have found that virtually all the points of our opinion have been taken up in the whitehead report. i can only express my gratitude for that. i have nothing further to say but to respond to the comment on mr whitehead's question just now on paragraph 15 of his resolution which is based on an original paragraph in my report. paragraph 15 deals with private international law. in my opinion i tried to say that when aspects of private international law are dealt with in consumer law, we should also be able to deal with them under article 95, the article on the internal market. in the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, article 153 has been added to that and i think that it can be retained. i do not think there can be much of a problem with that, mr whitehead. i suspect that it is more a problem of translation than a matter of substantial debate. i hope that i have clarified things and i would also like to give the presidency a half-minute to make up for what i said too much earlier.
