the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, for the past ten years there has been a broad consensus in europe to tackle the pesticide problem. there is also a broad consensus that the current legislative framework is inadequate and needs to be complemented by a community strategy on the sustainable use of pesticides. that is why i am very much in favour of all the commission's proposals in my report. the commission's proposals are good but they are, in my opinion, a little too unambitious and the commission is also a little too unambitious in its timetable. in view of the resistance we have all encountered as members of the european parliament in recent days to the report and the vote on it in committee, i would nevertheless like to take a moment to reflect on some of the challenges facing us on this issue. pesticides are a growing environmental and health problem. however, it is not just the so-called outdated stocks of pesticides, although it is important that we tackle this problem very accurately. but it 's about much more than that . our drinking water, our surface water, our groundwater, is increasingly contaminated with residues, and even rainwater today is not meeting the standards for drinking water. drinking water companies have to invest capital to get that waste out again. in addition, about half of our fresh food contains residues. the burden of medical evidence that pesticides are the cause of a host of welfare diseases should be of grave concern to us. pesticides cause cancer, nerve disease and fertility problems. if farmers in some sort of astronaut suit have to produce our food, shouldn't we start to ask ourselves if we should organize our food production differently? for all these reasons, and for many more reasons which i cannot list here because of the lack of time, i would like to emphasize in my report that, in addition to a strategy of risk reduction, a strategy of use reduction is necessary. a lasting solution in the long term also lies in a systematic reduction in the use of chemical agents in favour of preventive and other alternative measures. the problem of resistance, ladies and gentlemen, is not solved by using more, but by using less. i challenge you all to find a doctor who says that the problem of antibiotic resistance is solved by using more antibiotics. we know that is not the case, so let's face reality on that. a second point of concern, on which there is a consensus in this parliament, commissioner, is that we need a broader scope of the strategy. the agricultural sector is extremely important, but governments - particularly local authorities - amateur gardeners, industry and railways are also important users and it would be a shame if we did not include them in our strategy. we must not ignore them, nor the use of biocides. another difficult issue, and one which is very controversial, is the use of gmos. do we want to go from bad to worse, or do we want to go in a sustainable direction? because by far the majority of genetically modified crops are resistant to herbicides. the agrochemical companies have promised that the genetically modified crops would contribute significantly to reducing the use and risks of pesticides. i want to believe this, be open to it, but reality is wrong. these promises are not being fulfilled today, both in gmo soybeans, gmo maize and gmo beets, after a few years more herbicides are used than in conventional agriculture. in our report we ask that the precautionary principle be applied and that gmos not be tout courted as a safe and sustainable alternative. i ask no more and no less. then the cost. we have not yet made a proper calculation in europe of what the use of pesticides actually costs society. in the united states, the cost to society was estimated ten years ago to be between $2.5 billion and $4 billion a year. the question is: do we want to internalize that in the cost price, do we want to make the polluter pay? and then the use of taxes and levies comes up. colleagues, we all know that the current regulatory framework is inadequate. we are all asking for a few steps forward. i therefore ask my colleagues in parliament to support the report, which i know is very ambitious.
i am not going to go on for very long, mr president, but i do want to say something to the people who may be listening to this debate, who knows. it is very striking, it is the first time i have experienced that, that i have heard such a battery of epps all saying the same thing in a row. for those of you listening: there are other groups with different opinions in this parliament. that said, very briefly, i do want to help the world out of some of its prejudices. because there are those here who would love to put me in the corner of "a little out of touch, a little naive", "someone sitting in her little office working out some weird ideas". i have not done that, i have looked, i have consulted and i have seen, for example, that in a number of member states in the european union, such as the home country of our honourable commissioner, there has been an effective reduction which is very significant. what is possible in sweden is possible in the netherlands, in belgium and in the united kingdom. we can if we want to. i challenge anyone to look at my report. i'm not saying i'm against pesticides. i know they're needed, they're even desirable. but we could use less. of course, reducing consumption is complementary to reducing risk, which i believe is also more important, but because there is a consensus on this, i have not focused on it. i have always said in committee that i do not consider the 50% to be a fetish. maybe we should put 30 percent, maybe 40, maybe 60, but i want a target. we all know that if we do not set ourselves a goal in this parliament, we are not really making progress. it is necessary to ensure - and this is one of the amendments i tabled in the plenary - that account is taken of the efforts already made by other member states and the efforts required, which vary from one member state to another. i would ask my epp colleagues not to be dogmatic about my report either. i try not to do that myself. i would like to say a few words, mr president. everyone has gone over their time and they all said the same thing, so i'd like to say something about the charges. it says here: "of course, the levies will not reduce the risks". i'm not stupid, i know that. what are these levies for? to ensure that the polluter pays principle is properly applied. there are a lot of social costs, and i ask you: do you think it's normal that the social costs are borne by everyone or, in a fair way, by the products that pollute? that, mr president, is one of the challenges.
the rapporteur. - mr president, we shall not be short of compliments today: i would like, on behalf of all my colleagues, to thank the commission for its proposal. it is an excellent proposal and the work of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has been facilitated by the fact that the basis for discussion was initially very good. i would remind the commissioner that nonylphenols and nonylphenol ethoxylates - i will call them npe for ease of discussion - are classified as very toxic to aquatic organisms, substances that accumulate biologically in aquatic species and whose endocrine disrupting effect has also been demonstrated. the 2000 water framework directive classifies them as hazardous substances. the commission proposal proposes to end the use of these substances in eight specific areas and the committee on the environment, on this basis, proposes to extend the scope to co-formulants in pesticides and to active substances and co-formulants in biocides. it also proposes to align the concentration threshold for npe with that proposed for nonylphenols - np - i.e. to align it on a limit value of 0.1%. the commission also proposes to amend the directive on sewage sludge to introduce a concentration limit value for the same substances contained in sewage sludge. as regards cement, when it contains chromium vi in significant quantities, it can cause allergic reactions if it comes into contact with the hands. personal protective measures, i.e. the wearing of gloves, are necessary, but it is found that they are not sufficient to prevent such reactions. the addition of a reducing agent, usually iron sulphate, reduces chromium vi to a harmless chemical form. the committee on the environment proposes to extend the addition of a reducing agent to all cement containing more than two parts per million (ppm) of chromium vi, with a derogation limited to fully automated closed-loop use. this is a vital measure both for the workers concerned, and i am thinking in particular of the construction workers, and for the public budgets which have to pay large sums of money to the victims of allergic dermatitis. i would point out that we have a figure for germany - 36 million euros in benefits per year - which allows us to measure the economic impact of this problem. i should add that chromium vi is a carcinogen and mutagen suspected of causing lung and respiratory cancers through its presence in dust. the committee on the environment therefore asks for two things. firstly, on the directive on the protection of workers from the risks arising from chemical agents, it calls for an amendment introducing a limit value for the concentration of dust in the air. it also called for legislative initiatives of the same type as those adopted for end-of-life vehicles and electrical and electronic equipment to be introduced for other sectors using chromium vi in different products. the commission's proposal, as i have already said, was very good from the outset, which made our task easier and enabled us to consider, after discussion with the council representatives, a consultation before the vote in plenary, so as to reach an agreement at first reading. the vote in the committee on the environment has enabled us to take this step, which has been accepted by the leaders of the various political groups in the committee on the environment, and i thank them for this. we have thus reached agreement between the three institutions, not on everything - it is clear that there are still differences of opinion on a number of points - but on the essential points, before the vote in plenary. we say in my house, the best is the enemy of the good. i think that the good thing here is to achieve a result, the main objective being to speed up the implementation of the texts adopted. i therefore want to formally and very warmly thank the commissioner and the commission officials, as well as the greek presidency of the council, for having enabled us to reach a comprehensive agreement which will be put to the vote tomorrow. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the spread of the disease, and it is therefore important that the commission should be able to take action to combat it.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the report before us today is fundamental. tuberculosis, aids and malaria not only cause suffering for many people but have also become real obstacles to economic, social and cultural development in whole regions of the world, and they are also undermining many of our other initiatives for a more just world. the initiative under consideration, which aims to combat these scourges of poverty by injecting considerable sums - over eur 800 million - into the development of new medicines, must therefore be approved at first reading and implemented as soon as possible, because there is urgency. five million lives are at stake every year. we can appreciate and be proud of this decisive effort, because it is a real model of a research programme jointly conducted by the european union, the member states and the associated countries, and we can rightly hope that other actions will be inspired by it, in order to pay the same attention to other diseases that hit the poorest people on our planet hard. but this initiative is also a model in the light of the new philosophy of north-south relations which must be established, relations which are neither exploitative, nor dominating, nor paternalistic, but rather partnership relations to promote the autonomy and technological emergence of the countries concerned, as well as their active participation in the implementation and coordination of the programme. the question of the price of patented medicines and the development of public health services in developing countries should also be considered in this context. i am thinking of course of the gats and adpic cases, where the concept of partnership is being discarded in favour of the notions of intellectual property and liberalisation. i would like to say again that we must work together and develop synergies within the commission. in conclusion, mr president, i would like to thank commissioner busquin for giving us the means, through the sixth framework programme, to develop this programme and to thank mr caudron again for the quality of the work done. i would also say that we are only at the beginning of a long journey.
in writing. - i voted against the compromise resolution on iraq because it marked a departure from the position adopted by parliament on 30 january last. the new resolution does not condemn unilateral military intervention without the endorsement of the un, which i believe is essential. some members of the house seem more sensitive to the language of force and the fact of the matter that this war represents than to international law and their public opinion in the streets. that the european parliament, at a time when baghdad is being bombed and international law is being flouted, should content itself with deploring the war, regretting that inspections were not continued, or asking the belligerents not to use weapons of mass destruction, would have undoubtedly appalled our citizens. better no resolution at all than a lame compromise.
in writing. - the rapid end of hostilities in iraq is highly desirable, as is the need to reduce collateral damage as much as possible. in this context, we are in favour of the urgent establishment of a humanitarian programme that can provide immediate aid to the iraqi people. more in the future, we want a project to rebuild iraq under the auspices of the united nations. we also stress the need to obtain a commitment from the turkish authorities not to take any steps which could exacerbate a new conflict in northern iraq.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would first of all like to express, on behalf of the liberal group, my condolences to the families of the victims, 100 in total - the commissioner said - who have been swept away by this epidemic of atypical pneumonia, a virus - we also said - which is spreading at the speed of an airplane all over the world, particularly in south-east asia and canada. europe seems relatively spared at the moment: the commissioner referred to the current balance in the member states and the candidate countries. i would also like to thank him for giving us very precise details of the mutual exchange of information between who and the eu monitoring system. what can we say at this stage? firstly, there are reasons for concern, i would say, if you refer to the statements of a spokesman for the who, and i quote: 'it is going to be complicated and we could be engaged in a very long-term endeavour'. in short, the fight against the epidemic of severe acute respiratory syndrome (sars) is a race against time for health authorities and researchers worldwide, especially in terms of treatment. secondly, we have every reason to reassure european citizens as much as possible, as the commissioner announced in a statement: 'only sick people are contaminants, which makes traceability easier.' but, nevertheless, it must be said here: how can we not be outraged by the degree of ignorance and irresponsibility of the chinese government? it took almost five months after the virus was reported for the chinese health minister to decide to release the information. that said, and i come back to europe to conclude with the most important point: it is clear that this new crisis provides further support for those, including the previous speakers and myself, who are advocating that public health in the broad sense should become a shared competence of the union, a place it deserves today more than ever, at the time when our draft constitution is being drawn up. and i am convinced that this crisis will, in the long run, move this europe of health forward and accelerate the project, mentioned by the commissioner, of establishing by 2005 a european centre for disease control on the model of the cdc in atlanta.

there too. the fact that the possibility of an examination without training was prescribed, in which the choice is not with the candidate but with the country, was worse in our eyes. we hope, however, into account in the considerations and i think it was important to point this out. as regards the deadlines, everyone would like to see this directive implemented as quickly as possible. on the other afterwards - and this has become almost the leitmotif here in parliament - the control is not carried out and the execution is not correct. i would like to say to the commissioner that the commission - (fr) mr president, first of all i should like to congratulate the rapporteur on his report. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on the free movement of goods are respected. i am sometimes surprised at the backwardness of europe in road safety. the problem of driving times is another one which must be tackled urgently. we have to approve the report tomorrow to solve a number of problems. firstly, the transport sector can become more professional. there will be better drivers on our roads. the profession of truck driver will become more attractive and also more respectable. there is absolutely no place for cowboys on our roads. secondly, the training will also include attention to rational energy consumption. experiments show that even experienced drivers can permanently reduce fuel consumption by up to 30 percent after just one day of training. this is not unimportant for a kyoto-sensitive sector. third, and perhaps the most important point: road safety will also be improved. i am thinking here of training in the use of the mandatory blind spot systems and other safety technology on board lorries, but also of training in a more defensive driving style for the benefit of the weaker road users in general and cyclists in particular.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i can tell you that we received a reply to our questionnaire before christmas and that the answers to those questions helped us to incorporate them fully in the resolution. i believe that this is one of the first times that it has been possible to do so so early and i am very grateful for that. i have seen my report as a kind of annual report on the workings of the european parliament in 2001. i'll start with the main news. i recommend that you all give the secretary-general a discharge for the work he has done in the year 2001. my report contains 120 paragraphs. it is therefore impossible to explain everything, and i shall therefore only touch on a few aspects. firstly, the staff policy. i would like to speak more about equal opportunities policy, about women's access to senior posts in this parliament. parliament has repeatedly expressed its support for this, but it remains a sore point. i say in my report, together with the committee on budgetary control, that this objective has not actually been achieved. only 22 percent of the civil servants in the highest category are women. three of the newly recruited directors-general are men and in the a category only 14.3% of women were promoted in 2001 compared to 30.8% of men. that is why i think we should support positive discrimination measures. in the case of equal pay, priority should be given to women in management positions until a balance is achieved. another pain point is the appointment of senior officials. i recommend that we move away from the traditional practice of drawing up geographically and politically balanced packages. i don't think this is a good thing. we need to move towards an appointment policy based on criteria of qualifications, merit and competence. a second important chapter in my report concerns building policy. firstly, there is the fact that we have to work in three workshops. in the previous discharge report the bureau and the secretary-general were asked to publish a study on this subject. that study is now available and shows that working in three workshops costs 169 million euros a year, 120 million euros for infrastructure, 40 million euros for additional staff and missions and 9 million for all sorts of other costs. 25,855 days are lost on business trips to strasbourg. that's costing the taxpayer almost 4 million euros. needless to say, this creates a particular image problem. every time we receive groups of visitors, we have to explain it again. this creates not only an image problem but also a waste of time and money. it's beyond our control. hence the recommendation to colleagues working in the convention to raise this in the convention and to ensure that when the draft constitution is being prepared, consideration is given to the possibility of amending article 289 of the ec treaty. i know that this is still an important problem, because such a proposal still has to go to the igc and indeed there is still a veto from a number of member states. we owe it to ourselves to make an effort. another part of the building policy concerns the forecourt located above luxembourg station in brussels. the belgian state has a number of obligations in this area and should actually bear the costs. the belgian prime minister has been written to twice, but he does not reply. i therefore urge the belgian government to honour its obligation immediately and to pay us the 4.74 million euro. i also dedicated a section to the d4-d5 building. mr president, you are the vice-president responsible for building policy and i say this quite openly: i believe that you and the administration have made an excellent course. i believe that you have done everything possible to ensure that this construction project is carried out in accordance with the rules. only i think, and you can read that in my report, that a number of things have gone wrong, not on the part of parliament but on the belgian side. we are faced with facts beyond our control. the fact that at one point the belgian state promised that the land would be free, but that it has nevertheless been acquired by a private developer, has made our exercise even more difficult, and i regret that. finally, mr president, i should like to say a word about environmental policy. it's an important element of the game. i'm describing the many good measures you've taken with regard to the d4-d5 building. there's been a lot of effort to go to renewable energy, to take care of solar panels and things like that. i think that we as an institution need to have a real climate plan so that we as the european parliament can also contribute to compliance with the kyoto standards. on friday last week the commission presented a study which shows that if all public buildings in the european union were to use renewable energy, 18 per cent of the kyoto standards that europe must meet would already be met. let us set an example, let our parliament take this step too and ensure that the european parliament has a real climate plan. committee on development and the committee on budgetary control be listened to carefully. many mistakes have been made and there is a lack of measurable indicators. there is also the lack of coordination with the member states, so that the complementarity between the commission and the member states is often a dead letter. the council does not focus on policy issues but deals with fifty management committees which are intended to allow micromanagement of individual projects. there is a great deal of criticism of the way we exercise control ourselves. there is only a check on legality, while a lot of taxpayers are wondering whether we can really present the results of our development work. i therefore also support the wish of the committee on budgetary control to include the european development fund in the budget. i would like to return to something which i consider to be very dangerous, namely the risks of direct budget support. that is a growing trend and i understand that at first sight it seems much simpler. we are already at 2604 million in direct budgetary aid, and we know, however, that there are great risks involved, which have been very well explained by the rapporteur. first, there is the risk of substitution. if we want 35 per cent of the funds to be used for health and social infrastructure, especially education, in the fight against poverty, then we can see that it is all the easier for countries to spend their money on arms. i would like to point to the examples of uganda and rwanda, which are probably listed somewhere in the commission's records as countries that do things by the book. but you have to go and see what those countries are doing in eastern congo to see how they are complicit in land plunder there. and we are giving budgetary support to those countries! we are not paying too much attention to the conditions, because the report says that these conditions are - and i think it is right - poorly and inadequately formulated. we are dealing with a lack of control over management and spending, not to mention the potential for corruption that is being allowed and covered up. there are the persistent vision problems. in short, i would urge, commissioner, that the european development fund and the money administered by the committee on development should be examined not only in terms of legality but also in terms of the results achieved in relation to the priorities we all set ourselves, namely the eradication of poverty.

in writing. - the new information and communication technologies (ict) necessarily lead to a new model, a new paradigm based on new student/learner/teacher relations, and provoke and propose a pedagogical reorganization in the world of education. the main objective of exploiting and integrating ict in education should be to improve the learning situation of the student and his relationship with the world. any use that does not comply with these principles would lead to use for use. however, if they are the instrument and not the end, they can be wonderful educational allies. if they serve the objectives set by the educational and social community, they enable progress and development. these new technologies offer education today an unprecedented opportunity to meet an increasingly massive and diverse demand for education. the challenge is significant: it is clear that the ability of individuals to access and process information is decisive not only for their integration into the labour market but also in their social and cultural environment. any failure of education to train its pupils in the use or mastery of these techniques would have the effect of deepening social inequalities, if not creating them. the introduction of these in schools does not call into question the status of the teacher or the fundamental vocation of the school, but on the contrary, it can bring freedom and openness to the world.

commission. – questions on european civil protection corps, or ideas for similar initiatives in specific areas, such as a european coastguard, have been raised at different times in the past and also recently. however, to my knowledge, there is no specific proposal on the table. we must be aware that, according to the treaties, civil protection measures fall under the competence of the member states. therefore, any such measures at community level must strictly respect the principle of subsidiarity. furthermore, many different sectors are involved in civil protection. the ways in which they cooperate are organised differently in different member states. therefore the practical implications of establishing an eu-wide civil protection corps would require thorough analysis. however, the idea of creating such a corps is certainly interesting and i shall follow with interest the discussions on this issue in the european convention. independently from these discussions, i can inform you that the commission is already active in this field, although this seems to be little known. at the commission's initiative, a community mechanism to facilitate and reinforce cooperation in the field of civil protection has been set up. at present, all member states, eleven candidate countries and the three eea countries participate in this eu framework, which can provide a method for better civil protection coordination inside as well as outside the european union. one of the key elements in this context has been the establishment, within the commission, of the eu civil protection response centre. this centre has been in place since 1 january 2002 and is available on a permanent basis, 24 hours a day. once activated by a request from one of the participating countries, the centre can immediately call upon member states' civil protection resources, as already pre-identified for all types of major emergencies. if you wish, i can also mention when this response centre has been used and where we have intervened. the commission considers that the eu response centre, which builds on all available national capacities, represents an effective tool that responds to the concerns expressed by the honourable member.
- thank you for your answer, commissioner, but i have a few questions. you say that this european response centre is functioning, but did spain, for example, call on it during the prestige disaster? when did they invoke it? what happens if a member state does not invoke it and does not have the possibility to act itself? i was in galicia ten days after the disaster. the belgian civil protection arrived, but nothing was organized. if we had a disaster off the coast of latvia in two years' time, for example, that would be a problem. i think we should have a corps that can act immediately. secondly, the technical knowledge necessary to act in a correct and environmentally friendly way must be collected and made available to all member states.

in writing. - (fr) the the circumstances in which enlargement is proposed today are catastrophic. contrary to what had been promised, the community's structures were not designed to ensure a harmonious union of 25 member states in the future, much less of 40. worse still, the financing of enlargement as provided for in the treaty is by no means secured, contrary to what was agreed at the berlin summit. the community is thus entering a dead end. the chances of creating the social europe we so badly need will therefore be reduced. the territorial cohesion, which is more necessary than ever, will also be threatened. the community we have been striving for and building for 50 years will not survive enlargement and will give way to a weak and indefinite union. we wanted to build a cathedral, and now we're in the middle of a department store. finally, we are being asked to maximise the risk of blocking the union at a time when the key international mechanisms, with the un at the head, are breaking down. these are the reasons why i shall vote against the assent opinions, although i support the resolution accompanying the brok report because of the european parliament's improvements to it.
the fifth enlargement is a gigantic geopolitical step which is intended to bring to fruition an important phase in the great task of reuniting europe in a union based on a fully liberal democracy, in the political, economic, social and defence fields. it is a factor of peace and stability and helps to raise the standard of living of europeans. enlargement should also enable the eu to respond better to the challenges posed by globalisation. it must uphold universal values and be true to its historic commitment to all the countries of the mediterranean basin.
in the case of the united kingdom, the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. of course, that doesn't mean our work is done. there is still much to be done in the accession countries, including the development of a properly functioning administrative and legal organisation and the fight against corruption. in addition, monitoring must be taken seriously. i regret that we, who for many years have helped to prepare and encourage enlargement, have not revised our competences and decision-making mechanisms to be effective both in the eu and in the common foreign and security policy. i regret that we missed the opportunity to implement this treaty amendment in nice. and yet i voted yes. the political opportunities we had in 1994 to put pressure on the council with the european parliament and to demand a deepening of the union before enlargement no longer exist. this enlargement, of a different order, is again mainly a question of the basic principles of the european project: political stability, peace and progress. the accession countries and their peoples have been preparing for this day for many years under difficult conditions and with many sacrifices. this effort and the prospect of accession have brought stability and peace to europe. we can't let that score go to waste today. political stability, peace and security, after all, create the conditions for all other political objectives. the consultation on the review of the treaty is under way and in good hands. we put our faith in the convention. i am confident of that and i expect a lot from the next igc, and i can vote for it with conviction 10 times today. (parliament adopted the legislative resolution)

- mr president, i should like first of all, like the colleagues who have preceded me, to congratulate our rapporteur, mr liese, on the many contacts he has had from the beginning with all the parties concerned and, of course, the various fictitious rapporteurs, in order to arrive at this overall balanced report. this compromise was difficult to reach between the supporters of subsidiarity and the ethicists at all costs, and all those, including myself, who want to meet the expectations and hopes placed in us by millions of patients in europe, without hindering the development of the european biotechnology sector. yes, by transplanting corneas, heart valves, bones, tendons, and skin, the lives of patients are indeed being prolonged, improved. just one example: the repair of wounds from major burns affects nearly 20 000 european citizens every year; 60,000 diabetics had their legs amputated in 2002 because of a lack of an effective skin reconstruction procedure. it is clear that all these people waiting for treatment will not be treated or will be treated badly if tomorrow the european parliament dresses up as a moralist and directly or indirectly bans stem cell research, embryo research and germline therapy. that is why the liberal group, in its large majority, is firmly opposed to more than 30 amendments, including amendments nos 30, 31, 36, 37, 46, 50, but i shall not list them all here. a basic reminder: this is a public health matter based on article 152 of the treaty. the question is therefore one of quality criteria, standards of accreditation, qualification, training of staff, traceability tests, and the establishment of a european network of national tissue bank registers. that is the heart of the proposal and i hear very little about it, so much has been said about ethical issues. we had, however, reached a compromise in committee on organ donation, on the very sensitive issue of voluntary and unpaid donation, on cord blood donation, on equal access for all patients to the various treatments. we refuse to allow all this progress to be reduced to nothing here, because some people confuse the european parliament and the council of europe, because europe has no objective and no ambition to harmonize ethics and because conscience in the field of science has never cured anyone. for all these reasons, the liberal group, as our defence, reserves the right to vote against this report and to return to the commission's balanced proposal.
- mr president, i hope you will not be more severe with me than with my colleagues. i shall try to keep to my speaking time, however. what are the main issues in this report? i think mrs ferreira, who has done a very serious job on this subject, has mentioned them very correctly. i would like to give you my views on what i think are the main points, starting with the addition to the list of additives to sweeteners of a new sweetener called sucralose, and i would like to draw attention to the fact that sucralose is not a common substance. it is an organochlorine substance obtained by processing sugar with the addition of three chlorine atoms. i would point out that organochlorines are substances which generally pose problems for the environment and even for health. i am therefore surprised that a proposal which seems to make it seem trivial to include this new sweetener in the list of authorized sweeteners. to complete my remarks, i would point out that the adi, the acceptable daily intake, proposed for this sweetener is three times higher than that proposed by the food and drug administration in the united states. it seems to me therefore that there are enough unknowns about this new sweetener to exclude it from the list of authorized substances. my second point concerns aspartame. despite the considerations raised by mr bowis, i share the view of the rapporteur who considers amendment no 4 to be perfectly justified. a re-evaluation of aspartame does not seem to be a problem at all. it does not question the honesty of what has been done before. it simply shows that knowledge can change and that this change sometimes leads to a re-examination of previously given authorizations. so i personally think that this proposal is correct. my third point concerns cyclamates. in this connection, the european commission proposes to amend the maximum use dose in water-based drinks. we'd go from 400 milligrams per liter to 350 milligrams per liter. mrs ferreira's proposal is more ambitious and i think she is right. however, it seems to me that given the role of cyclamates and given that they are banned, or at least not authorized, in the united states, we should be asking ourselves about the possible risks of this sweetener. i would draw the attention of our colleagues to the fact that sweeteners are often found, as is the case with cyclamates, in drinks or products widely consumed by young children, who thus quite easily exceed the daily allowable dose for this kind of sweetener. i therefore think that it is desirable to be even more ambitious and i would like to see support for mrs schörling's amendment which proposes to set the maximum dose at 100 mg/l. i think that's a realistic proposal.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, is the price of our coffee the price of our conscience? coffee is one of those commodities whose export is the main source of income for many poor and over-indebted countries. countries such as burundi, ethiopia, and uganda depend on more than 50 percent of coffee exports. the situation on the world coffee market is therefore not an incidental, marginal issue. it is at the heart of the world's injustices, which condemn millions to poverty and misery. in this sense, the coffee crisis is a clear proof of the failure of the global policy of "everything for the market" and of the exacerbated liberalization. it is indeed the international institutions such as the world bank, the imf and the wto that encourage these countries to pursue an economic policy oriented towards the external market. strangled by debt and subject to the orders of the global usurer, which may sometimes be the imf, these countries are throwing themselves into productions where they have a comparative advantage over developed countries: in the case of coffee, favorable climatic conditions. secondly, the wto promises them free access to the world market. but the law of supply and demand creates price volatility, and when prices fall, the multinational corporations that control the market do their best to preserve their profit margins. the full effect of the fall in prices is thus passed on to small farmers and plantation workers, so much so that consumers in developed countries do not even see a drop in the price of their cup of coffee. not to mention the conditions of women's work and the exploitation of children. the evidence is there. the end of global injustice does not mean more liberalization, more free trade, more deregulation, quite the opposite. what coffee growers need is a more regulated international coffee market, to prevent overproduction, to guarantee and control the quality of the products, to distribute the profits of this farm fairly and to ensure decent working conditions. hopefully this lesson will be remembered at the next round table of the international coffee organization, at the next g8 summit and, above all, at the next wto ministerial conference in cancún in september. we will be very vigilant, commissioner.

mr president, this proposal from the european commission is the first attempt to introduce a comprehensive system in the european union for the prevention and repair of environmental damage. perhaps everyone will agree that such a system is badly needed. but however united we may be on principle, this parliament seems to be divided on the scope of its action. how far should we go in protecting the environment and the obligation to repair damage? there are many concrete points of disagreement: the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment and the environment from pollution, which would be compatible with the common market. our rapporteur, toine manders, whom i warmly congratulate, has tried to reconcile often very different views and the result is, in my view, a balanced report, a well-balanced result which takes account of the many interests and economic sectors involved in this subject. this is what we all want in the end: a good, well-defined and uniform european framework providing for a comprehensive approach to environmental damage, with clear european standards, the same rules for the whole of the european union and where everyone knows what to adhere to. i am convinced that many, including our green fundamentalists, want to go much further. the question is not whether this is necessary or useful, but whether it is feasible at the moment. the question is whether this will still be affordable, insurable or unpredictable for the economic sectors. those who want to go much further now must realise that they may be pursuing the unattainable and that we risk standing empty-handed at the final vote. you can't do that. what is most important now is a good, general arrangement for the near future which tackles the real culprits. there's no more time to lose.
- mr president, with this proposal for a directive, the european commission finally came to the end of a process that had lasted for ten years in january 2002. it must be admitted that in 1993 there was already a green paper on environmental responsibility. the european parliament, through its committee on the environment, has then repeatedly insisted that the commission should take a rapid initiative in this area. so it has been a long time coming for a consistent proposal to be put on the table. what's the matter with him? this commission proposal clearly does not live up to the stated ambitions. i believe that the commission's initial ambitions were high, but the final product is disappointing, and i think we must stress the elements which justify this disappointment. first, there is a narrow definition of the environment. in the case of biodiversity, only an estimated 20% of biodiversity would be protected and 13% of the european territory would be affected. the second element is the scope. annex i lists the various economic activities which fall outside the scope of the principle of strict liability. this list of activities is very restrictive. in particular, everything related to nuclear energy and everything related to the transport of hydrocarbons is excluded, without any real justification. we refer to international conventions which do exist but which are not aimed at repairing environmental damage. i would like to recall what one of our epp colleagues, mr bébéar, said yesterday in this chamber when he complained about the low amount of compensation that fipol was considering for the victims of the prestige accident, and he is right. we should therefore be consistent and adopt a very strong amendment calling for what is not provided for in the international convention to be covered by our directive in respect of accidents of this kind. but this is not the case with the commission's proposal. the third element to be taken into account is the exemptions provided for on the basis of the existence of an operating licence or the existence of scientific knowledge which, at the time of the accident, would not be sufficiently relevant to justify the liability of the economic activity in question. think, for example, of gmos or pesticides, which are endocrine disruptors, and with such a principle, virtually all activities related to them will escape the application of the directive. which is unacceptable. i would like to conclude, mr president, by saying that our group supports a number of amendments which are in line with the proposals made by mr papayannakis to the committee on the environment, which were taken up by mrs gebhardt. i think that these important changes are essential if we are to give real substance to this directive. president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, as rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities on this report, i should like to begin by thanking mr max van den berg for his excellent work and, of course, for having taken over parts of our committee's opinion. so what do we got? we have a good report, we have a good rapporteur, we have a motivated commissioner here among us, many motivated people on the ground, development workers, educators. still, there are a few things that make me mad that it's all taking so long. the clock keeps ticking, and i still see so many organizations on the ground begging to build schools, to pay teachers. it's going to be a long ride. the right to education and access to education and training are often abstract concepts in developing countries. it 's correct . 113 million children are still out of school, and most of them are girls, as mr van den berg has already said. they have to help in the household, they have to help provide the daily food supply for the whole family, they have to keep up with the young children, they have to sell the surpluses at the market and they have to fetch drinking water. so they don't get that space to work on themselves. i therefore fully endorse the conclusions of this report, namely that education is the key to combating poverty. only through education can a country develop, only through an adequately educated population can a country move forward. educate a girl, educate a woman, and you educate a whole family. we also know that equal opportunities for women cannot be achieved by changing legislation and social conditions alone. - no, please! president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, thanks to the good cooperation of the european parliament and the other european institutions, we are now on the eve of the liberalisation of the energy markets in europe. many colleagues have been working on this for years. the result, therefore, is at least commendable. the long-awaited liberalization should first of all have a positive impact on consumers. i am thinking in particular of better service and more competitive prices. i would point out that there is a paradox here. i think that liberalization will also have a price. the price, paradoxically, will be borne primarily by the consumer. particular attention will therefore have to be paid to the way in which the distribution network will be used. the price to be paid by the user is the sum of the production price, the transmission tariff on the high-voltage network and the distribution tariff, plus all the distribution and transmission charges. the share of distribution costs is the highest cost item on the invoices of the customers of the distribution networks. in addition, in some member states, the likelihood that the average user will see his electricity bill significantly reduced as a result of the liberalisation of the electricity market is very low. the reason for this is obvious. the limited efficiency gains in the sector are too small to compensate for the new charges on the sector. on the other hand, it should also be borne in mind that the scarce connections between the various national networks are not intended to import or export large quantities, but to provide mutual assistance in the event of unexpected shutdowns of production units in a country. so there is still a big lack of commercial interoperability. a real internal market requires a significant increase in this commercial inter-capacity. the conclusion is that this is an important step in the liberalisation of the energy markets in the european union, but that the way in which this liberalisation is put into practice will have to be monitored closely at all times.
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the bride is very beautiful! energy is not a market like any other. on the one hand, there is a natural monopoly in the distribution and transport networks, which reduces the scope for competition. on the other hand, it is a sector where price variations, in the absence of sufficient regulation, can be immense given the rigidity of demand and the impossibility of electricity storage. finally, access to energy for all citizens must be considered a genuine right, which again raises the question of public service. public service obligations are included in the texts, but this is not enough. the member states are given too much latitude in the organisation and definition of these public service obligations, which will allow the behaviour of member states less attached to the imperatives of social cohesion and citizens' rights to be legitimised. secondly, free competition between operators is given priority in these texts over public service obligations. this is at a time when the commission, with its green paper, is launching the debate on services of general interest! what would have been the problem if we had taken some time to assess the processes of sectoral liberalisation and to make this issue the heart of european policy?

the report by our colleague, mr mann, makes a very interesting contribution to the interesting debate on the relationship between regionalism and multilateralism. i am a strong supporter of the multilateral approach, but i also remain convinced that regionalism and multilateralism can reinforce and complement each other in many ways. i therefore agree with the rapporteur that the european union must continue to support and promote regional cooperation and integration in the future. i would also like to say that the idea of using the establishment of free trade zones as development instruments seems to me to be a very interesting way of thinking, provided that they are part of a general package of reform measures. i would point out, however, that we must be careful not to get caught up in an inevitable clutch of regional and multilateral initiatives for the benefit of the developing countries. i believe that the multilateral approach still deserves priority over regional initiatives today. in other words, the european union's top priority at the moment must be to successfully complete the doha development agenda. the debate on regionalism and multilateralism, important as it is, must be postponed until then. the mann report is an interesting contribution to this, and i thank her for it.

the first question is why an own-initiative report was proposed. because we see that the method is spreading to many areas. in lisbon, it was recommended that this method should be applied in the fields of the information society, research and innovation, enterprise policy, education and training, social inclusion, economic policy, migration policy, etc. we see, however, that parliament's involvement and control are very limited. we see great opacity in decision-making and in the reasons why some elements of the method are used or not. the committee on employment and social affairs has therefore decided to make a thorough analysis of the way in which this is done. the resulting report proposed the following: if the european commission wishes to start an open method of coordination, it must not only seek the consent of the council but also that of parliament. in addition, the commission should indicate which elements of the method it intends to use. these elements are: the commission will also be presenting a report on the implementation of the programme, which will include common objectives, guidelines, common indicators, a synthesis report, national reports and recommendations. these are the traditional elements that are used in part or in full. the european parliament must give its opinion on the guidelines, the synthesis report and the recommendations, and the social partners must be consulted on the social and labour aspects. finally, the commission is also asked to carry out a study on the effectiveness of the method and on the linking of structural fund money to the recommendations made to the member states. in order to structure this matter and to avoid the open method of coordination being developed in some field without the knowledge, participation or co-decision of parliament, it is necessary to establish the general mechanism and the co-decision right of parliament in the constitution. the question is, however, will we succeed? i look at the texts drawn up by the convention bureau so far and i see that we have succeeded to some extent. for example, it provides that the open method of coordination is to be applied in economic policy and employment policy and that it can be applied in social policy - an extension compared with the current texts. it also provides that parliament must be consulted or informed of the guidelines and recommendations. this is a step in the right direction, of course, but it is a small step. it is, in any case, more than was thought possible at any given time on the basis of the discussions in the convention. but it is still too little. parliamentary powers and controls remain limited. those who oppose the method and think that if we do not mention them in the convention they will not be applied are mistaken, of course. no one can stop that method from being used. a simple council decision without parliament's say in it will therefore have the effect of applying the method in one area or another. i will give you an example in relation to immigration policy. if the ministers responsible for migration decide to apply the method - partly because of the inability to legislate in this area, for example - then the open method of coordination for migration policy will be applied. our problem is not so much that it is being applied, but that parliament is not involved or is involved only to a very limited extent. that is why we said in committee: i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in the negotiations, but that it should be involved in the negotiations. that was the whole point of the article. we have seen that some progress has been made in the convention, that we have achieved some of what we wanted. however, it is imperfect and incomplete in relation to what it should be. i repeat my two main points: i am afraid that the open method of coordination could lead to no legislation being passed and that the open method of coordination might lead to a loss of national competence. in either case, there is really only one solution, and that is: the commission has already done so. the opposition to the open method of coordination has precisely led to this being insufficiently achieved. all i can say is that's too bad. i think we have made a good report and i thank all the colleagues in the committee on employment and social affairs, because they have worked in a fantastic way on this report.

president. - the figures are sufficiently well known. worldwide, there are about half a billion small arms and light weapons, more than half of which are illegal. in 46 of the 49 major conflicts since 1990, these weapons have been used. these weapons are also largely responsible for the phenomenon of child soldiers. small arms and light weapons are easy to get, in some places for 15 dollars, they're easy to handle, easy to hide and transport and most importantly, they're oh so deadly. according to an independent study in 2001, small arms kill more than 1,000 people a day, mostly innocent people. there are hardly any international regulations on the production and proliferation of small arms and light weapons. the ottawa convention on the use of antipersonnel mines is an exception. unfortunately, we must note that the major producers of small arms and light weapons, the usa, russia and china, are also the main opponents of international regulation à la ottawa. if we are to really get a handle on the proliferation of small arms, the first thing we need is more transparency. the un register of conventional weapons should be opened for this category of weapons. we need to tighten up the rules on arms exports. the labelling of new weapons should be made compulsory in order to facilitate tracing of their origin. i share the view of commissioner patten that the 1998 european code of conduct could serve as a model for a global, binding agreement on arms exports and transfers. the european union must take the lead in the fight against the illicit trade in small arms and light weapons at the un conference in july. all efforts to prevent conflict are in danger of becoming a wasted effort if the real causes are not addressed.
mr president, the prestige disaster has thrown a harsh light on marine pollution. the event was media-heavy, dramatic, and it revealed institutional and political dysfunctions. but it's really just the tip of the iceberg. marine ecosystems are threatened by many other pollutants than single-hull ships, and their biodiversity, both marine flora and fauna, is at risk. the predators are many. first, overfishing and illegal fishing, which deplete fish stocks, but also landfills, agricultural fertiliser and pesticide residues, clandestine ship discharges, products from oil platforms - all human activities that have taken no account of anything other than immediate profit and that have no prospects for sustainable development and no concern for the environment. the commission's achievement has been to draw up an inventory of these risks, to identify the state of knowledge in this respect, and to examine the various conventions or laws capable of regulating these risks at different levels: international, european, national or regional. on the basis of this inventory, the commission has developed a very goal-oriented strategy for the protection of the seabed, as they say, with clear objectives and an action plan. the excellent report by mrs gonzález álvarez welcomes this effort, but it also suggests that the commission should, firstly, go beyond analysis and be more firm and precise in terms of the actions to be taken and, above all, their timetable. in particular, where knowledge is still lacking in a specific area, the precautionary principle must be applied for rapid intervention and must be supplemented by those of polluter pays and sustainable development. in this context, there should be a fundamental change in the legislation on international navigation and a review of old conventions which do not include these principles. the second suggestion from the report to the commission is that it should complete its communication by emphasizing various aspects which may not have been sufficiently developed, areas of particular vulnerability - the baltic, the mediterranean and the black sea have been mentioned - dangerous substances which have not yet been mentioned - various nitrogen substances, hydrocarbons and landfills - activities which have been left behind, such as flags of convenience, or species which are particularly threatened, such as posidonia. the committee on the environment has welcomed both the commission's communication and mrs álvarez's report with a very, very positive feeling. and it is probably not tomorrow that fundamental opposition will emerge in parliament. but if we can hope that the commission will be firm and vigilant in this area, we can be even more worried about the council, where there must be a real political will to implement this integrated strategy for the protection of the seabed. the past has shown that internal resistance, sometimes linked to national interests, can hamper the best-thought-out european strategic policies. hence the justified insistence of the gonzález álvarez report that concrete action be taken now within an integrated strategy at all levels of government, but also at all geographical levels, including with local authorities.

in writing. - (nl) the financing of european political parties is a new step towards a european federal 'super-state'. there is a desire in the upper echelons to unify the existing parties, to strip them of their singularities and to extract them from their national traditions. moreover, political parties in several member states are already government-funded. in belgium, for example, they are financed by the provinces, the regions and the federal government. it is a matter of real concern that funding is subject to conditions. concepts such as 'respect for democratic principles' are nowhere precisely defined. the door is thus wide open to arbitrariness. with the exception of the communist parties, only one party represented in the european parliament is opposed to democracy, but press reports have since revealed that national far-right parties are subject to specific surveillance. in some circles it has become commonplace to claim that opposition to 'politically correct' dogmas, for example on the issue of immigration, is incompatible with democratic values. such aberrations must be confined to a strictly polemical use and cannot be translated into european legislation. consequently, the representatives of the vlaams blok and the front national will vote against the report and will consider all possibilities of legal action against this violation of the principle of equality.
in writing. - (nl) as flemish socialists, we voted against the compromise proposed by the council on the status of european political parties as presented to the european parliament, because it does not provide sufficient guarantees as regards the independence of european political parties. the compromise contains a financial arrangement for european political parties to be co-financed by donations from 2004. the compromise reached, which allows a donor - whether a natural or legal person - to give up to eur 12 000 per year to a european party, with a traceability threshold of eur 500, is unacceptable to us. we continue to work towards a ban on corporate donations and the introduction of a threshold of eur 2 000 per year for private donations. donors are also asked to identify themselves if they make a contribution of €125 or more.

mr president, the representatives of the flemish bloc and also the national front approve this report without necessarily approving the wording of mr camre. the european parliament must take a firm lead in defending the right to freedom of expression. in a democracy, everyone should have the freedom, the opportunity to express their opinion on any subject, even if that opinion is perceived by some as disturbing. the right to freedom of expression applies a priori to elected representatives of the people. these have not only the right but also the duty to put the problems and aspirations of their constituents on the political agenda. parliamentary immunity must be interpreted as broadly as possible, since members of parliament are not only active in an assembly but also in the political arena in the broad sense, such as meetings, debates and all possible political and electoral communication. it is also a worrying development that any criticism of islam is met with legal and other actions. since most islamic states are not democracies, it is of the utmost importance that any possible totalitarian, intolerant and archaic aspects of this religion remain open to discussion in europe.
- madam president, on gmos, what can we say in a minute, except that the precautionary principle is not, and should not be, the postulate of inaction. we must therefore welcome the forthcoming entry into force of this european regulation on the labelling and traceability of gmos. the demanding provisions contained in the reports by my colleagues trakatellis and scheele will make it possible to ascertain the composition of the feed, its distribution system and whether or not the animals have consumed food based on gmos. in these strict conditions, the lifting of the moratorium is welcome. however, will consumers be reassured, because the test of truth is tomorrow? it can be summed up in three words: the consumer's free choice of what is on his plate. the two rapporteurs and the commissioner have made this point before me. to convince, to reassure, you'll have to be perfect. the label error for products certified as non-gmo will not be allowed, as there is no question of making farmers in the traditional and organic sectors pay the bill for the unavoidable risk of contamination of crops. in conclusion, i would say: continue the research, remain rational and convincing, communicate, inform citizens more and more, explain to them that gm crops are not just about soybeans, maize or rapeseed, but that gmos are also gene therapy, which, for example, has allowed children with primary immunodeficiency to live outside their bubble.
