in writing. - (nl) mr president, the zappalà report has become a controversial issue in this house following a series of amendments concerning the awarding of public contracts. social and environmental considerations must be allowed to be decisive in a public procurement procedure, provided that they are decisive at the level of the compliance requirements defining the product or service concerned and at the level of the selection criteria defining the suppliers who may participate. however, these considerations cannot be the only ones to be taken into account in the tendering phase. the tendering phase must be able to take place on the basis of an objective comparison. that is why i did not approve many of the social and environmental amendments concerning the award criteria. the same applies to problems of discrimination. we must also tackle these problems. legislative measures may be taken to this end, but they have no place in the award criteria for public contracts.
in writing. - no compromise is completely satisfactory. the european parliament's vote on gmos simply means that it was not possible to take the negotiation any further. the moratorium on gmos will therefore be lifted, as requested by the member states. the 0.5% threshold, at which the proponents of the precautionary principle wanted the presence of gmos to be reported, would not have won the qualified majority in either parliament or the council: a threshold of 0.9% was therefore voted, and i regret it. nevertheless, the achievements of this compromise are not negligible, as we also voted for greater traceability, which will allow increased consumer vigilance, strict regulations on the detection of gmos, accurate labelling and the possibility of clearly distinguishing between gmo and non-gmo production. transparency has won, even if the precautionary principle is a little bit corny. it is not a victory, but the amendments adopted set out important milestones which will have to be further strengthened in the future. that is why i voted in favour of the scheele report.
in writing. - (nl) the sp-a delegation approved the compromise proposals made by the rapporteur with the council and the commission. these compromises will allow for the rapid adoption of appropriate legislation on gm-based feed and food, as well as on traceability and labelling. at the same time, a number of guarantees are being removed as regards the coexistence of farms with and without gmos. an amendment to directive 2001/18/ec will explicitly allow member states to prevent the unintended presence of gmos. it is also stated that the parliament is the requesting party to european legislation on coexistence. traceability and labelling are not sufficient to guarantee consumer choice in the long term. therefore, measures to promote coexistence are equally important. in exchange for these compromises, parliament is abandoning its demands for stricter limit values for gmo contamination (0.5%), values which would not be supported by all members of the european parliament and council. it is therefore more important to us to ensure that there is a coexistence agreement, because without such measures no member state will be able to apply the limit values. with this legislation, and even with the compromise, europe is the first to introduce strict regulations on the authorisation, traceability and labelling of gmos. the question of liability should also be resolved as soon as possible. this is what the european parliament asked the commission to do during the first reading of the environmental liability directive.
in writing. - (nl) mr president, the rapporteurs on the gmo dossier have tried to present reports which offer a certain complementarity and which allow agreement with the council. i respect their work. i also believe that progress is needed on these issues so that the de facto moratorium on the authorisation of new gmo applications can be lifted. these are the two reasons why my group and i voted in favour of these reports. and yet i am not at all convinced of the quality of some parts of these legislative documents. i'm afraid we'll have to see them again later. i also want to increase consumer confidence in food produced from gmos. i think that in this respect, what is needed above all is a lot of information on the usefulness of the new techniques and on the safety of the foodstuffs concerned. i hope that consumer associations and universities will be involved, because according to eurobarometer, they are the ones with the greatest power of persuasion with consumers. i too want to leave the consumer the greatest possible freedom of choice, even if the separate storage of varieties, traceability and labelling make food more expensive. but the information received by the consumer must be extremely clear and accurate. detectability must also replace the paper criterion. unfortunately, our political group, the epp-ed, has already lost this battle at first reading. i conclude, mr president, that we are currently following a costly path - for the farmer, for industry and therefore also for the consumer - without achieving the real objective. it is therefore worth asking whether we could have done better by ensuring other confidence-building measures, namely massive and objective information. fortunately, we have a lifeline: the evaluation after a few years will bring us back to a certain level of sanity. at least that is what we hope.
in writing. - (nl) for the last 15 years, belgian meat inspectors, who are not technicians but qualified veterinarians, have used all their energy, strength and creativity to combat the illegal use of hormones in livestock farming. this work is done under difficult circumstances, in which these veterinarians have to resist attempts to bribe and are physically intimidated. some are even affected in their privacy as their property and even their lives and the lives of their loved ones are threatened. the inspector and veterinarian karel van noppen was even murdered on 20 february 1995. the murderers and the sponsors were fortunately convicted last year. they're currently in lockup. these are the difficult circumstances in which these courageous officials are enforcing the total ban on the use of hormones in europe. the inspectors are also sometimes hampered in their stubborn work by the thoughtless statements of some scientists who have declared out of hand that the use of sex hormones is totally harmless to public health. anyone who reads the olsson report knows that this is an aberration. i therefore regard the approval of the olsson report as a tribute to all european meat inspectors who do a quality job and as an incitation to constant vigilance.
in writing. - the european union must take concrete action to reduce its emissions of the most polluting gases. it must do so to protect the health of its citizens, to meet the kyoto targets, to set an example for the world and to avoid the catastrophic scenario that is looming: the latest report by the international energy agency's climate scientists calls for a doubling of energy consumption and greenhouse gas emissions by 2030! i therefore voted in favour of introducing this 'permit to pollute less' applicable from 1 january 2005 to the main industrial sectors. companies that exceed their quotas will be fined €40 per tonne of excess, then €100 per tonne after 2008. this system of trading allowances therefore appears to be a real breath of fresh air which will reduce these emissions by more than 20% from 2010 onwards, not to mention saving €2 billion in energy. a disadvantage, however: it seems unreasonable to me that belgium should have to bear 1/8th of the overall european "kyoto" cost, when it has only about 2.5% of the inhabitants and represents almost 3% of the european gdp. protecting the planet is the business of everyone, of all rich countries, of all industries, including transport, and also of individuals.
i suggest. mr president, i greet the representative of the commission and all the honourable members of the european parliament in the chamber. if i may, mr president, i would like to introduce this statement on chechnya with the firmest intention of my personal commitment to continue my presence, my regular participation in the work of the european parliament, my willingness to listen to the views i shall gather in this and other cases and to keep parliament constantly informed of the council's dossiers and activities, as far as my competence is concerned. turning to the question of chechnya, you all know that the situation in the chechen republic, one of the republics of the russian federation, has long been the subject of the attention of the european union because of the serious incidents of war violence and terrorism which it has caused and the consequences it is likely to have for europe. the game being played in chechnya has a much wider significance than this small republic. its consequences, whether positive or negative, will affect the whole of the caucasus. i believe that even a brief knowledge of past events is important for judging the present and future prospects. so that's why i'm going to give you this little preface to the past. since the break-up of the soviet union and the subsequent creation of the russian federation, the chechen republic has been the scene of violent russian-chechen clashes and bloody independence movements. the 1997 peace treaty laid the foundations for a situation of very wide de facto autonomy under the presidency of the chechen leader mashkadov, while guerrilla formations were active in the territory. in 1999, moscow intervened in ingushetia and north ossetia, while in chechnya russian troops occupied the capital grozny. years of attacks and repression followed, in a climate of violence by both the hardline separatists and russian forces. the european union, the council of europe and the osce recognised russia's right to defend its territorial integrity and to fight terrorism, but condemned the disproportionate use of force and called on the russian government to initiate a political solution to the crisis by allowing humanitarian organisations access to assist in chechnya. after 11 september 2001, the russian counterterrorism thesis was strengthened, although the link between chechnya and islamic fundamentalism - a link that also exists - appears indirect. the roots of the war remain essentially nationalist in nature, despite the growing and dangerous contamination with islamic extremism, especially in the methods and instruments. despite the opening of a channel of dialogue between the russian authorities and one of the main figures of the chechen resistance in september 2001, the prospects for a political solution to the conflict remained nebulous and confused. it was in this context that the serious terrorist incident at the na dubrovke theatre took place, followed by the numerous, equally violent attacks of recent months. the situation on the ground is very difficult: the european union is also committed to the development of the region, and to the development of the region as a whole. while chechnya had a flourishing economy in the past, due of course primarily to oil and a highly developed metalworking sector, the economy is currently at a reduced level; the unemployment rate is 42%, there are 64,000 chechen refugees in the neighbouring republic of ingushetia, 8,000 displaced in camps, 290,000 homeless. civil life has been largely destroyed, reduced to a truly dramatic state by the destruction of archives and offices. honourable members of parliament, this is the basis on which president putin has launched the important institutional reform initiative with the launch of a new constitution to be approved by a local referendum. in president putin's plan, this path should be supported by the international community, in particular europe, the council of europe and the osce. the political design is the granting of broad autonomy and strong powers to local bodies within the russian federation, whose territorial integrity is safeguarded. the withdrawal of unnecessary military contingents and the reduction of checkpoints have been initiated to facilitate the gradual normalisation of the region and the holding of the referendum. the outcome of the referendum on 23 march, with 89% of the votes cast, was, as is well known, broadly in favour of the draft constitution. this shows that the chechen people, tired of the war situation, have chosen the political solution within the russian federation. at the same time, it emerged that rebel leader mashkadov and his followers have largely lost the support of civil society. the newly adopted constitution provides for presidential elections to be held by october, and parliamentary elections to be held no earlier than three months after the election of the future president. despite the good result, the referendum is only the first step in this process of political transition. there are a number of unresolved issues, such as the delimitation of powers between the chechen republic and the russian federation, compensation for those who have lost their property, measures to combat crime, including crimes committed by federal forces. as you all know, there are currently 200 criminal proceedings for serious crimes against members of the police. on 6 june the state duma approved an amnesty for chechnya, both for rebels and for members of the security forces who, of course, did not commit any bloodshed. clemency has also been offered to those who renounce armed struggle and separatism by 1 september. despite the amnesty, however, attacks continue on the ground, with the consequent, consequential, harsh response activity of the law enforcement agencies. the international community has been actively following events and moscow's initiatives. while the parliamentary assembly of the council of europe adopted a resolution envisaging the possibility of an international tribunal to try those responsible for serious human rights violations in chechnya, more recently the institutional political path was examined during talks held by the commissioner for human rights in moscow. in the framework of the osce, the presidency-in-office is preparing, in agreement with russia, the resumption of the organisation's activities in the republic, based on specific agreed programmes on elections, good governance, the judiciary, the media, the police and humanitarian aid. the 15 members of the union presented a draft resolution to the conference on human rights in geneva in april condemning the serious violations in chechnya. as you know, the resolution was not adopted because it did not receive a majority of the votes. the failure to adopt that resolution was, as is well known, described by moscow as a 'triumph of common sense'. the council, ladies and gentlemen, takes note of the european parliament's concern about the situation in the chechen republic and will endeavour to ensure that the political process recently launched leads to the restoration of the rule of law and the reconstruction of the economic and social fabric in that region. the union agrees that the institutional process initiated by president putin towards chechnya is generally positive. the eu is also expected to make a major breakthrough in respect for human rights and to punish those who have committed abuses. the european union's continued attention was expressed both in a recent visit to chechnya in april by the troika of heads of mission in moscow and in support for the return of osce activities in the region, which i have just mentioned. the russia-eu cooperation council on 15 april was the first occasion for the union to send this message of attention, interest and readiness to act at a high political level. the st petersburg summit between russia and the european union expressed the hope that the political process recently launched, with economic and social reconstruction, would lead to the restoration of the rule of law, the promotion of human rights and genuine reconciliation. the st petersburg summit condemned any act of violence, particularly terrorist violence, which could jeopardize the prospects for a political solution, and recognized the substantial contribution that international organizations can make in cooperation with the russian authorities. finally, once economic reconstruction has begun, the union's commitment will also have to be financial, albeit subject to strict conditions. the commission representative, who will be speaking shortly, will be able to explain the aid package that is being drawn up. the cooperation of the russian authorities will be essential to ensure that this aid can actually reach those in dire need. in the future, it seems worthwhile to consider seriously the possibility of launching a union initiative in chechnya, an initiative which, in line with the process initiated by president putin, could extend community intervention from the humanitarian assistance sector, on which we have all concentrated so far, to the other sector - i dare say - of institution building and the promotion of socioeconomic development, which is equally important, by framing some concrete initiatives in the cooperation currently operating between the european union and the osce. the commission is currently examining, with its russian counterpart, the possibility of assistance in certain areas: i have already referred to the electoral sector, which is of great importance, the judicial sector, the reform of the police force, the reintegration of refugees and displaced persons. these institution building elements have traditionally been a priority for the union as well. in conclusion, with reference to the institutional process initiated by moscow, the italian presidency will endeavour to encourage a definitive political solution involving as far as possible all the representatives and segments of the chechen population not compromised by terrorism. we believe that an improvement in the current standards of respect for the human rights of the chechen population can be a concrete and strong consequence - i dare say, almost automatic consequence - of the institutional reform that has been initiated.
the commission (es) mr president, following the council's position, clearly expressed by the president-in-office of the council, mr frattini, i have to say that the commission cannot but reiterate that the european union is firmly committed to the fight against terrorism and, in that sense, supports russia in its legitimate fight against terrorism, in chechnya and elsewhere. there must be no doubt about that. this means that the defence of human rights is a whole and that, if the first attack on human rights is terrorism itself, which does not respect the basic right to life, that does not mean that we should not always bear in mind, in any action and fight against terrorism, that human rights are a fundamental and basic element that must be respected at all times. that said, and entering into the areas most directly related to the commission's work - which i will focus on in order not to repeat what has already been said - i would point out that progress is needed on the possibility of providing humanitarian aid in chechnya. as you know, the european union, through the european community humanitarian aid office, is the largest donor of humanitarian aid in the north caucasus. since october 1999, we have provided over eur 110 million in aid to the victims of the conflict in chechnya. the security situation in the north caucasus remains poor, which creates a number of logistical problems. the commission hopes, however, that the russian authorities will take a more open attitude, for example, in relation to attempts to release arjan erkel, the european aid worker who is still being held hostage. the reluctance, in some cases, of the russian authorities to allow ngos to use the necessary radio communications, as well as the system for granting residence and work permits in chechnya, remain the main obstacles to the delivery of humanitarian aid. in this respect, we will continue to advocate the improvement of the treatment offered, in order to facilitate the implementation of humanitarian operations, in accordance with basic standards of safety, efficiency and supervision and, of course, independent evaluation. moreover, although the russian authorities have assured us that they will not forcibly repatriate to chechnya the thousands of displaced persons in the border areas, to which the president-in-office of the council referred a moment ago, echo has informed us that it is being denied the possibility of replacing the deteriorating tents and building more solid shelters in neighbouring ingushetia. the displaced should not have the only option of returning to chechnya. in addition to closely monitoring developments in general, we will continue to insist in particular that any repatriation of displaced chechens should be voluntary and unconditional. looking ahead, the commission will continue to monitor developments, including the ongoing work towards the conclusion of a treaty clarifying the status of chechnya within the russian federation, as well as the preparations for the presidential and parliamentary elections in the russian republic scheduled for october and december. we shall therefore continue to monitor developments in this area. the commission now stresses that all these actions, all these advances, must be carried out in the most transparent way, in the most consonant manner with international standards, and above all in such a way as to facilitate the settlement of this conflict once and for all.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. i see the referendum on 23 march as a first step towards trying to stabilise the situation in chechnya. i also welcome the efforts of the administration to make life more tolerable, to gradually reopen schools, to install at least part of the energy supply, public transport and medical services in grozny, but these are far from sufficient. the reconstruction must be forced on the russian side. in this context, i hope that the elections in december 2003 and march 2004 will bring political stability to chechnya. the european union should be prepared to provide the necessary support for the holding of free and fair elections. i think it is very problematic, however, that international organisations are still not being given free access to chechnya to provide much-needed humanitarian and medical aid. it has been said: the situation of refugees remains precarious. humanitarian aid must not be curtailed, nor must refugees be returned against their will until the conditions for this are met. i think it is problematic that after 11 september the council and the commission have de facto stopped discussing the actions of the russian army in chechnya, which have caused massive suffering, primarily to the civilian population. we condemn the inhuman violence of chechen terrorists, but we also condemn the disproportionate use of military force and arbitrariness by the russian army. human rights are indivisible and their violation does not lead to de-escalation of the conflict. if the european union wants to contribute to the resolution of the conflict, it must in cooperation with russia push for further changes in chechen policy!
i would like to recommend that you read our travel report, because i think it is a balanced report and contains more nuanced points than what i have heard from some speakers so far. the fight in - mr president, i would not like to repeat what commissioner lamy has already said, namely that although the international criminal court is not a political instrument, it is being set up in a particularly difficult political context and that, since 2002, the united states has been trying, in a marked way, to bypass this court by violence, both at the un and by signing bilateral agreements with a large number of countries. so i would like to say how shocked i am, as a member of parliament, at the way in which pressure is being put on certain countries - and i am thinking in particular of the pressure on colombia, which is threatened with the suspension of us aid to fight drug traffickers, or the pressure on the baltic states or other countries applying for nato membership - but also at the way in which the whole of europe is currently suspended from the european union; the fact that we are in agreement, the council, the european parliament and the commission, has a very special symbolic significance. it is absolutely important that the italian presidency should realise that this international criminal court is much more than an instrument to combat the americans and their unilateralism, it is a guarantee for us to regain european coherence in matters of both international policy and international law. we shall therefore be watching very closely what happens to this court.
i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the work of the european parliament. many passengers are confronted with a system, and that's what people are upset about. in europe alone, 250,000 people are victims of money transfers every year. that's not acceptable anymore. in future, compensation will have to be paid not only in the event of denied boarding but also in the event of cancellation and serious delay, and we welcome this. more importantly, however, passengers should be able to be confident about their flight and the contributions should therefore be high enough to encourage airlines to opt for good service rather than compensation. we want all this to apply not only to scheduled flights but also to so-called unscheduled flights. compensation shall not be payable in the event of force majeure. unfortunately, we find that a very vague concept. we would like to link this to a definition, for example of the european court of justice, so that it is more specific. the appeal to force majeure must not be an easy way of leaving the traveller to his fate without compensation. of course, finding a solution will often be more important to the traveller than compensation. in fact, if all transport operators were to have similar arrangements, we would have far fewer dissatisfied passengers on public transport.

the commission (es) madam president, mrs ghilardotti has already referred to the importance of the subject, its complexity, the experiences we have already followed in countries such as australia and south africa and the experiences we have also put into practice in the union. it is true that the commission first raised the issue at a technical seminar in october 2001 and that in 2002 the advisory committee on equal opportunities for women and men was asked to analyse the way in which the integration of the gender perspective in the budget is understood and raised in the member states and to collect existing experience. the committee on women's rights has issued an opinion which is available to this parliament, which has been circulated through the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities. the commission is of course prepared to draw up the brochure to which the honourable member has referred, which could be useful in providing this information, on the basis of this document. we already have some good practices in this direction in various areas, such as the european employment strategy, social protection, science and research, development cooperation and - perhaps the most interesting example - in the structural funds, where it is clear that equality between men and women is being tackled more effectively in the current programmes than in the previous period. clearly, much remains to be done, but progress is being made in this direction. the commission communication on mainstreaming gender equality in structural fund programming documents therefore recommends that member states clearly indicate the funds allocated to specific actions related to equality. member states will provide us with the relevant information, with precise indicators, as part of this year's review. the member states are also being urged to use gender-disaggregated statistics and gender impact assessments for structural fund interventions, while the commission is stressing the need to maintain and even increase, in most programmes, specific funding for activities dedicated to gender equality. the commission will continue to develop methodologies and tools to put gender mainstreaming into practice, but this means moving forward with our reflection on exchanging experiences and cooperating amongst all of us. we therefore consider your report on gender mainstreaming to be a very useful contribution to improving the application of the principle of gender mainstreaming. the fruitful cooperation which exists between parliament, the member states and the commission must be maintained and increased to achieve the objectives which we all want to achieve.
madam president, i would like to congratulate mrs ghilardotti on her initiative in drawing up this report. even though the commissioner says that a lot is already being done at european level, i see this report as a start and an encouragement to build on the little that is already being done. they say there is a lot of expertise or some expertise already, but i have to be honest, i haven't seen much of it. why is gender budgeting being called for? because some items in the budget of the member states and of europe, for the most part, benefit only half the population. i'll give you two examples. it is well known that when funding scientific research in health care, much research is done on half the population, on the male half and not on the female half. a second example. when money is given to promote democratization and respect for human rights in developing countries, it is often the case that this money is used primarily for the benefit of half the population and not the other half. that is why it is expressly asked that we perhaps start by analysing some budget lines which are very gender sensitive and where something could really be done. if this could be done in consultation with the member states, i think something positive could come out of it. of course, a brochure is good, but we are still only at the stage of developing a mechanism. in order to develop a mechanism, a certain amount of expertise must be built up. i support the commission, i support mrs ghilardotti and i hope that this own-initiative report will produce some results.

in writing. - the presentation of mrs kratsa's report, which i commend for its excellent work, is the reparation for a guilty silence on the fundamental role of women in rural development strategy. as we move towards gender budgeting, the need to be able to quantify and evaluate this participation to its full extent is a major concern. it is also a question of thinking about their social protection, the necessary and specific support for their vocational training and their quality of life. this is all the more so on the eve of enlargement. the aim of this recognition will only be validly achieved if the commission works to submit a revised directive which would finally allow the development of a genuine equality policy in this sector, which should translate into a balanced representation of women farmers in the various decision-making bodies, access to autonomous social rights, the principle of equal pay, a special focus on the needs of women in rural areas when selecting actions, and the extension of the leader+ programme to the new realities of enlargement. this is how we will emerge from the invisible as caring spouses and make democracy work.
- mr president, we have just learned from the reuter agency that the government of laos is prepared to pardon the two journalists and the american interpreter arrested on 4 june and sentenced on 30 june to 15 years in prison for unsubstantiated charges, which had aroused public indignation. i have here the press release from 1:30 p.m.: 'the laotian minister for foreign affairs is waiting for the governments of the three men's countries of origin, namely belgium, france and the united states, to ask for their pardon' - which will of course be done, if it has not already been done. we can only congratulate ourselves on the effective role played by our foreign ministers and diplomats in the country, a role which, without doubt, now gives us hope that liberation is imminent. while we are pleased with the outcome of these proceedings, we remain very concerned about the fate of the four laotians who accompanied thierry falise and vincent reynaud and who, we are told, are expected to serve a 20-year prison sentence. we are appealing to the lao government for leniency. too many journalists have paid with their lives or have been sentenced to long prison terms for doing their job: reporting. we recall that freedom of expression is a fundamental right. we expect the commission and the council to support the efforts of our diplomats and we want our european office in the region to intervene so that the four lao citizens are also released. we want to express to this house, mr president, our solidarity with journalists and their families who have suffered so much and to assure them that the european parliament, the guarantor of freedom, will unceasingly defend the right of the press, the right to free information and, more than ever, human rights, whatever the regime in power.
- mr president, in the ranking of press freedom in the world compiled by reporters without borders, laos occupies the unenviable 133rd place out of 140. for reference, italy is in 40th position, after benin and south korea. in laos, the media remains exclusively in the service of the one party, and the parliament is still delaying the passage of a law allowing the creation of private media, which was promised in 2001. there is still no news of the five missing leaders of the october 1999 movement, including thongpaseuth kenakoun, author of numerous articles and pamphlets on the situation in laos and the need for democratic reforms. the case of thierry falise and vincent reynaud is therefore not a judicial accident. it comes in a climate of continuous human rights violations and the scandalous verdict that sentenced them to 15 years in prison on 1 july raises at least two questions. the first is freedom of the press in general, which we have seen is often flouted, including in europe. this freedom is one of the pillars of democracy and we must defend it at all costs. the second is the democratic clause in cooperation agreements, which is often a mere formal clause, an empty shell. how can europe be taken seriously when, after signing an agreement, it fails to enforce this clause by appropriate sanctions? we know, as mrs andré-léonard confirms, that joint pressure from belgium, france, the united states and the european union will undoubtedly lead to the release of falise, reynaud and naw karl mua. that'll be a relief. but i expressly ask parliament to demand the release of thao moua, tha char yang, the two guides, pa phue khang, their driver, and a fourth lao unknown, because they are neither reporters nor foreigners. they're just laotians. the sentences imposed on them range from 12 to 20 years in prison and we cannot, mr president, wash our hands of this.

commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, this summer of 2003, which is not yet over, has been tragic in many ways. you asked earlier for a minute's silence in memory of the entire un team murdered in baghdad and there are other signs of the difficult and sometimes tragic summer we have just experienced. last thursday i flew to portugal and spent hours over the devastated regions of santarém, portalegre and castelo branco. it was a spectacle of desolation, the scale of which my colleagues diamantopoulou and vitorino had already been able to measure a few days earlier; a spectacle of desolation which also reminded me of the one i had been able to witness almost a year earlier, day after day, in germany, for other reasons. the summer of 2002 was also tragic for much of central europe, the east german länder, the czech republic, austria and france. in the face of such ecological disasters, not all of which are natural, as we know, in the face of their human consequences - in portugal eighteen people lost their lives in these fires - in the face of their economic consequences - hundreds of thousands of hectares destroyed and, therefore, thousands of farmers without work in portugal, in the centre of portugal, the poorest region - in the face of these thousands of people who have no reason to work if they are not offered a new job immediately, and who will be tempted to migrate to the towns or to the coast, we must act on all fronts and at all levels. in this context, it goes without saying that the level of intervention that we represent here, you, parliament, us, the commission and the council, is a good level. so i thank you again, mr president, i thank the european parliament and all the political groups for giving the commission the opportunity to take stock of what it is doing, what it wants to do, and what it should do at european level. a first tool, a first instrument of intervention is the european union solidarity fund. i can remember, ladies and gentlemen, practically my very first speech here in front of you. many of you know that i have long been interested in the environment and ecology and in october 1999, when i had just been appointed commissioner, i had to go to greece to assess the damage caused by an earthquake. i said then that we do not have an exceptional instrument to respond to exceptional situations, to tragedies, to exceptional natural disasters. we got only the usual, mundane answers. it took the disaster of last year, the floods in central europe, to lead to the creation of this solidarity fund. the initiative taken by the commission in this respect was immediately supported, and i would say very effectively, by your assembly: i do not forget the work of your rapporteurs, mr rolf berend and mr ralf walter, who supported us. the fund was set up in three months. with a reserve of €1 billion a year, it is now our main means of intervention to repair - i will say a word just now on prevention - the damage caused by fires such as those experienced in portugal and those still experienced in france today. not only has the fund itself been set up very quickly, but the response we are giving is extremely rapid. portugal's request reached the commission on 14 august and last week, a fortnight later, we proposed emergency aid of €31.6 million for relief, temporary accommodation, energy supply, repairs to basic infrastructure and animal feed, because everything is devastated. this year the solidarity fund has examined five applications and my services have accepted four (etna eruption in italy, earthquake in the molise region, prestige shipwreck and, of course, portugal) for a total of €88 million. in 2002, after only three or four months of existence, the fund granted eur 728 million in aid to germany, the czech republic and austria. as regards germany, i would like to inform parliament that i will be visiting dresden, in the land of saxony, this thursday, where i was a year ago. i will check, note and evaluate the use of eu funds to repair flood damage. ladies and gentlemen, apart from this solidarity fund for emergency measures which receive no european aid, we are not without other forms of support, particularly for rebuilding a destroyed economy; this is the purpose of the structural funds and the cohesion fund, which can be redeployed or reallocated at the request of governments. portugal has informed us that it will ask for the redeployment or reallocation of €182 million of community structural funds to deal with the reconstruction of the forestry or agricultural economy. as regards the structural funds, mr president, allow me to open a parenthesis: mr cox mentioned the departure from your assembly of a man who was very important to you and to me, as chairman of the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism, luciano caveri. i would like to take this opportunity to pay tribute to him and thank him for the quality and frankness of the relations we have had with this commission over the years. ladies and gentlemen, i have an idea that some of you may be raising: the idea that the criteria for the solidarity fund, as we have adopted them together on a proposal from the commission and as they have been adjusted or amended by the council, deserve an evaluation. i do not claim that these criteria, which we have applied consistently, objectively and impartially in every disaster for the past year and a half, are perfect. i therefore reserve the right to assess the functioning of the fund - we will have to do so before 31 december 2006 - and, if necessary, to propose a change in its criteria for assistance. because of the heat that promotes or accelerates fires, many regions, especially in southern europe, have experienced other forms of economic and human tragedy. i am thinking in particular of the drought, which some of you will no doubt mention. here, too, the damage is considerable. for example, in northern italy alone, damage to milk production was estimated at €70 million and damage to crops and fodder at €380 million. of course, in the specific case of drought, the european response cannot rely on the solidarity fund, which was not designed for this purpose. the fund is intended to deal with disasters which do not receive any aid. in the case of drought, and particularly in the context of the common agricultural policy, we had and have the means to intervene. in this connection, i would like to recall that at the initiative of my colleague, mr franz fischler, the commission adopted, at the beginning of july, a number of measures in favour of producers in all the regions concerned. these include the use of the vegetation cover of set-aside land, the use of certain intervention stocks of cereals and rice for animal feed, the possibility of increasing advance payments to 80% in the cattle sector and of anticipating certain payments in the cattle, sheep and arable crops sectors. of course, the commission is prepared to examine as soon as possible any applications for eligible state aid or amendments to the rural development programme which may be submitted by the governments of member states affected by drought. my third point concerns civil protection. i had the opportunity the other day in castelo branco to pay tribute to the volunteer firefighters who have done a great job, and wherever such disasters occur, there is a spirit of solidarity, cohesion and generosity. in the many emergency situations we have experienced, and are still experiencing, the question of the coordination of civil protection means nevertheless arises at european level. in the field of civil protection, there is a community response centre established by a council decision in october 2001 and responsible for implementing european cooperation in emergency situations. it is above all a centre of coordination and expertise which was very active in the case of fires - it was also active in the case of the erika and the prestige - and which used all the means available within its mandate. but experience, particularly in portugal this summer, has shown that the centre's current mandate unfortunately does not allow it to provide all the assistance needed in the event of a major disaster. that is why the commission, at the instigation of my colleague margot wallström, will present, by the end of september, the results of its reflections on how to respond to this situation and will make proposals. i think that this is an area where we need to be more ambitious in thinking about the financial and operational means to be used to enable more effective interventions, in particular from the centre i have just mentioned. whatever proposal we make, whatever reflection we put to you, i would like to remind you that civil protection is generally a national competence, it is often even a regional competence, and therefore nothing will be possible without the council of ministers asking the commission to submit a draft. i would also like to say that your assembly, as it has already done through some of its members, will have to tell us what it wants, how far it wants us to go in this matter. i mentioned the solidarity fund, the drought, the state of our next proposals and our thoughts on civil protection. i would like to conclude this talk by referring to a very old conviction for me. i have always thought that when we talk about natural disasters - again, not all disasters are natural, the prestige and the erika were not natural - but i have always thought that in all these cases prevention is cheaper than repair. i therefore want to express my concern about increasing prevention efforts at all levels: at the global level - i will remind you of this in conclusion, in relation to kyoto - and at the local, regional or national level. that is why, when we are reviewing the regional policy programmes at the mid-term review in a few months' time, i will be very attentive to requests from the states or regions for assistance under the 2000-2006 programming of the structural funds, for everything that will affect prevention. but i want to go further, beyond what we have done, beyond what we are doing at the moment, to make disaster prevention measures virtually compulsory in all regional programmes, as a result of our reflections, in the proposals that the commission will make on future regional policy, future cohesion policy. i also hope that we can find ways together to increase the budgetary effort for renewable energy or clean transport in regional or national programmes. moreover, since i am speaking of prevention, we know that to prevent it is necessary to understand, to know, to be well informed. in this respect, geographical information is an important element in risk prevention and management. the commission has developed a european forest fire information system. this system makes it possible to draw up a daily fire risk map valid for the whole of the european territory. it is already an integral part of the community support system for forestry civil protection services in the member states. finally, i would like to emphasize that this system also allows for a rapid and reliable estimate of the damage caused by forest fires. this is how we were able last week to estimate that over 400 000 hectares of portugal's land was destroyed by fires. i would like to conclude, ladies and gentlemen, by referring to the most important, most difficult and most necessary global prevention: the action of our member states and our union on climate change. if, as all climate scientists predict with increasing probability, the ongoing global warming is leading to major changes in the distribution of rainfall and drought conditions, then it is essential for the community and each of its members to stay ahead and lead by example in the fight against climate change. no country or even a group of developed countries like the union is in a position to solve such a problem on its own, each in its own country or each for itself. the union therefore hopes that the kyoto protocol, which has already been ratified by 113 countries representing more than two thirds of the world's population, will be able to enter into force as soon as possible. the union has already taken a number of measures to achieve the objectives set by the protocol. it has also integrated environmental principles into its strategy and decision-making process. that said, ladies and gentlemen, as you well know, the commitment or involvement of all the member states is unequal, and at the same time indispensable. the most recent emissions data show that 10 of the current 15 member states are still far from meeting their share of the greenhouse gas emission reduction target. on 22 july, the commission launched legal action to ensure compliance with eu legislation to improve air quality in europe. we have also, as you know, adopted a proposal for a regulation to reduce the emission of fluorinated greenhouse gases. this proposal represents a new step in the european union's efforts to meet our obligations under the kyoto protocol. finally - and franz fischler would say this better than i - the new environmental and ecological requirements are now being taken into account in the guidelines of the common agricultural policy. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, when we talk about natural disasters, about everything that has happened this summer, we naturally talk about the economy, we talk about the hundreds of thousands of hectares of devastated forests, about an agricultural economy that is severely handicapped for a long time, but we also talk, and perhaps first of all, about the men and women whose property and lives are affected. i cannot conclude this speech without mentioning the memory of the many thousands of our compatriots who have died as a result of the exceptional heat we have experienced in recent weeks. it may also be necessary to try to understand the situation in each of our member states, to think about ways of coordinating our actions and measuring instruments more closely and, perhaps, to make more significant progress in the field of public health in europe. as you know, i have been involved at length with klaus hänsch and inigo méndez de vigo, on your behalf, in the work of the convention, in the praesidium and in the convention. with many other representatives of the parliament, we have tried to improve the texts on european action in the field of public health. the text of the constitution is undoubtedly making progress, but i am not sure that it goes as far as it should when we see the true european dimension of certain problems. so, although this subject may seem a little more remote from the fires in portugal, i wanted to mention, in connection with the heat wave and other major scourges affecting the public health of european citizens, the need to go further in common, or even community, action in the field of public health. - mr president, i would like to thank commissioner barnier for the clarity and transparency of the report he has presented to us. so this summer of 2003 was hot, scorching all over europe, from rovaniemi to the algarve, and the consequences of this heat wave were dramatic in an unprecedented way. the european summer has been a cruelly unfair one, and as we have seen, the most vulnerable among us have been the main victims. nearly 20,000 elderly people, isolated, suffering from, in some cases, respiratory diseases, succumbed to this deadly heat. on behalf of the liberal group, i would first of all like to express my sincere condolences to the families of the victims. in addition to this human tragedy, this health shock that has not yet ended in france, the forests of the mediterranean countries have suffered almost irreversible damage. the most serious fires this summer have destroyed more than 400,000 hectares of forest. the human, economic and ecological toll could have been even heavier without the courage and efficiency of the firefighters, who are often volunteers, the red cross' assistance to the affected populations, the solidarity between european states, and not to mention the material cooperation of third countries. europe has done well: the commissioner mentioned the €31 million spent on solidarity with portugal - a solidarity which is at the very heart of our treaties - and the structural funds. another important point is that the €23 million or so that has been spent so far on the community programme for research and centralised management of forest fires has not been spent in vain. pilot projects for prevention, identification of risk areas and restoration of disaster areas have shown their usefulness in portugal in particular, but also their limitations. europe must do more. these huge fires in portugal and in the south of france have shown one thing: the firefighting air fleet is ageing in most member states. without the help of the neighbouring countries i mentioned, and especially without the help of the russian heavy tankers and their 12-ton tanks, well over 400 000 hectares would have been burnt to the ground. that is why the liberals are in favour - and i am answering one of your questions here - of the establishment of a european civil protection force which would be competent for forest fires, floods and also risks in the event of industrial disasters, among other things. such a strengthening of human resources must be accompanied by a strengthening of material resources. europe cannot lag behind canada, australia, the united states or russia; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i therefore expect commissioner wallström to make the proposals that his colleague mr barnier has mentioned. europe cannot ignore either, and it has not been mentioned until now, that man is responsible for 95% of these fires. it is urgent, indeed vital, to strengthen information campaigns among citizens. a cigarette thrown out of a car window, and it's a whole forest that can disappear. so, holding the negligent accountable is also our credo for prevention. finally, and i could have started with that, how can we not make a link between these recurring natural disasters and global warming? the liberal group supports the commission in all its efforts to reduce greenhouse gases. this summer, ozone pollution records were broken in all member states. despite the regular blows to the back, despite its limitations, the kyoto protocol must remain our tool, our minimalist objective for some, but also our voluntarist one. the european union must make it its main instrument in international negotiations, more than ever.
mr president, on the north sea, on the flemish coast, there is a lot of work being done to clean up the wreck of the tricolor. in that one-year period, there were nearly 60 accidents, it's almost inexplicable. last month we were surprised by three serious accidents on the scheldt river, in the direction of antwerp. this is no longer a coincidence. clearly, one of the most important ways of preventing accidents on ships is to have a well-trained crew. all steps to improve the quality of crews therefore deserve the support of this parliament. my group was very pleased with the speed with which the commission launched the proposal to establish a uniform system of seafarers' qualifications. my group has also insisted that this commission proposal should include an equal opportunities aspect in relation to the career path on board. the commission proposal is designed to ensure that non-european staff do not become second-class staff. there is also a lot of talk about the quality and social status of crews and the impact of these factors on the safety of shipping. we hope that an evaluation report after the adoption of the amendments to this directive will provide clarity on the relationship between crew training and the nature and number of accidents on board ships. this could be the first step on the long road to a true european flag.

continue producing in a domestic situation, characterised by a patent lack of purchasing power, and therefore to prepare themselves to compete on the national and regional markets when the post-conflict economic upturn begins. furthermore, the commission's intervention, via this ngo, will help avoid a situation where food aid produced outside the country weakens an agricultural sector already hard-hit by the country's political conditions, which are having huge repercussions on production. all this has given rise in kinshasa to a steep increase in the market price for maize. it rights, we also recall that discrimination on the grounds of disability is prohibited and that the integration of disabled people must be ensured. but above all, in its first article, the charter this end, it seems to me that our policies must be proactive in integrating people with disabilities into society as a whole on the same basis as any other citizen.

i congratulate mr van den bos on his very balanced report, which is in line with the human rights reports of recent years and which deals with delicate subjects such as freedom of religion in a very nuanced way. i fully endorse his call for greater consistency and coherence in human rights policy. the european union is in danger of losing all credibility if it fails to speak with one voice, even when it comes to strategically or economically important countries such as china or russia. mr van den bos rightly denounces the strong politicization of the most important human rights forum, the un commission on human rights. i myself have been twice on this un commission and i have always been struck by the systematic collusion of governments to acquit each other. the motto seems to be: if we spare others, we don't risk being judged ourselves. the recent meetings in geneva have shown once again that it is the foxes who have to guard the chicken eggs. it is highly worrying that countries such as libya, sudan, zimbabwe and cuba play a leading role in the un human rights commission and that the european union, the main sponsor, has little impact. i am therefore pleased that the rapporteur has accepted my amendments on this point. on the one hand, conditions must be imposed on membership of the un commission. such as the signing, ratification and observance of human rights treaties and the admission of un special rapporteurs on human rights. on the other hand, the decision-making process must be revised and the unanimity rule replaced by a two-thirds majority rule. if these conditions are not met, the european union must seriously consider withdrawing from the human rights circus. otherwise, it runs the risk of legitimising human rights violations instead of condemning them, and that can never be the intention.
- mr president, i am confused by two points in mr van den bos' excellent report. the first is the almost excessive emphasis it places on the religious phenomenon. the second is an implicit one that runs through the report, namely that freedom of conscience is guaranteed by tolerance of pluralism of beliefs. i shall not discuss the first point, it is a political choice of the rapporteur and he has explained it. but on the second, i.e. the pluralism of beliefs as a support of human rights in a society, i am infinitely more skeptical. first, because this view places atheists, agnostics, in the category of infidels, a kind of default state. but also, more profoundly, because i am convinced that only the structural secularism of a state or a federation of states is capable of guaranteeing freedom of conscience for everyone. this structural secularism places beliefs where they are essential, i.e. in the private sphere. whether you are an atheist, a catholic, a jew, a muslim, or a member of any other peaceful sect, it does not matter. i would also like to quote the words of anna morelli, a belgian historian of religions, who said of sects: "what is a sect but a religion which has not received the support of a political power?" but in the public sphere, and especially in the political sphere, all religious interference must be banned, all occult influence denounced. these are the only real guarantees of the freedom of conscience of citizens, and indeed of their freedom in general. the van den bos report would have been even more impactful if it had reached its conclusions there.

- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, this report, which makes it possible to examine the situation of fundamental rights in the european union each year, is an excellent stimulus for those who would like to forget the universality of fundamental rights. the pill is sometimes bitter, but i think very healthy. i would like to stress in particular the strengthening of european citizenship, which is unquestionably linked to the right to vote in local and european elections. in many of our member states, legal residents are denied this right on the grounds that they are non-nationals or non-europeans. at a time when we are developing a constitutional model that bans exclusion and discrimination in the enlarged europe, this situation must end, otherwise our european project and the values we defend will lose all credibility. the timeframe is short, but the assembly will have to meet this major challenge in the coming months.
mr president, i would like to ask you to take this opportunity to thank the rapporteur for the excellent work he has done and to thank him for the excellent report he has presented. these are two joint proposals. i would like to start by recalling the situation faced by a large minority of human beings on the planet with regard to access to water. 1.7 billion people do not have access to safe drinking water. three billion people do not have access to sanitation. every day, 30,000 people die from a disease related to the lack of clean water or sanitation. water is a natural resource that is essential for life, health, nutrition and well-being. the united nations committee on economic, social and cultural rights has listed access to water as a fundamental human right. the 145 countries that have ratified the international covenant on economic, social and cultural rights have an obligation to guarantee access to water to people in a fair and non-discriminatory manner. the question that is therefore being posed more and more acutely is: what cooperation policy is needed to help poor countries meet the major challenge of providing access to drinking water for all? as early as 1991, at the end of the world water and sanitation decade, an international commitment was proclaimed to provide safe drinking water for all by the year 2000. two years after that deadline, in johannesburg in 2002, it was clear that this commitment was far from being met, despite the efforts made, in particular, by the world bank. in fact, to some extent, the situation has worsened. a new target was set in johannesburg, which can be described as a discount target: to halve by 2015 the number of people without access to safe drinking water and sanitation. the european union wants to play an active part in achieving this objective and the commission sets out its priorities in the communication which has been presented to us. the committee on development, in the draft resolution which will be put to the vote tomorrow at noon, suggests adopting the following priorities, knowing that it is not credible to continue on the path traced in 1993 by the world bank in its report on water management. it has been found that the privatisation of water services, based on the principle of total cost recovery, has most often resulted in higher prices preventing the most disadvantaged people in the poorest countries from having access to water. the solution thus found does not solve the problem but rather, to a certain extent, amplifies it. we therefore believe it is essential that water distribution is considered a public service. public-private partnerships can be one way, among others, to improve access to water, but they are not the panacea. we also believe that it is necessary to adopt a pricing system that allows everyone to have access to water to meet basic needs and ensures efficient use of water by empowering users. as regards management, it is the right and duty of the countries concerned to adopt a policy which clearly involves the people in management. the committee on development and cooperation considers that substantial financial resources must be mobilised to make the necessary investments in the countries concerned, and welcomes the creation of a european water fund. however, it considers that the main objective of the fund should be to support the water policy of the beneficiary countries, which should be based on democratic management and equitable distribution. we therefore propose that the fund be managed on the basis of an acp-eu partnership. the committee on development also believes that the success of the fund depends on the participation of the beneficiaries, who must be involved in the design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation process, as well as in the management and control of the fund, as i have already said. we also believe that new financial instruments must be devised which call for international solidarity, and in this connection the first thing that comes to mind is of course to ask for the cancellation of the acp countries' debts. the amounts thus released would enable these countries to finance basic infrastructure, water supply and water sanitation. we would also like the commission to study - i say 'studies', not to make a proposal as such - the possibility of introducing a tax on bottled mineral water in the european countries and the acp countries concerned, a very low tax of around half a euro cent, which would make it possible to make a significant contribution to the fund. finally, i would like to inform the various political groups of my attitude to the amendments tabled. amendment no 1 tabled by the socialist group perfectly complements the mr president, it is high time that we joined forces and helped the developing countries to achieve greater prosperity. the best way to achieve this is through a general free trade system which offers the best guarantees for sustainable economic growth in all parts of the world. it is true that trade liberalisation is the key to development, but this also requires an important nuance. i am in favour of the free market, not the unfettered or unlimited free market. the free market no longer exists. today we are talking about a socially corrected market, with attention to social standards, the environment, consumer protection, respect for intellectual property rights and so on. the involvement of less developed countries in the world trading system must continue to take account of these preconditions. in the next round of wto negotiations, we must, from europe's point of view, see the developing countries as our partners rather than our adversaries. i do not share the view of rapporteur morgantini, who says that there is no evidence that liberalisation is stimulating the growth of wealth. globalisation offers benefits to all countries that engage in the world economy. similar countries that have followed a different trade strategy show marked differences in per capita income. i am thinking, for example, of south korea against north korea, of east germany against west germany before unification. i am thinking of the asian tigers against the south asian countries. or even more flagrantly, about 20 years ago, china and africa were at the same level of development. since china has resolutely embarked on the path of an open economy and has integrated itself into the world trading system, the consequences have been spectacular. on average, chinese people today earn three times as much as africans. not only in terms of income levels, but also in terms of knowledge levels, the difference is enormous. it may well be that globalisation has made the rich richer, but that it has made the poor poorer is patently wrong. the figures from the united nations prove it. the share of the world's population living in extreme poverty has fallen from 29 percent to 23 percent in ten years. by the end of the 1990s, 826 million people were undernourished, 40 million fewer than in the early 1990s, despite the population explosion. the global infant mortality rate has declined from 64 per 1000 in 1980 to 56 per 1000 in the 1990s. about 80 percent of people in developing countries have access to a good water supply, and illiteracy has declined to 20 percent. it's not perfect, but it's moving in the right direction. so there's a lot of work to be done, but there's also hope. a proper globalization is the way ... i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. first of all, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr ebner, on his report. in this report, he draws on the positive experience of the european year of languages to highlight the growing awareness in europe that languages are bridges to cultures and cultures to people, and that we are therefore - i would like to put it bluntly - finally no longer dreaming of the 'american model': one language and no culture, but that we are really discovering in the diversity of our languages and the diversity of our cultures a trump card and even a fundamental value for our future. knowing another's language and culture means understanding him better, and it also means respecting him. i believe that language has an essential role to play, namely that knowing the language of the other is also a means of promoting peace. if we look at the hotspots in and around europe, we find that the lack of respect for otherness - and that includes languages - has very often led to the emergence and development of these hotspots. so for me, language is more than just knowing and respecting the other person. i believe that language is the best peacekeeping force we can deploy in europe. but the report also shows that in an ever-growing europe, awareness of regional cultures and languages is also growing. this must be respected - and i come from the german-speaking community in belgium and can see it that way - not as a luxury of autonomy for certain regions, but in my view as a fundamental european right. the first task of governments - we have just mentioned this - is to guarantee the smaller regions, their cultures and their right to say what they want. we should then consider at european level what instruments we can add to this. we appreciate the work of my colleague ebner from south tyrol and our representation here in parliament. you know that in italy and belgium, they speak german...
- but that doesn't stop us from speaking the language of our neighbors. we do it with the conviction of our own culture and also with love for the culture of others. we dream of this becoming the standard for the europe of tomorrow. speaking one's own language, speaking the language of one's neighbour; getting to know one another better and, from there, building the foundations of a europe of all languages and, above all, a europe of respect for others.
in writing. - culture is economically important, but it is above all a question of citizenship. the diversity and quality of the cultural offer and the capacity to participate in cultural creation are essential elements of a democratic society. in this sense, public intervention is crucial. the aim is to support cultural creation and production financially, to ensure its diversity and vitality and to avoid its concentration in the hands of a few multinational companies, often american. however, public aid systems are now under threat from the logic of the market. in the eu, these schemes can be considered 'state aid', prohibited as contrary to the sacrosanct european competition law. we must regret the outcome of the convention, which leaves the existing texts as they are, instead of promoting cultural aid as aid which is automatically authorized. a formidable sword of damocles therefore hangs over our cultural policies. at international level, the cultural exception must be safeguarded, particularly in relation to the gats, by ensuring that the rule of unanimity is maintained with regard to trade agreements in the field of culture.

in writing. - (nl) respect for and safeguarding linguistic and cultural diversity in europe is an honourable objective. any erosion of this diversity would be an impoverishment of our cultural heritage. measures to avoid such an erosion, such as the creation of a european agency for linguistic diversity and language learning, should therefore be supported. however, the draft resolution is explicitly based on the council of europe's framework convention for the protection of national minorities. the flemish government has always refused - and rightly so - to sign this text, because the french-speaking belgians living in flanders intend to abuse it to impose the recognition of french as an official language in flanders, which is incompatible with the language legislation adopted after great difficulties and with the principle of territoriality in belgium. this problem is mainly about people who refuse to conform to the dutch-speaking character of the region and who make the non-learning of another language (in this case dutch) the subject of a political debate. since the report does not propose any measures to prevent groups of people who have settled in the region fairly recently from exploiting the treaty to break a hard-won linguistic balance, the representatives of the vlaams blok will vote against it.

carefully whether they're okay or not. the prestige, if i may believe all the reports, has not been inspected properly on one or both of its ballast tanks. i think it is important that we also take a look at the classification society and ask them if they cannot tighten up their regulations on this point. it is not enough to look at the condition of a ship at a given moment and to test it every few years. one must also look at the way the ship is used. in the case of the prestige, there may have been a problem with the use of the ship as a floating reservoir and that may have weakened the structure of the ship. this has not been sufficiently taken into account because it was not included in the regulations. we have to make sure that things like that can't happen in the future. one solution we have put forward - or at least one decision that has been taken very quickly - is the decision to transport the kind of material that was carried in the prestige only in double-hulled vessels. i agree with that in principle, but we cannot regard this as a kind of miracle solution, because new questions arise in connection with double-walled ships. how do double-walled ships react as they age? now all the ships are new and everything is perfectly fine, but are these ships still good when they are 20 or 25 years old? because there are specialists who say that there will be problems with rust and weakening. the same things that happened to the prestige can happen to double-walled ships. we must be very careful about that. i would like to see both the classification society and the agency keep a close eye on this. we also have suggestions for the tightening of port state control and we have suggestions for the proper follow-up of the monitoring directive. there i have personally followed this case very closely from the beginning, because i, and many others like me, have experienced this disaster as an attack on europe. ten months after the disaster, we are debating the resolution which is the result of the research of our good colleague, mr dirk sterckx. it's a real investigation, because he even went to the captain of the prestige to talk to him. the captain, who was not allowed to attend the transportation committee hearing. i was with them when we went on the fact-finding mission to la coruña and brest. i would point out that the level of openness among the competent maritime authorities varied greatly. apparently, openness is still a difficult issue. the resolution gives a clear analysis of the facts which led to the prestige disaster. it clearly identifies a number of actions which the union must take. the pain points are mentioned, for example port state control, and a good analysis of the necessary environmental measures is given. i would therefore like to congratulate the rapporteur on the work he has done. i am pleased with the result, as are my group's colleagues. if the council and the commission are careful to follow what is written here, the call 'nunca mais' (never again) will be heeded. the commission's work shows that the message has been received. the european maritime safety agency will be given the mandate and the means to act actively. the resolution also indicates the limits of the procedure followed. my group was initially not happy with the procedure followed on this matter. we have called from the outset for a parliamentary committee of inquiry or a temporary committee to 'find out the facts' of the matter. how unfortunate that expression sounds in this context! madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, no one disputes that, given the european union's great dependence on the supply of petroleum products, initiatives must be taken to ensure the continuity of the supply of these products. the commission is indeed proposing a number of measures to ensure the continuity of oil supplies, but one may well ask, as many other members have already done for me, whether these measures can achieve the objective they seek. the most essential measures in the commission's proposal cannot, in any case, be deprived of my support. i'll sum them up here in triplicate. firstly, the increase in the security stocks from 90 days to 120. why should the european union increase its stocks when the international energy agency has a global emergency response mechanism?
