the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to thank mr olle schmidt for his concise and clear report on this austrian initiative. and i would like to make it clear that this is not the commission's initiative. this is an initiative of a member state, it is an austrian initiative, of the austrian government and therefore some of the questions which the honourable member has put to me cannot be answered by the commission because this is not a commission proposal. however, the austrian initiative raises a complex problem, which has to do with the use of the concept of safe third countries in the european asylum system. the council has examined this question at expert level and i would say that at this stage there is a council position in principle in favour of examining it in depth at the next meeting on 2 and 3 october. as you know, in june five member states made a joint declaration calling on the council to draw up a common list of safe countries of origin. and what the council is discussing at the moment is the possibility of including a legal rule on the form of the definition of a list to be included in the proposal for a directive, this one from the commission, on asylum procedures. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in any case, both the subject of safe countries of origin and the subject of safe third countries will be discussed at the next council meeting in early october. and from the commission's point of view, it will be necessary for the council to consult the european parliament again on the whole of the directive on asylum procedures, including also in this framework any possible rule on safe third countries or safe countries of origin which may possibly be included, which, as i said, is not yet consolidated.
mr president, widespread abuse of asylum is a very serious problem in the european union and it would be inappropriate to discuss the sex of angels endlessly. indeed, let us adopt as soon as possible a new directive on the criteria for the recognition of refugee status, and let us start with minimum criteria, whereby member states can and should apply stricter standards. europe must have the appropriate instruments to control the asylum problem. in belgium, for example, more than 98% of asylum applications are found to be unfounded. the treatment of all these cases requires time, manpower and resources which cannot be used for the treatment of the serious cases. the resulting backlog then leads to the non-processing of files and, ultimately, to the granting of residence permits without any examination. this inefficiency and laxity only result in a further influx of false asylum seekers. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not affected by the crisis. it is therefore surprising that the report asks how the list of safe countries relates to the principle of individual treatment as laid down in the geneva convention. it is not at all sensible to use a list of safe third countries on the one hand and to treat individual dossiers from these countries in the same way as other dossiers on the other. we must have the courage to recognize and acknowledge that the geneva convention is in need of revision. the convention dates from 1951 and was mainly intended to take in refugees from communist dictatorships. it's a product of the cold war, no longer adapted to today's needs. the list of safe countries should not be limited to this, but the principle of territoriality should also be included. asylum seekers are best received in neighbouring countries in their own region, so that asylum policy in europe is no longer abused to circumvent the immigration stop on a large scale.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, mr schmidt's report gives the essence, in a concise and precise way, of the options we want to defend in this house. as a democrat and one of the parliamentarians who asked for the charter of fundamental rights to be included as such in our draft constitution, i cannot but agree with his conclusions. i would like to add to the facts and points which have been very well put forward by my various colleagues three points. the geneva convention was drafted in the middle of the cold war, at a time when the world was polarized. since then, the european union has become a pole of growth that in the majority of the world's states represents a hope, a goal to be reached to escape poverty, war and political instability. the dramatic increase in social, religious and ethnic tensions and civil wars is causing states to break up and individuals to despair. their presence and their exodus to our countries remind europe that poverty and crisis are rampant at its doorstep. europe has a special role to play and the answer is not a 'fortress europe'. yes, we need a legal framework. yes, we must be very careful about the choices we make. however, in my view, a list of safe countries is neither useful nor desirable. by listing them, we would be violating article 3 of the geneva convention which prohibits discrimination on the grounds of race, religion or country of origin. to refuse to take into account an asylum seeker's application on the basis of a list of so-called 'safe' countries is to deny the refugee his most basic right, the right to be a fully fledged individual with inalienable rights. i shall conclude on the urgent need for a european directive on the asylum procedure and, above all, for a concerted community policy. without this fundamental legal basis, our actions will not be consistent.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, mr kirkhope's report on transit assistance in the context of air-sea measures has already been discussed in this house. unfortunately, the subject has been on the front page of the belgian news recently: let us remember the way in which semira adamu was treated, with the known disastrous consequences. it is clear that all these removal measures are rarely carried out in practice with the respect and dignity that every individual is entitled to expect, especially from this european body which so often stands as a paragon of human rights. on the other hand, when we send these people back or when we send them away, we often do not know what awaits them when they return home and i think we are showing an undignified indifference to the responsibilities we have been given. behind this text, at the heart of these expulsions, are people in transit, men and women who have the right to safety and respect. with regard more particularly to the conditions of removal and residence of thirdcountry nationals, i would like to draw attention to two very important points. first, there are the unacceptable and even inhumane living conditions of hundreds of adults and children in closed centres, which are land transit zones. i also think it is urgent to solve the problem of asylum seekers living in the transit areas of airports. these zones are not designed to accommodate them and no one can or should live in a no-go zone. couldn't we consider other ways of doing things which would be more respectful of human dignity? finally, i would like to conclude by saying that we must give a concrete twist to the various directives we have been discussing here for almost two years, so that our policies bear witness to our unconditional respect for the right to dignity and fundamental rights and our refusal to take any action that might harm the physical or moral integrity of people, and so that we can finally have a credible and honourable immigration policy at european level.
will we all be able to use our computers without paying patent fees? that is the question we all have to ask ourselves. what we don't want is american conditions. a situation in which patents can be granted on simple computer languages or software. but we disagree less on how to define this. we also often run after reality; you see what happens at the european patent office. we cannot therefore afford to delay any longer and must seek a proper clarification of what is patentable and, above all, what is not. i myself believe that the right balance has been struck in the position of the committee on industry and in the amendments by my colleagues mr manders and mr plooij. any form of software is explicitly excluded from patentability. only if there is a genuine invention should it be patentable. it must therefore be a technical process with industrial applications, not just an idea or a language. the piece of technology that this new technical process involves must be protected. not the fact that you need a computer to use it.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, this report is being lobbied heavily because there is a lot at stake. i understand that and i share your concern. this directive, which may be well-intentioned, is a threat to many small and mediumsized enterprises in europe, which are in danger of losing their creativity because of all these patents. these small and mediumsized enterprises are the vanguard of business. we must encourage these smes, because they are the ones who are driving innovation. it is this innovation that will enable us to maintain our position on the world market. we are against this directive because it means over-regulation. the current copyright protection is sufficient. we greens are often accused of being the rule-keepers of our constituency. well, now we have proof that this is not who we are. in addition, there is now a weakening of patent legislation in the united states, which is seen as counterproductive. why should we in europe pass an outdated law?
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your reply. at a time when we want to boost innovation, growth and competitiveness in the european union, under the guise of simple harmonisation and legal clarification of divergent practices and interpretations in this area, this directive represents a real regression, a real change of regime compared to the european patent convention and the software copyright directive, because it aims to extend the possibilities of patentability of software to the maximum. the directive should therefore be rejected because it is too vague. first, computers have become a crucial tool in the transmission of ideas, thought and knowledge. excessive private appropriation of this tool is therefore dangerous, whereas copyright is more appropriate. secondly, from an economic point of view, software innovation is often the work of smes, independent researchers, for whom the patent is not a suitable instrument, too expensive, too burdensome, and will create a legal jungle. there is no evidence that protection will develop innovation, growth and competitiveness. finally, in the context of the economic rivalries between the united states and the community, the adoption of this directive would allow a few large monopoly firms to maintain their dominant position on the market. we will therefore wait for a directive to be drawn up after further study of the economic implications of innovation, competition and ownership policies in this sector.
mr president, commissioner, if it is up to the epp, there will be no software patents, and if it is up to you, commissioner, there will be no software patents. that is how i understood it, after proposing it, after reading the existing legislation on the subject - and the numerous letters we have all received - more than once. many in our group support the principles and objectives of the commission's proposal. i hope, commissioner, that you will support a number of amendments tabled on behalf of our group which, building on the report by mrs mccarthy and the report by the legal affairs committee, will define matters more precisely and give a more explicit form to the monitoring mandate. mr president, i do not believe the persistent claims that this proposal would be particularly bad for small and mediumsized enterprises. more legal certainty and uniform application of the rules of law in the internal market are things that smes in particular need. in my view, smes do not have a specific problem with this directive, but they do have a general problem with the patent system as such. many of the criticisms in the letters are also due to this. this applies both when they themselves enjoy patent protection and when they are confronted with patent claims from others. i therefore ask you, commissioner, whether you and your administration will not think creatively about how, through a european approach, smes can be given a better place in the patent islands. i have not tabled an amendment on this because i think that this is not legally appropriate in this text. i think it is politically important enough to make this suggestion now and i would therefore be very grateful if you would also give me an answer.
the rapporteur. mr president, amendment no 11 is about a temporary committee. it is an amendment which i myself tabled because the two large groups had tabled a proposal for a temporary committee, but there was no agreement on the substance. i try to keep out of the political debate which is taking place in one of the member states of this european union. i've always tried that, and i'm trying it again. that is why i have tabled this oral amendment, which i think will make it easier to combine different proposals. i shall read it in english because that is the language in which the amendment was drafted.
the rapporteur. mr president, i had written, on the basis of previous data, that there would still be 13 000 tonnes in the wreck. i was in contact yesterday with the people trying to get this oil out and i was told that there are still 14,000 tons in the wreckage. so i would like to change those two numbers.
in writing. - maritime safety must be strengthened in europe, it must even become a priority, otherwise we risk seeing all our seas and oceans polluted in the near future, as is the case with the atlantic coast with the successive shipwrecks of the oil tankers erika and prestige. this is why the member states must not only remove single-hull oil tankers, which are often the cause of disasters, from traffic as soon as possible, but also impose stricter rules on airlines issuing airworthiness certificates and strengthen inspections in ports. in short, they must apply the erika package of december 1999 without delay. that is why i voted for the excellent report by my colleague, mr dirk sterckx. it includes a whole series of innovative measures to prevent and combat black tides effectively: a european coastguard service, a fund for compensating victims, the provision of safe havens to accommodate vessels in 'distress'. the commission is also proposing the setting up of a temporary parliamentary committee to shed light on the prestige disaster, find those responsible and act for the future. so that once and for all, as is the case in the united states, with the oil pollution act, the polluter really is the payer.
mr president, we were all a little hasty because we do not want to delay tomorrow's debate on the presentation of the budget unnecessarily. i have a real point of order, mr president. i am referring to the rules of procedure because i believe that the conference of presidents and the president of parliament are in breach of the rules of procedure, and in particular article 3 of annex xiii. yesterday, commission president prodi sent a letter to mr cox to arrange how the members of the committee on budgetary control and the members of the conference of presidents will deal with the documents on the ongoing investigation into eurostat. mr prodi has proposed a formula in accordance with the second indent of article 3.2 of annex 3. that is the second-least strict rule, so we shall be looking into the matter tomorrow night behind closed doors. however, according to the agreement between parliament and the commission, this is to be negotiated between the president of parliament and the president of the commission. i would therefore ask mr cox and the conference of presidents to recommend a more favourable arrangement. the second point is that the meeting between the conference of presidents, the members of the committee on budgetary control and commission president prodi will take place behind closed doors on thursday. however, all the press people in the chamber below tell me that the spokesman for president prodi has said that if parliament wishes this meeting can be held in public. i think it is in accordance with our dignity and also in our interest to ask very explicitly that this meeting be held in public. i think that all citizens in europe have a right to it and that it would be counterproductive if it were not done. i would therefore ask you, mr president, to convey these two questions very clearly to mr cox. i shall be most grateful to you for that.
the committee on foreign affairs, human rights and common security and defence policy has adopted a report on the situation in the middle east. president, the draft budget. the council has cut only 0.3% of the initial proposals of the european commission for heading 4, external measures, which represented an increase of 3.9% compared with last year. the european union, as we all know, is making more and more commitments. these concern the fight against terrorism and organised crime, the reconstruction of iraq, mismanaged states such as afghanistan and the congo, enlarged europe, conflict prevention in the middle east and so on. these are important issues, and it is also clear that the population is in favour of a more assertive foreign policy: 67 per cent of europeans are in favour of more european union in the world. on the other hand, it is clear that the financial limits of the possible commitments have been reached, unless more money is put on the table. in a few weeks' time the european union will be participating in the international donor conference on the reconstruction of iraq. commissioner patten suggests a possible eu contribution of around €200 million for the period 2003-04. others propose an amount of 500 million euro. in any case, our committee on foreign affairs does not want this to come at the expense of other geographical areas and commitments, for example in relation to afghanistan, or of non-geographical budget lines, such as the rapid reaction mechanism and landmines. if the european union is to assume its share of responsibility for peace and security in the world, it must also be prepared to allocate the necessary financial resources. if not, europe risks remaining a political dwarf on the international stage.
the rapporteur. - mr president, commissioner, as you yourself have pointed out, this proposal has a long history. i will not go into all the historical details, but i would like to point out, in connection with what you have just said, that in 1999 the european parliament adopted a report, the rapporteur of which was pat cox, which improved the commission's proposal, particularly in three respects: parliament called for the increase of all the minimum rates announced in the commission's proposal; he also asked that these minima be indexed; finally, it called for a reduction in the number of derogations and exemptions. as you yourself have pointed out, it took four years for the council to reach an agreement and for parliament to be consulted again. the report generally aims to support the compromise reached in the council, which was forced to reach unanimity as you said, despite the major shortcomings in the text. the council of ministers did not accept the proposals in the 1999 cox report. i would like to point out two points among others on which the council's positions seem weak to us. firstly, the minimum rates indicated by the council and to be applied from 1 january 2004 are, almost without exception, lower than those proposed by the commission in 1997 and by the european parliament in 1999. such a backsliding is in flagrant contradiction to the council's stated commitment, and in particular the european council's, to present instruments aimed at decoupling economic growth from energy consumption. the second issue we have considered is that of derogations and exemptions. the text of the directive is full of them. the council added an annex with a list of purchases, exemptions and individual derogations so that each of the 15 member states could be included in this list and thus give its consent. referring to the position adopted by parliament in 1999 and taking into account new political elements such as the declaration of the gothenburg european council on a sustainable development strategy, i therefore, as rapporteur, proposed to the committee on economic and monetary affairs that the council's position be strengthened on certain points, while remaining moderate in view of the different political sensitivities. i proposed, in particular, to bring forward by two years the second stage of fuel taxation and the revision of tax rates. i also proposed a clause for the gradual abolition of all derogations, a clause which would allow exemptions and derogations to be maintained only in cases where the council actually decides to do so. finally, i proposed mandatory support for renewable energy. in principle, all forms of renewable energy should be fully exempted. i must admit that i was unpleasantly surprised by the rejection of all the amendments by the committee on economic and monetary affairs - and in this i do not share your approval, mr commissioner. it is true that it has often rejected them by a majority of one vote and when the committee was not full. i therefore think it is logical for me, as rapporteur, to reintroduce these amendments in the plenary. i would also like to draw your attention, commissioner, to amendment no 2, tabled by my colleague mrs kauppi and voted on in committee. if it were to be confirmed in plenary, it would mean that parliament rejects the very advisability of this proposal for a directive on the grounds that we have just voted a directive on emissions trading. in conclusion, i would like to say that the vote by parliament in september 2003 is not different from the one that parliament itself adopted in 1999, albeit in the context of another parliamentary term.
i am speaking here on behalf of those who dream of a truly united europe based on regions and peoples. the proposed constitution does not bring us that europe. as the tsatsos report says, it is an important step forward in the democratization of the union. but we are not yet getting the union that is as effective and as transparent as it is capable of playing a role on the world stage and inspiring confidence in its citizens. sovereignty rests solely with the member states with their own constitutional order. the diversity of languages and cultures is recognised only within the member states which grant it. the regions with constitutional competence will see a dozen new member states join the union. member states which are sometimes smaller than their own constitutionally empowered regions. this applies to flanders, scotland, catalonia, wales and so on. perhaps these sovereign states are even less willing to cooperate in the creation of a real union. that will be clear in the igc. but the small states are also our allies. i would like to call on them to help defend the result achieved in this convention. in any case, a certain balance has been achieved in these texts. there is a certain unity in diversity, but we must continue to defend together these latter aspects.
- mr president, commissioner, i would like to say that you are right. i regret the political weakness of the message conveyed by the european parliament's report. the draft constitutional treaty is clearly better than the treaty of nice, but it is worth pointing out the serious shortcomings which, if maintained by the igc, will seriously hamper the government and the future development of a union of 27. in particular, the maintenance of unanimity for the decision-making process on crucial issues and the procedure for future treaty revision are problematic. politically, on the eve of the igc opening, and in view of the positions taken by governments who wish to renegotiate certain articles of the treaty, the european commission was right to point out in its remarkable opinion the weaknesses of the project and the need to improve it. as a political signal, parliament is wrong and the european commission is right; i therefore vote against the european parliament's report, in disagreement with a majority of my colleagues.
- mr president, my colleagues and i voted for all the amendments which could improve the text, and we were pleased to see some improvements but also, with great regret, some failures. these have strengthened our conviction that the directive, far from encouraging dynamism and creativity, is giving way dangerously to the bureaucratic desire to regulate even inventiveness itself. we have seen that this slide has been in favour of the big financial and industrial groups. that is why we voted against the final report.
in writing. - (nl) the vlaams blok is opposed to the patentability of inventions implemented by computer, since software patenting in practice slows down the innovation capacity of small and medium-sized enterprises in the it sector. the computer giants (especially in the us) are already abusing these patents far too often, with huge in-house legal services to sue the "offenders". such practices are detrimental to free competition, since many patents relate to software which is de facto in the public domain. applying for and obtaining patents is an expensive business. creative it companies starting up in europe rightly consider that the legal aspects arising from this are not part of their core business. europe is cutting the grass under its own feet by adopting this report.
in writing. - i abstained on the mccarthy report because the arguments of the rapporteur and the european commission in favour of the patentability of computer-implemented inventions are not substantiated. i am convinced that software patents are the perfect weapon for companies with an army of lawyers who are trained in the logic of legal blackmail. this all-round investment in patents is aimed at weakening or even eliminating competition. as a liberal, i cannot accept this monopolistic commercial logic, which ultimately aims to kill the creative spirit of young computer scientists and the innovation brought to the sector by the manufacturers and users of free software. that is why i supported all the amendments which would give this report a human face. the commission has also adopted a number of measures to limit the scope of patentable software. finally, i welcome the fact that a clear signal has been given to the european patent office in munich. the european parliament calls for transparency and accountability. this is the least that can be done for this organization which, in violation of the european patent convention of 1973, has allowed patents to be granted on over 30 000 computer programs.

president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i would like to congratulate mr stockton on this report, which my group fully supports. when we talk about the future of europe and the future constitution, we sometimes make the comparison with a scale. on the one hand, the union must be democratic and open, and on the other, it must be efficient and functional. we in parliament must guard that balance and ensure that it does not go wrong and openness is sacrificed. the first european ombudsman, mr jacob söderman - mr president, mr president-in-office of the council and mr president of the commission, several of my colleagues have already pointed out the precarious economic situation and also called for structural remedies to be introduced. however, we are bombarded daily with opposite messages. in this country, belgium, the news this week that 3,000 workers at ford genk were to be laid off was received very strongly. the fact that a reduction in sales of a particular car model can have such an effect, that heavy and promising investments have been cut and that all these jobs disappear at once, can also have major consequences for the rest of europe. today, a major newspaper ran an editorial entitled: "will ford disappear from all of europe?" i do not want to go into this further, but i would ask you to propose realistic solutions which no longer give the impression that employment is something that can be manipulated by capital. people now feel that they are being sold. a second concern you have expressed is the concern for peace. you know that our great concern for the middle east is growing every day. since the war in iraq, peace has not been won. we want the united nations and the european union to play their part in this, but we see with sadness how the idea of military solutions seems to be growing throughout the region. the fact that a wall is now being built, partly even in the occupied territories, to separate israel from the palestinians is a flagrant violation of what the road map is about. the fact that new settlements are still being planned is a blow to the road map. where does the roadmap lead? it's a road to nowhere. we must act. we must monitor this plan on the ground and we must ensure that the situation does not escalate further. what happened after the dramatic attack in haifa, which we deplore and condemn, an attack deep in syria, that is an invitation to a new military escalation! what has been decided in the turkish parliament, namely to send troops from turkey to iraq, against the wishes of the turkish people, but under american pressure, also against the wishes of the provisional government and the iraqi kurds, is clearly intended to threaten the pkk. the turkish people rightly fear that this will set in motion a new spiral of violence. the iranian regime, which has been so heavily criticised by its own public opinion, is also playing along with the situation in iraq. they are the winners! they will have greater influence over their shiite co-religionists. and not just in iraqi society, but even in the government! they are now pressing to destroy the mujahideen. once again, there is an attempt to impose a military solution, a sham solution. and we keep talking about steps on the road to peace. i ask the european countries, the commission and the presidency of the council to provide answers to these pressing questions. can we continue to work with israel uncritically in these circumstances? can we continue to implement the association agreement? can we remain blind to the apparent presence of nuclear weapons in israel, which are being kept secret as a political tool, but also as an alibi to facilitate conventional wars? isn't this the place to look for weapons of mass destruction? shouldn't the atomic energy agency be doing its job here? i therefore ask for a less painful and more active attitude on the part of the quartet and i hope that we can do something to stop the war from spreading so that we can set out again in the direction of genuine peace.
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, this is not a politically controversial issue, but it is a matter of aviation safety and therefore in the interest of all of us and in the interest of all travellers. we are not experts, but we are experts in the field of aviation. as experienced experts, we know that not all airlines and not all aircraft are equally safe. today, some airlines may still be less vigilant about security, being able to divert to other airports where checks are less strict. this shows the importance of a community policy on airport security. even if the accident rate remains the same as in 1996, the commission estimates that, with the expected growth in air traffic, a serious accident would occur every week in 2015. the risk of accidents varies greatly between regions and continents. did you know that the airlines you fly in africa, asia, south and central america are much more likely to have accidents - at least twice the world average? western europe and australia have the lowest rates, while eastern european countries have the highest rates of accidents, 50 times higher than in western europe! this directive is therefore absolutely necessary to harmonize internationally accepted safety controls. unfortunately, we do not yet have a community policy. this is still the task of the member states. unfortunately, the member states and the airlines are also afraid to give the public the information we think is necessary.
i had the opportunity to be in romania last week, where i attended the preparations for the referendum. i would like to say that the referendum has become a slap in the face to the application of democratic rights. i have never seen so much fraud in the preparation of a referendum as in romania. let me give you some examples. the people who took part in the referendum were promised that their energy bills would be paid by the government; mayors are being offered trips to china if they support the referendum. two hours before the polls closed, turnout was only 45 percent. in addition, and perhaps even worse, there was a restriction on freedom of expression. it is not necessary to agree with the views of certain parties, but if i find that the position of a major opposition party, namely the prm, has simply been removed from the website by the public authorities, then there is a need for an investigation by the european parliament to establish what irregularities occurred in the organisation of this referendum. it would be good, mr president, if we followed that up, since romania is a candidate country.

i am very pleased that the action we have taken with some members of our group is bearing fruit. i am also very pleased with the statement made by the commissioner here, which has given us hope. however, i fear that the departure of the two vessels to the uk is part of a wider strategy to set up a larger waste carousel to countries such as india and bangladesh. if these ships are allowed to cross, the dam will be closed and a precedent will be set. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the waste is not sent to countries which have no strong social or environmental policies. in recent years, various efforts have been made to prevent the export of waste and to achieve a system where waste is dealt with as close to the source as possible. the crossing of the ships is in line with this. that is a very bad signal from the united states, which has already suspended several environmental conventions. this country has the capacity and the knowledge to destroy these ships itself! mr president, ladies and gentlemen, there are probably few subjects on which there is a consensus in this house. i hope that today's debate will be an exception to this. anyone in the european union who is concerned with the quality of education at any level will be pleased to see that the council and parliament have agreed to launch a programme exclusively aimed at improving the quality of higher education in the member states. in the past, only a select few could gain experience at a foreign college or university during their studies. the fact that europe is striving to offer this opportunity to more and more students in the future is a development that we can all - whatever our background - rejoice in. as a representative of flanders, one of the smallest nations in the european union, i often observe that our language, dutch, is under pressure at the university more than once, partly because more and more subjects, not only in flanders but also in the netherlands, are taught exclusively in english. i am therefore particularly pleased that the council and the european parliament, in their common position on the erasmus mundus programme, have paid particular attention to the promotion of the language skills of students from within and outside the european union. by offering students the opportunity to learn at least two languages spoken in the countries where the higher education institutions are located within the erasmus programme, we may be able in the long term to reverse the negative trend we are seeing today, mainly in the large member states. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the debate on the future of the european union. i would therefore like to stress that it is not only students from third countries who should be encouraged to know at least two languages of the european union. it is also urgent that our own european youth should realise that a thorough knowledge of several languages is the best guarantee of learning to appreciate other cultures. this is also the best way of preserving the rich diversity of the european union in the long term. in this way, our universities and colleges, our very higher education, will remain a showcase for the rest of the world, of which we can rightly be proud.
the rapporteur. mr president, it is clear that safety is essential. the safety on the railway at the moment is high in most cases and it absolutely must remain high, at least as high as it is now. i would also like to thank many colleagues for their valuable contribution to the debate on safety and also many people from the railway community who have made a lot of suggestions, made improvements to the texts, pointed out difficulties, and so on. if there is to be a european railway market, then there must of course also be a european safety system. and with this directive, we're going to do that. a european safety objective, a method to achieve that objective, a safety authority in each member state, which uses a common method to issue licences to all those concerned. the council's common position was followed by a series of talks and then by mediation between the commission and council representatives. we have been able to eliminate a whole series of problems, because there were quite a few differences between the common position and our first reading. i have tabled a number of compromise amendments on this and i ask the house to support them, including compromise amendments on the driving licence for train drivers, which we will therefore be solving by means of a new commission proposal, and also on interoperability. i would also like to stress the importance of the agreement reached by the social partners (the trade unions and the european railway companies). i would ask the commission to implement this agreement as quickly as possible, so that we can also show that we attach great importance to the social framework of the reforms we want to undertake. i think that is an important point, commissioner, and we should therefore emphasize it. the second point on which we have reached agreement concerns communication and the crossing of language barriers, something i had stressed quite a lot. if mrs ainardi agrees, we will bring that point to the directive on interoperability and that will be settled. but there are two important points on which we disagree with the council. first, the speed with which that new safety system must be set in motion. the european parliament wants to go faster and i think we should stick to that position. secondly, the pursuit of a european security system - and mr jarzembowski has already pointed this out - means that as few new national security rules as possible are created. that is why article 8 is important: the role of the commission as the guardian of the system. we must not lose sight of our position as a parliament on this point. we have already reached agreement with the council on a number of points, but there is one point which remains and which we must negotiate vigorously. we shall see tomorrow whether the council can also follow up the points on which we reached agreement. i think that an agreement is possible and we should strive for it. we must also - and i would now like to speak about the other reports and the whole - we must continue to keep the four directives as one package. we must enter the consultation procedure with these four directives at the same time. in general, the european parliament, more than the council, is in favour of an open european railway market and of european railway companies. the council has not yet done so, much less the commission and parliament. we must negotiate hard in the conciliation procedure to get the council to go a long way in our direction. the declining market share of the railways is self-explanatory: our belgian railways lost another 6% of the freight market in the first half of 2003. that is a very regrettable development. there is a small increase in passenger traffic, but the situation is still bad for freight. i think inland and coastal shipping, especially in our country, are poised to take over the market share. it is therefore important that the railways defend themselves much better, that they ensure that they are present on the market. the railway must become more efficient, much more vital than it is now. there are a number of countries where that is the case, but there are others where it is not at all the case. the railway companies should not be concerned only with themselves. they have to be concerned with their customers, their users, they have to be concerned with their service, making sure that they are serving their customers in the best possible way so that those customers stay with them or come back to them. i think that's important. i meet almost every week with a company that says, we'd like to do some things with the track, but we can't. it's bad, it's too slow, it's too expensive, it's not reliable. i think that is very regrettable news and we must do something about it. that is the very first purpose of this package of directives. what we are proposing here - and i should like to say this - is not a wild capitalism on the run. that is certainly not the intention of the directives which are now on the table. this is not an antisocial or anti-social package. my request to ratify the agreement between the social partners is, moreover, socially inspired. this package will enable the railways to regain control of the transport market. i am using a military term very deliberately, because unless the railways regain their share of the transport market, we are heading for a very serious social massacre.

european transport route which is useful for an open economy. the iron rhine, in the ruhr area of the german hinterland, opens up the port of antwerp, but also the ports of ghent, zeebrugge and ostend. the iron rhine exists and has proved its usefulness in the past, but has been out of service for several decades because the cost of environmental adaptations, such as tunnelling in certain places, is far too high to make the line profitable quickly. however, this is a false argument behind which the dutch government is hiding in order to block the case permanently. however, flanders now feels the need to reactivate this railway line, given the increasing traffic problems in the exponentially increasing container traffic in flanders, the netherlands and germany. germany, and more particularly the land of rhineland-westphalia, has been supporting flanders for years in this economically necessary demand. unfortunately, however, the netherlands is pursuing an obstructionist policy in this area, motivated by its own plans to provide the port of rotterdam with a modern connection to the ruhr area via the expensive and environmentally damaging betweel line, but with no economic advantage for any european country. for years, this has created a deadlock, which has not benefited anyone and has only caused great economic damage to flemish ports and container traffic. - (it) madam president, it is unnecessary to stress the importance of the vote on this second railway package and, above all, the importance of ensuring that the council adopts this package soon. we all agree that we need to ensure the necessary shifting of demand and congestion in certain modes of transport, in particular road and air transport, in order to avoid congestion and problems due to overuse and the resulting overloads. to some extent, it is a matter of moving from intentions to facts and concrete proposals, ensuring competitive prices, safety, reliability and, above all, the provision of the infrastructure and investments necessary to optimise the various solutions available, starting with railways. although this is necessary, i must admit that i never thought that the opening up of the market and the process of liberalisation would be the only answer, a kind of shortcut to a miracle. it is necessary to make them subject to certain conditions: if we remove the pretexts and the ideological rigidity, we must recognize that this can help to facilitate these processes. under certain conditions, of course: all the aspects of safety, professionalism and social protection which must be provided for in the processes of market opening and liberalisation are certainly indispensable, precisely in order to avoid competition being achieved at the expense of these factors and guarantees. similarly, it is also necessary to expand the supply of universal and public services to prevent competition from again concentrating investment and management interests only in strong markets where demand is high, without taking into account situations where the supply of services is crucial, rather than to encourage and advance development processes, in other words to meet the really vital needs in the freight and passenger transport sectors. in view of these conditions, we must set deadlines for liberalisation, but they must not be too short, because only under these conditions will it be possible to guarantee that private resources will be devoted to infrastructure investment projects. otherwise they could be postponed because of the constant difficulties we are having, as we know, in collecting the public funds needed to complete these processes. we must guarantee the principle of reciprocity in this context, as provided for in the amendments tabled in parliament, because we believe that it will avoid the distortion of the market, which has already occurred in other liberalization processes. i hope that it will be possible to find adequate solutions, taking these principles and choices into account, also in our negotiations with the council.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. when we talk about railways, we are always talking about the relationship between services and liberalization. i regret once again that we have never succeeded in discussing this issue globally in parliament, instead of always carrying this discussion over from one mode of transport to another on a project basis. i think that if we had had this discussion, we would have been able to reduce some of the prejudices that come up in every debate. liberalisation, in my view, means that it can only happen - and road transport is a good example for me - if we also combine it with accompanying regulatory measures. they concern safety, working conditions, access to the profession and, among other things, technical interoperability. it is our task to achieve this, and what we have here today is, in my view, a major step forward in talking not only about railway liberalization but also about the measures which will provide an answer to the questions raised. does liberalization have to mean social dumping? in my eyes, no. does it have to mean job losses? no, not either. and does liberalization mean less security? i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals, but i would like to say that i am not going to go into the details of the commission's proposals. the british are not setting a good example for us, and no one in this house wants to follow that example. what we are proposing here is precisely what has not been done in england, namely a proper preparation for liberalization. and i would like to say to my colleagues from belgium and france that we are already in the process of job cuts. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the lack of a single market. this also means that the sector has not proved its efficiency and accordingly jobs have been progressively eliminated. so to warn that jobs will be lost in the future and to forget that they have already been lost, even though the instrument is in the public domain, does not in my view represent a correct and objective picture of the situation. we must also get to the root of the problem in our own countries and realise that our governments have under-financed the railways and that we have not given them the opportunity to organise tomorrow's intermodal transport in this area. we must also recognize that the personnel costs of these institutions are far too high and that countries and governments must intervene in advance. i do not want to fetishize the data, but i would also like to point out that it would be bad for the railways and for the countries if we did not set the data here in the house, because we are convinced that tomorrow's transport area will be called europe and it must not fail on the basis of individual measures and individual rules in the small countries.
