the commission (es) mr president, i would point out that germany has notified the commission of its intention to introduce a toll system based on the distance travelled by goods vehicles, with an average price of eur 12.6 cents per kilometre. on 6 march, germany notified the commission of its intention to introduce a toll reimbursement system - at least partial reimbursement or compensation - based on excise duties, which could allow compensation up to an average of 15 cents. the commission has to assess whether the notified measure is compatible with the common market and complies with the applicable community legislation. in view of the doubts raised by this measure, the commission decided on 23 july 2003 to initiate a formal investigation procedure in accordance with the procedure laid down in article 88 (2) of the ec treaty in order to allow germany and other interested parties to submit their comments. the text was published in the official journal of the european union on 27 august 2003 and the commission is expected to take a final decision by the end of the year. the commission considers that the tolling system could be discriminatory if certain technical problems were not resolved before its introduction. the commission and the german government have therefore set up a working party to examine the technical problems posed by the introduction of the toll system, and this working party is proving to work well. i would also point out that the commission has asked the german government to ensure that the tolling facility impedes the free movement of traffic in europe as little as possible and that if problems arise once it is in operation, the commission will take appropriate action. but i would like to say that the commission has acted to defend the treaties and that, since the end of august or the beginning of september, we have established channels of cooperation with the german government which are working more than satisfactorily.
- i'm not sure. i have also stressed in the various questions that the commission has acted correctly on this issue. my concern is that the toll has been postponed without a date, and this working party which you have already set up has also helped us to point out that deficiencies have arisen and that this too has led to discrimination between different countries. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. or, if not, will the commission initiate infringement proceedings? i think it is clear that this toll system is not in principle being called into question, but that, among other things, the control devices with which this toll is introduced for germany are a system which, firstly, has technical shortcomings and, secondly, is not made available to all farms. i think that is sufficient to continue this investigation or monitoring.
commission. firstly, i would like to thank mr staes for tabling a question on adding water and protein to chicken meat. this issue was debated during sitting of 1 july 2003, so it is now an opportunity to inform the house about the present state of play. i would first like to say a few words on the background to this issue. as you know, the irish and uk authorities have revealed fraudulent practices designed to artificially increase the weight of poultry-meat by adding water and protein to other ingredients. the process is used to retain a large quantity of water, up to 40% or indeed, even as high as 50%. that is the reason why i have asked my services to do all they can in order to improve this unfortunate situation. in line with the proposal made by the uk authorities, my services have been working on a commission directive in order to highlight, on the label, the presence of added water in such products. a draft has been discussed with experts from member states and stakeholders. it provides a requirement to include 'with added water', and the percentage of water added, in the name of the product itself. however, i remain of the opinion that labelling products to which water has been added is only the second best solution if water has been added only to increase artificially the weight and therefore to mislead the consumer. the practice of adding water to food in order to cheat the consumer is despicable. i have therefore again asked my services to examine whether there is any possibility of banning this practice under current community law, and that work is being undertaken at the moment.
thank you for your answer, commissioner. i'd like to ask a few additional questions. so far, it has been reported that these practices have taken place in ireland and the united kingdom from the netherlands. is there any indication that these practices have been carried out in other member states? in the debate on 1 july you announced that there would be more cooperation between the member states and the commission to tighten up checks on fraudsters. you have answered a number of other questions i wanted to ask you. but you also announced on 1 july that you would be drawing up standards for meat products. as regards the presence of water, you have answered that. but what about the presence of other proteins, "chicken meat" containing beef or pork proteins? what is the situation on that point? can you give any idea of the possibility of banning this practice altogether? i think the last part of your answer is absolutely correct: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the protection of the environment. do you have any idea when and if such a ban could be issued?
commission. the community is pursuing a coherent and comprehensive tobacco control policy through legislation including contents, packaging and labelling of tobacco products. although it has been able to develop robust tobacco legislation, it faces challenges every day, ranging from litigation to the incorrect implementation of provisions of this legislation. the honourable member asks whether cardboard covers into which cigarette packets are put in order to hide the health warning, are against the 'spirit of the directive'. this issue is bound to be considered in the context of implementation of the tobacco products directive. article 5 of this directive requires that the content of tar, nicotine and carbon monoxide yields should be printed on the cigarette packet and each unit packet of cigarettes must carry general and additional health warnings covering at least 30% of the front and 40% of the back surface, aimed at informing the consumer about the risks of tobacco use. one of the main purposes of the tobacco products directive is to alert consumers to the health effects of tobacco use through clear and visible health warnings. in this respect, the directive provides that these texts must in no way be hidden, obscured or interrupted by other written or pictorial matter or by the opening of the packet. if the cigarette packet and the cardboard cover are sold separately, it could be argued that this is not in direct contradiction with the provisions of the directive which concerns the sale of tobacco and not the sale of cardboard. if the cardboard cover is purchased voluntarily by the consumer, this then is an individual decision made by the consumer who has no doubt noticed the texts and the health warnings and, presumably, does not like to see them. so, in this context the purpose of the directive of informing the consumer about the health effects of tobacco use has been met. however, where the cardboard is promoted directly or indirectly by the cigarette manufactures themselves and, for example, is given free of charge in tobacco shops, even where it is proposed or sold separately, this might affect the sale of tobacco which falls within the scope of the directive and jeopardise its useful effect. it could then be seen, as the honourable member suggests, as going against the spirit of the directive. the commission is aware, for instance, that in some cases these cardboard covers are on sale at government-licensed tobacco shops, and brochures advertising them are sent to the tobacco shops by the same distribution companies that bring the tobacco. it is up to the member states to take all necessary measures to ensure the correct implementation of the directive. the commission urges the national authorities in charge of its implementation to be vigilant in that respect. the community has a powerful legal instrument which needs to be implemented correctly. the commission will analyse all practical issues emerging from the implementation of the directive and will report to parliament before the end of 2004.
commission - i agree with the implication of your question. the last part of your question attempts to find out whether the tobacco industry itself is involved in all this or whether it is the retailers. as i have already indicated, there is some evidence to suggest that government sponsored tobacco companies would be involved in this exercise. the extent to which these products are sold or distributed free of charge, together with the sale of tobacco, is a problem which we need to address and solve, especially in the context of the implementation of the provisions which is the responsibility of the member states as such. however, if it were two totally separate and distinct transactions, the purchase of cigarettes on the one hand and the purchase of one of these cases in another store in a different place where the individual is free to do as he pleases with his pack of cigarettes on the other hand, it seems to me that we then reach the point where regulation becomes complicated. but, as i said earlier, the fact remains that the purchaser of the cigarette pack will have been aware, brought to his attention, that this information is on the cigarette pack. the photographs you refer to may also be included if the member state in question chooses to print them on cigarette packets, although this is not compulsory. in this context, the information is communicated to the consumer in the manner provided for by this legislation. the legislation is therefore not defeated at this level. but there are problems at this level. one is to ensure that the law is properly enforced and the other is to ensure that we refrain from any interference with the freedom of the individual to do whatever he wants with his property, even if we do not approve of it.
the rapporteur. president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, i think that the violation of women's rights worldwide is well known. if it exists, it is because it is based largely on a set of values such as tradition, social cohesion or the honor of the male members of a family. female genital mutilation is seen as honoring this tradition. murder or mutilation for reasons of honour is considered a way of preserving social peace, and public stoning and flogging of women is permitted as part of maintaining social order. the international community is fortunately increasingly showing its dissatisfaction with this situation. examples include the reaction to the stoning of amina lawal, the delayed response to the taliban regime and the persistent questions about the death of photojournalist zahra kazemi in iran. at the same time, actions for women's rights are being recognised. the award of the nobel prize to shirin ebadi, the iranian women's and children's rights activist, is also a very important signal, as is the speech by president wade of senegal in this chamber this afternoon. wade specifically referred to his fight against female genital mutilation. the question we must ask ourselves is whether europe is pursuing a structured policy on serious violations of women's rights through the various channels available to the union. at least there is a beginning of a policy. for example, the cotonou agreement with the acp countries explicitly refers to genital mutilation. and also the explicit reference in the budget to the failure to take measures to prevent serious violence against women - and they are listed: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the illegal trade in human beings. but this policy of the european union is too little structured. the european parliament therefore calls for a certain system to be introduced. what might be the elements of such a system? a number of elements are sanctioning. for example, the inclusion in trade and cooperation agreements of a specific clause expressly stating that gross and repeated violations of women's rights may ultimately lead to the suspension of cooperation is a sanctioning element. secondly, these sanctions and suspensions should be implemented when governments systematically fail to take legislative, administrative and judicial measures. but there can also be positive aspects, for example the systematic inclusion in development aid programmes of projects aimed at combating violence against women or the urging and taking into account of the fact that partner countries ratify the convention on the elimination of all forms of discrimination against women, as well as the additional protocol. an example might be: the discussion, or the negotiation, that is going on with iran. we look forward with great interest to the text that will eventually be tabled. we believe that this text should make explicit reference to the fight against violence against women and that women's rights should be explicitly mentioned and that it should also make reference to the ratification of cedaw. another possibility is that the commission provides staff trained to look at agreements with third countries from a gender perspective. local women's organizations in developing countries must also be involved. in the member states of the european union, and also from europe, it is possible to press for legislation against genital mutilation. and when granting refugee status, it must take into account the fact that some women are fleeing violence in their own countries. commissioner, president, ladies and gentlemen, the european union has included in its banner the defence of human rights, including women's rights. more than any other region, it is expected to be the conscience of the world in this respect. i would like to ask the commission whether it is prepared to adopt a much more structural approach to policy on this problem. i got a good answer to some of the questions i asked, but they're all punctual actions. there is actually little structure in the policies that are being pursued at the moment.
i would like to begin by congratulating mrs smet on her courageous report. not everyone in the various states is cheering and rejoicing to receive your report. it may be lost on many of us that in the context of international relations, and particularly in the field of development cooperation, it was not so long ago that talking about human rights was a veritable taboo. we have always been so friendly in the past that negotiations have not even mentioned what people really thought about the situation in certain countries. so what we have now is a radical change. i hope that in future we will have a women's rights clause when negotiating with certain countries. human rights are women's rights, we hear it all the time. the fact that women's rights are a necessary element in development cooperation should be abundantly clear. women in all cultures, everywhere in the world, play a specific and decisive role in the social, economic and cultural construction and progress of society. in my experience, the fight against trafficking in human beings, and especially the fight for the victims, clearly shows the need for a strong women's policy. why is this report necessary and should it not become a mere lip service? i immediately think of the situation of women in several future member states, where women are still traded like a wardrobe. if we look a little further: the european parliament has been very active in this area, and i am sure that the commission will be very active in this area. in that text, in the kanun, in the laws of the dukajin, it says that a woman is nothing more than a bag, made to last for a time. you can compare it to a second-hand item with an expiration date. and then they talk about women's rights in 2003! or you can think of the policy maker in ghana, who told me that women yield more than tomatoes. then why shouldn't we trade in it? perhaps it would be a very good idea to simply send this report to a lot of embassies, consulates, people who work internationally, so that a lot of people's attention is drawn to the content and they start thinking about the fact that a women's rights clause is a human rights clause, and is necessary!
i would also like to congratulate mrs smet, the rapporteur of this report, for having succeeded in producing a report on female genital mutilation, public corporal punishment, stoning, crimes of honour and forced marriages, without ever using the terms 'islam' or 'muslim' on paper. you must forgive my sarcasm, ladies and gentlemen, but i can only regret that it is not considered politically correct to call a cat a cat and to go to the heart of the matter. even though it is a very complex problem: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to combat this problem. the report excels at times, and i can only regret that too, in its use of obscure language. for example, it refers to a cultural heritage that places men in a privileged position, or to traditional views and stereotypes imposed on women. but nowhere does it mention man and horse. one has to ask who is really benefiting from such misplaced fear. the cultural relativism which is so dominant in europe today prevents a correct forecast of the problems and thus rather constitutes an obstacle to any fundamental solution. not every muslim is against women's rights, just as not every muslim is a fundamentalist. but the reality is that the oppression of women has unfortunately become the norm in muslim states and countries where muslims are the majority. the seriousness of the situation may vary from country to country or region to region, but the marginalization of women is mainstream in the muslim world and as such has nothing to do with the existence of fundamentalist so-called minorities. it is not an encouraging thought, i realize, but on the contrary a reason to take the measures mentioned in the report with all the more conviction. that being said, i can agree with the general outline of mrs smet's report. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the sanctions must also be effective when violations of women's rights are detected and the local authorities permit, tolerate or even impose them. the report rightly points out that the european member states must ensure that all forms of violence against women are recognised and punished in their own legal systems. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. the demand for such interventions is growing rapidly and i can predict that there will be further discussions about whether social security should finance the maintenance of such backward practices in the future. it is a pity that the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities has apparently decided to make a broad turn on the issue of headscarves. the ultimate symbol of the oppression of women by islam. one has only to take the subway in cities such as brussels, paris or london to see how much the headscarf has been pulled up compared with a year or ten years ago. the radicalization of islam is not just something that happens in pakistan or egypt, but also in our own big cities. europe must therefore send a strong signal that our standards and values, such as equality between men and women, cannot and will not be discussed. and anyone who doesn't want to accept that is no longer welcome here!
- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, thank you for giving us the opportunity once again to talk about women. at a time when a woman who fights for human rights, and women in particular, has just been named nobel peace prize laureate, at a time of intense reflection on the need for a more coherent development of the world, to find solutions to conflicts, let us take this opportunity once again - albeit at a very late hour - to recall the very specific situation of women. every year thousands, if not millions, of women are victims of all forms of violence. how many silences are hidden behind the statistics, how many aspects are hidden and hidden behind the non-interference clause or the cultural heritage argument? in the european union we affirm that human rights must prevail over any social custom or philosophy. but when we talk about international relations, since that is the very subject of this report, two worldviews often clash. should we be satisfied with a statement and not listen to the demands of these women, whether in their own countries or in their search for protection under the international law which we support? what then is the margin for manoeuvre to say that we condemn such practices and are not being deceived? the instruments set out in this report are proof of the excellent pragmatism of women in general, and of mrs smet in particular, whose work i would like to commend. we must reaffirm that all violence against women is in itself sufficient, sui generis, to recognise the right to asylum and to grant refugee status. we must use the violence of economic sanctions against this discriminatory violence, unless we put women's lives on a priority list that is worth far less than cooperation agreements or statues of sad memory. let us stop paying for words and paper and try to be as pragmatic as women in general can be.
- mr president, commissioner, i too would like to congratulate our rapporteur, mrs ferreira, on behalf of the liberal group, for her excellent work and cooperation throughout the whole of the examination of this dossier, which is a subject, it must be said, of a technical nature, which does not speak much to the citizens and which should nevertheless be of the greatest interest to consumers, in any case to those, who are increasingly chasing sugar, calories and also tooth decay in children, in particular. it is indeed a good thing that we are talking about tomorrow's authorization of the marketing throughout the union of two new sweeteners: sucralose and aspartame-acesulfame salt, two sweeteners which have at least three common characteristics: no safety problems - their safety has been confirmed and reconfirmed, as we have already said - easier applications - faster dissolution, better resistance to higher temperatures - and also a sweetening power far superior to traditional sugar. these substances may therefore be useful in the daily diet, especially that of diabetics and people with obesity. as for cyclamate, a danish study showed that overconsumption can be harmful to the most vulnerable, pregnant women and children under three years of age. it is therefore good that the maximum dose for cyclamate has been reduced. on the three amendments adopted in committee, we must welcome the compromise reached between the three institutions, which allows us to avoid the conciliation procedure, even though we had to add a little water to our wine. the liberal group will not, however, vote, as it did at first reading, on amendment no 4 tabled by the green group, which calls for a re-evaluation of the authorisation for aspartame. we trust the opinions of the european scientific committee, a completely independent body, and the opinions of several national authorities, which confirm the safety of this sweetener. however, there are some reports of problems with aspartame overconsumption, and we want to remain vigilant and our rapporteur has reminded us of how. i am also pleased to note that the common position has provided for the mandatory labelling of aspartame-acesulfame salt, reflecting our concerns, which were expressed by a number of us at first reading. in conclusion, as mrs ferreira has said, i would like to say that this debate has some frustrating aspects. it does not answer the vital, i would say priority, question of the value of these fake sugars. are they really of interest to everyone, are they really safe for health, and i am speaking here in a broad and long-term sense, do they really help to combat obesity or, on the contrary, do they maintain the taste of sugar, whether true or false? all these questions have been only partly resolved, although, as i would remind you in conclusion, the liberal group is completely satisfied with the results obtained on the questions which were on the table here.
the european council is clearly aware of its task, given the multitude of subjects discussed at the summit. the council wants transeuropean networks, but in practice member states are often reluctant to build a few kilometres of railway, which would make it a little easier for the neighbouring country to export its own goods. we are talking about the need to provide development opportunities for people in their own countries, but when it comes to sugar or cotton, let us always let the economic interests of a small group, of our own people, prevail over the opportunities of the many who live in misery, who try to escape it and then drown on our shores! we have been talking for years about the right of the palestinians to have their own state alongside israel. but where is the effective and credible action of the european union? once again, you are forced to acknowledge the need for such action. but where is it? the european union needs more effective structures to enable it to act more effectively without the member states having to put their hands in the wheel. that's why we need the constitution. we have always hoped that europe would become a home for all, not just for citizens and member states, but also for those people who are in a region which has not become a member state for some reason or other of history. but why do you not see europe as a great patchwork of cultures, peoples and regions, where everyone should have opportunities for development? we must take better control of our common destiny and it must not be thwarted by the politics of prestige or the short-sightedness of a few member states! we want a european constitution which is really a new treaty, which gives a new impetus to the future of the peoples of europe and which also gives the regions access to the court of justice when their rights are violated.
the rapporteur. mr president, there have been very long negotiations to make a correction in the text. i shall read that correction and ask my socialist colleagues whether it is the correct interpretation of what they have asked for. if not, i agree to send it back. i am reading for what the text of paragraph 13 should be, because there have indeed been some difficulties, including the omission of parts of the text.
mr president, the world has not become much safer since the collapse of the communist dictatorship in the former eastern bloc. as samuel huntington pointed out, we have evolved from a bipolar to a multipolar world since the end of the cold war, and more than ever before, it is necessary to define friend and foe and therefore the threats we face. as has rightly been said, terrorism, weapons of mass destruction and badly governed states are three major threats to europe. i would also add to that the demographic pressure of an uncontrollable and conquering islam. europe must therefore ensure that it has an efficient and well-developed security and defence system. that will require political will, but also investment. and in that respect we have to note with some embarrassment that the average expenditure per military person in belgium is three times lower than in the other european member states. the belgian government has already announced that it will not increase its defence spending during this parliamentary term, which has hardly begun. it is therefore a certain hypocrisy on the part of the belgian government to want to take the lead in building a defence system at european level while refusing to accept the consequences and obligations that go with it.
the debate on european defence has been accelerating in recent months. the brok report comes at a good time. what was once the exclusive domain of nato or national sovereignty is now increasingly the domain of the european union. at the end of 2000, the nice conference still held back on enhanced defence cooperation, but today there is a growing consensus on this issue. the eu's operations in bosnia, macedonia and east africa have demonstrated that europe has a global political responsibility. and as our colleague mr brok's report also makes clear, a european headquarters of our own is a sine qua non for an independent and efficient european defence. it is of great importance to me that more member states, both present and future, should join the european defence project of belgium, luxembourg, germany and france. only then will such a european capability put an end to the useless duplication of defence efforts among member states. only then can the image of a europe that is economically a giant, but politically a dwarf and militarily a worm be broken.
i suggest. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i have no difficulty in explaining clearly the presidency's view on the subject of relations between the european union and the russian federation, especially since in a few days' time, on 28 october, i shall be in moscow, together with the european troika, to discuss with mr ivanov the prospects for the european union/russian federation summit scheduled for 6 november in rome. i would like to make the point that the presidency believes that the relationship between the union and the russian federation must have a strategic value and that it is therefore in the interest of the european union to develop and deepen this partnership: the european union has a foundation in the partnership and cooperation agreement, which was the subject of one of the first common strategies developed by the european union and updated by the st petersburg declaration. we believe it is in our interest to encourage the reform process initiated by the russian federation and its move towards the european union. this process seems at times to be characterized by contradictory aspects. in the russian federation itself there are probably still forces which, perhaps covertly - let us say - oppose this progressive integration of the federation into european structures, which is a strong and shared objective of president putin. this is why those who wish to foster and promote a stronger and more positive relationship with the european union should be encouraged. the st petersburg declaration marked an important turning point in our relationship with moscow and indicated a path which the presidency, in agreement with the commission and with the support of the member states, intends to follow: we must give substance to this privileged and strategic partnership which we intend to establish with the russian federation. the st petersburg declaration set the stage for the creation of four common spaces. this approach, which we share, will be the basis of the work of the next summit on 6 november. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. commissioner verheugen will hold the final meeting with his russian counterpart, deputy prime minister kristenko, on 28 october. we shall have to reach a definitive agreement on the very concept of a common economic area and on the operational follow-up. we hope - and we are working towards this - that a common commitment can be agreed to pursue a higher level of integration of our economies through greater trade liberalisation and regulatory convergence. in parallel, the european union is engaged in negotiations for russia's accession to the world trade organisation. the negotiations are well advanced, although there are still problems to be solved: i am referring in particular to the prices of energy products and access to the services market. commissioner lamy has recently reported to the council on this subject and has received strong encouragement from the council to continue the negotiations. we are aware that russia's accession to the world trade organisation is an important and essential condition for a genuine common economic area. as regards the common external security area, we are preparing a draft joint declaration, which will set out the criteria, principles and objectives of cooperation in the field of crisis management, and which should include both the use of military instruments and the use of civilian instruments. we hope that this joint declaration will make cooperation in an area of particular importance for the union's external action more visible. on the common internal security area we can see good cooperation, particularly in the fight against organised crime. we are on our way to the signing of the agreement between europol and the russian ministry of the interior, and we hope to be able to record this event at the summit on 6 november. also in the field of internal security, russia had asked us to study a new visa waiver regime for short stays. we are aware that this is a medium- to long-term request, but that russia will have to fulfil certain preconditions. the dialogue with the russian authorities on this subject is, however, under way, and this is a positive aspect; a working group met on 13 october to discuss this issue. we attach importance to the conclusion of the visa negotiations, but also to the negotiations on the readmission agreement: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in the meantime, we hope to make some progress, even if we say 'on a steady basis', without changing the rules. some member states, including italy, have already established bilateral arrangements, others have announced their intention to do so. the presidency will of course work towards a common position which, if possible, incorporates bilateral agreements. finally, the common space for culture and education: we must work much harder here, because so little has been achieved. however, i would like to mention as a positive aspect the recent accession of the russian federation to the so-called 'bologna process', a collaborative mechanism that promotes agreements on qualifications and the harmonisation of university curricula. this is an important development. the prospect of renewing the agreement on cooperation in the field of science and technology and the prospect, encouraged by the italian presidency, of including the russian federation in certain community programmes in the field of culture and education should also be noted. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the information provided by the european parliament. the russian side continues to give an interpretation of the st petersburg declaration according to which the council should meet not only in different formations depending on the different subjects, but also in the 25 + 1 format. on the european side, however, it is considered that the council can function mainly according to the troika formula, albeit a troika open to the participation of other members who may be interested. i hope to explore this further when i visit moscow on 28 october. in our dialogue with moscow we will continue to make our principled positions and our concerns about the continuation of the crisis in chechnya and respect for human rights known. the game being played in chechnya is more important than its territorial dimensions: the eu is committed to the development of a comprehensive and effective policy on the caucasus, and its outcomes, whether positive or negative, will have an impact throughout the caucasus and thus have an impact on the overall security of the continent. we have expressed the european union's support for the broad initiative of constitutional reform launched by president putin, through the launch of a new constitution, approved by a very large majority in a popular referendum. we also supported the adoption of the amnesty law, which is important for the process of reconciliation. in the presidential consultations on 5 october we saw a further step towards the gradual resumption of normal political and administrative functions in chechnya. today we reiterate our support for president putin's institutional approach and also for a political solution involving those ever-wider segments of the chechen population who are not compromised by terrorism and who are therefore entitled to participate in the process of democratic reconstruction. we believe that, in parallel with constitutional reform, they will also improve the current human rights standards in the region and the relations between the chechen people and their administrators. we will also confirm our interest in and encouragement to the russian authorities to release promptly and in good condition the médecins sans frontières representative, arjan erkel, who was abducted in the caucasus in august 2002. the summit will also stress the urgency of increased efforts by all, including the european union and russia, to help resolve the conflicts in the former soviet union: i am referring to transnistria and the south caucasus, in particular nagorno-karabakh and south ossetia. with the enlargement of the union and the rapprochement of its borders, the union is much more sensitive than before to this issue, which directly affects the security of the continent. at the same time we recognize the great responsibility moscow has to face and the great contribution it can make to the search for solutions satisfactory to all parties. we shall spare no effort and no encouragement in this direction.
commission. mr president, i am extremely grateful for the minister's comprehensive and very clear explanation on the prospects for the summit with the russian federation. it is of course long-standing good practice for parliament to review our relations with russia on the occasion of forthcoming summits. parliament may recall that it was briefed at the may part-session before the st petersburg summit by my colleague and friend, commissioner verheugen, and we are now in the phase of implementing its conclusions. we have five major expectations from the rome summit, which were alluded to by the presidency. first of all, we want to find a common understanding with russia on the approach to creating the common spaces, stressing the strategic nature of relations between europe and russia. we should agree on how to do this, for example, by drawing up a joint document, which should contain specific, achievable objectives for the medium term, building on the partnership and cooperation agreement and taking into account the wider europe initiative. secondly, we want to intensify discussions on the impact of enlargement. it is in russia’s interest that the partnership and cooperation agreement should be extended smoothly to the acceding member states, without any discrimination. this is a formality, and one on which we want to tie up loose ends by 1 may 2004. if we cannot achieve this, we will need to agree to apply the partnership and cooperation agreement on a provisional basis, as in 1997. thirdly, we will take note of reports on the common european economic area and the energy dialogue, to both of which the presidency referred in some detail. i spent 18 months myself working with deputy prime minister khristenko on the european economic area. we were at what one could call the conceptual stage. i hope that will bear fruit by the time of the summit. fourthly, i hope we can reach agreement with russia on ways to address common challenges. this means tackling problems primarily in the fields of justice and home affairs, and the environment. we will, for example, request russia once again to ratify the kyoto protocol as soon as possible, and ask russia to intensify cooperation on nuclear and maritime safety, on terrorism and on the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction. fifthly, we are ready to agree a joint declaration on cooperation on political and security matters, including crisis management. in particular, the european union expects positive signals from russia on its readiness to cooperate on the transdniestrian region of moldova. we are also ready to look at ways to step up cooperation on civil protection, in order to make best use of existing structures. achieving these results – and perhaps i uncharacteristically understate the point – is not without obstacles. the presidency has referred to two examples. one in relation to the permanent partnership council and the other in relation to visa requirements and the negotiation of a readmission agreement. in making all these points, the european union will recall the importance of the common values which underpin european union/russia relations, and which are crucial with regard to chechnya. the commission has followed recent developments in chechnya. as parliament will be aware, we support a political solution to the conflict and we support all genuine efforts to bring about a lasting peace. we welcomed the constitutional referendum in march, which we hoped would lead to a peaceful, political settlement to the conflict by providing a platform for genuine dialogue, restoration of the rule of law and the protection of human rights. it was in our view essential that the population of chechnya should recognise the legitimacy of the presidential elections held on 5 october. but the conduct of the elections has raised many questions regarding, amongst others, due process and access to the media. also, there are worrying reports of continuing human rights violations. let us be clear. we condemn terrorism in all its forms and we recognise the territorial integrity of russia. but we also insist on the respect of human rights and the prosecution of those who ignore such rights. as the biggest provider of humanitarian aid to the region, the commission is asking russia once again to remove all obstacles to the provision of such aid and we are asking for assurances that internally displaced persons will return to chechnya on a strictly voluntary basis. i hope that in putting these points to russian officials once again we will have a better response than we have had on the many previous occasions when we have raised them. in july, parliament requested the commission to consider how to support reconciliation and rehabilitation. this we are doing, but the most important precondition for assistance – namely security – is simply not met at this time. in fact, the security situation in chechnya has, if anything, deteriorated since last march, and it would be difficult even to carry out a needs assessment, let alone to start work. we are fully aware of the conclusions of the september meeting of the eu-russia parliamentary cooperation committee and we look forward to a new resolution on eu-russia relations following the oostlander report of december 2000. speaking for myself, i hope that by the time i give up my present responsibilities my speaking notes for meetings with my russian interlocutors do not cover nearly all the same points that they did four years ago.
mr. president, that's a little earlier than expected, thank you. i would like to express my thanks, but also my disappointment, to the italian presidency. i would like to thank the foreign minister for mentioning arjan erkel's name spontaneously. and has made it clear that he will urge the russians to take additional steps to secure the release of arjan erkel, the humanitarian aid worker who has been missing for over a year. this is a message of hope, mr foreign minister, the family is happy about it, the organisation doctors without borders is happy about it and i hope that you will therefore expressly refer to this in your talks with mr ivanov on 28 october. but i must also express some disappointment, mr prime minister. this is a statement by the council and the commission. i have listened very carefully to mr patton, who has said very right things about the situation in chechnya, about the elections in chechnya. mr foreign minister, you have only said - and i have noted it - that you will express your concern about the human rights situation in chechnya. mr. secretary, there are far worse things. that election - and mr patton has said it very explicitly - was not a real election, it was a masquerade, it was a farce. the elections were not held in accordance with international standards and the hopes that we had with our ad hoc delegation when we came back, when we saw that a political process was indeed being launched, have now been dashed! i suggest. the council is concerned about the deterioration of the food situation in zimbabwe and opposes any attempt to make the provision of food aid conditional on political criteria. the european union's food aid policy and its management are governed by a 1996 council regulation under which the commission adopts all the provisions necessary for the proper implementation of food aid programmes and actions. to this end, the member states and the commission shall provide each other with the necessary assistance and any useful information. in its common position of 18 february 2002 on zimbabwe, the council expressed grave concern at the situation in that country, in particular the escalation of violence, and imposed sanctions on the country. this common position has been extended until 20 february 2004. according to the eu guidelines on food distribution in zimbabwe, agreed by the commission and the heads of mission in harare in 2002, food management and distribution will be based on criteria of need and not political bias. the impartial delivery of aid will be a joint effort in consultation with the main stakeholders. the selection of beneficiaries will be carried out in a participatory and transparent manner. each distribution programme shall have an autonomous monitoring system to manage selection, distribution, monitoring and possible complaints. the european union expressed grave concern at the publication by the government of zimbabwe of the new policy on the operations of non-governmental organisations providing humanitarian and development assistance. in late september 2003, the government of zimbabwe signed memoranda of understanding with euronet and wfp on the principles of food distribution and made some efforts to clarify the means by which it intends to apply the new policy to ngo operations. following these developments, member states agreed that the heads of mission should launch a widespread initiative in all major capitals, with the exception of harare, to raise the concerns of the european union's regional partners regarding issues related to the delivery of food and humanitarian aid in zimbabwe. the presidency is currently preparing the mandate for this initiative. the union will continue to follow closely the developments in the food crisis, in the hope that the government of zimbabwe will effectively respect the humanitarian principles of the recently signed memoranda of understanding.

the rapporteur. - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i shall not repeat the excellent introduction by my colleague mr langen, which gives us an adequate framework for our report. a few years ago, in lisbon, the european union set itself the clear objective of becoming the most competitive and dynamic knowledge-based economy in the world, capable of sustainable economic growth, coupled with a quantitative and qualitative improvement in employment and greater social cohesion. i am convinced that this objective is primarily aimed at industry, and in particular manufacturing, because the industrial base is the backbone, the real economic engine of growth in the european union. the commission communication, which is the source of this report, is therefore of crucial importance. let's welcome the event. however, in recent years, it seemed to us that the manufacturing industry had been somewhat forgotten, with all the attention being paid to the service sector or the new information and communication technologies sector. in addition, industrial policy as such had not been discussed for several years. the communication finally raises the question of what the european public authorities can do to help our industry. the commission believes that industrial policy should have only one objective: competitiveness. it makes sense. in fact, in an increasingly open world economy, european industry can only have a future if it is able to integrate successfully into this global economy. we still need to agree on what competitiveness is. let us remain true to the spirit of lisbon and gothenburg. the balanced approach of three pillars - economic, environmental and social - must be our guiding principle in our analysis of competitiveness. the european competitiveness must be based on research, skills, training of the workforce, productivity, respect for the environment and, last but not least, the quality of social relations. on the other hand, any attempt to achieve competitiveness by continuously reducing costs, particularly wage costs, and/or by lax environmental policies would be completely illusory. as for the means of achieving this objective, we know that industrial policy is the result of the interaction of policies in multiple areas: research, training, competition policy, the internal market, taxation, employment and social affairs, and many other areas. while the european union's action in many of these areas is considerable, it is essential to see how all these different instruments can be best coordinated towards a genuine industrial strategy for the european union. the commission's communication asks the right questions but does not provide sufficient concrete answers. communication opens up a real debate, which we shall see tonight. the council has already spoken. parliament will do so through this report. the aim is, of course, to make this exercise useful, as my colleague langen said, by ensuring that it is followed up, which must be translated into concrete measures. we therefore ask the commission to put the ideas expressed in this communication into practice in an action plan, which will then be submitted to the council and parliament for adoption. the action plan is based on the following priorities: first, the incorporation of industrial policy objectives into all the community's common policies, in particular competition policy and the control of state aid. secondly, the harmonisation of national tax systems, which have a negative impact on the single market, must be one of the objectives. thirdly, the plan must also include a considerable effort to simplify the administrative process for setting up new businesses. fourthly, the question of research and its optimal profitability for industry and innovation must be a central issue. the question of the 3% to be devoted to research and the european patent are obviously far from negligible. fifthly, the questions of financing of enterprises, in particular smes and venture capital, cannot be left out either. sixthly, it is a question of integrating the social dialogue dimension into the implementation of this industrial policy: a good industrial policy can only be made in consultation with employers and workers' representatives and certainly not against them or without them. finally, education, training and skills are also crucial elements: human resources or human capital are proving increasingly important for industrial competitiveness. this is why i propose that the european union launch an ambitious initiative, such as setting a target figure for expenditure on lifelong learning. the role of major european industrial projects, involving private operators and public efforts, such as galileo, is also worth highlighting in key sectors such as alternative or renewable energy, nanotechnologies, space and steel. the technology platforms initiative seems to me to be a step in the right direction. in addition, we are all aware of the need for a good transport, energy and communication network to take full advantage of the potential of the great european single market. as the union is enlarging to include less well-served regions, there is a need to consider the need to significantly increase the resources available to finance the major works needed. i think that the european council last week also confirmed this view. a good european industrial policy must not only seize the opportunities opened up by the new sectors of the future, but must also be able to anticipate changes in traditional sectors in order to manage restructuring in the best possible way, both at social and territorial levels and at economic level. the commission has drawn up a sectoral analysis. this is a welcome and notable development compared to the purely horizontal approach. it would be desirable, however, not to stop at the sectoral analysis but to use it as the basis for sectoral action. the subject of industrial policy is therefore at the heart of our discussions. we have only to read the conclusions of the last european council. i hope, as does my colleague, that we will move beyond the stage of enchanting declarations and that this will lead to determined and fruitful action.

the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. the accelerating pace of technological progress and the increasing pressure of competition on global markets are forcing car manufacturers to adapt their production methods and their business structures to these changing conditions. in this context, it is important that firms learn to identify and implement early on the measures and investments that will ensure the continuity of their economic activity. however, restructuring is often synonymous with job loss, often with severe consequences for the social fabric of a region, especially in those with little potential for job creation. this is particularly true in the automotive industry, where changes in production processes and the opening up of new markets have a significant impact on employment. the commission is well aware of the difficulty of this balance and of the need for a minimum of security for citizens and workers in these difficult times. we believe that the key to successfully adapting to change is dialogue at all levels based on comprehensive and transparent information. the commission has always acted in accordance with the following principles, which it has repeatedly stressed: first, decisions on restructuring are and remain the prerogative and responsibility of management. this means that when planning measures that may have serious social consequences, companies should actively seek the most acceptable and least harmful solution possible. secondly, when redundancies are unavoidable, everything must be done to strengthen the employability of those affected. this requires anticipation, timely preparation and appropriate control of these processes. thirdly, there is no justification for not providing timely, effective and comprehensive information and consultation to the workers' representatives before such measures are introduced or publicly announced. in any case, and especially in the case of restructuring, the participation of employees is not only a fundamental right but a necessary precondition for the social acceptance of such a measure. we have set minimum standards at european level which all member states and all companies must respect in the event of massive job cuts or other measures which may affect workers. the directives on mass redundancies and transfers of undertakings and the directive on european works councils form our legal framework for protecting workers' rights and ensuring fair standards of information and consultation at national and supranational level. we have also recently adopted the directive on information and consultation. these measures can help workers in europe to successfully meet the challenges of change by providing opportunities for early development of their employability and adaptability. the development of effective information and consultation procedures will also be one of the main elements to be discussed by the european social partners when the commission starts the revision of the european works council directive. more importantly, the commission is very hopeful that the european social partners will find a way to develop best practice for restructuring across the eu, as they decided to do in their positive response to the consultation on this issue last year. these best practices will further advance the information and consultation of workers. these include anticipating market trends or technological developments, investing in people on a long-term basis, promoting employability through training and retraining, career guidance, alternatives to closure and redundancies, and integrating restructured workers within the company wherever possible. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. a better understanding of the future prospects of the automotive industry should benefit all stakeholders. the commission is currently examining developments in this sector in order to identify the elements necessary for the success of restructuring and the lasting competitiveness of the union industry. it plans to make the results of its analysis available in about a year. the immediate concern today is the recent restructuring and i feel it necessary to make it clear first of all that i share with you the deep regret and concern i feel about the possible social impact of the decisions on closures and job cuts on those affected. i hope that every effort will be made to avoid or at least to mitigate these consequences, and that is through dialogue between the parties concerned.
the tragedy of the drastic reduction in production at ford genk has exposed a number of pain points in the european car industry. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a fire is a good example of the need to ensure that the environment is protected. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the sector. angles of incidence are: first, further development of a concept for a sustainable car. whoever is the first to market with it and the most advanced in it will sell the best. if china and india switch to general car traffic, the environmental burden will become unbearable. secondly, to support and frame new technological developments. europe must compete on quality and value added rather than on cost control. because we can't do the latter! to do so, it will have to respond to the ever-growing share of electronics in the added value of a car. thirdly, the social framework for large-scale restructuring. the next structural funds programme is to be adopted in 2006. i hope that the commission will devote more resources to accompanying major industrial transformations when it reviews the european structural funds, particularly with a view to limburg. secondly, as you have already mentioned, there is the question of the renault directive. she absolutely needs to be evaluated. i am told from the field that the procedure adopted would lead to a mechanical application. the employers are so afraid of making a procedural error that consultation is actually prevented in advance. so we absolutely have to evaluate that directive. these are just a few initiatives, there are many more around which europe could work. finally, it is not all gloom and doom in the automotive sector in belgium: volvo, opel and volkswagen continue to invest and ford decided to develop the new mondeo further in genk. the de-localization of the european car industry, and therefore also of the belgian one, is not to be feared in principle. these are high-tech products with an increasingly important knowledge component, the final production of which will be closer to the market. i sincerely hope, commissioner, that a sectoral policy for the future can be developed.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. the mass redundancies announced at ford in genk are not the first and will probably not be the last of their kind. i think they simply reflect the current corporate philosophy of global companies. the decline in profits due to reduced demand is almost exclusively offset by mass redundancies. this is a purely business-oriented and, moreover, very unimaginative approach to which policy must therefore also counter economic components. their job must be to ensure that demand rises. if i look at the current policy, i can see that the cuts made in all sectors of society are not boosting demand, but are, on the contrary, stifling it. the fact that there may be other business strategies than mass redundancies to safeguard employment strategies is particularly evident in the automotive industry. this can be seen in the factory contracts at vw or, more recently, at opel, the reduction of working hours to 30 hours and thus the redistribution of work to more people. but it is clear that, if current media reports are to be believed, other politically motivated reasons are also at play in genk. at least it was stated that ford is in particular carrying out mass layoffs at sites in the countries - adopted by the commission at the end of june. it is based on an overall evaluation of the first years of implementation, reported annually in progress indicators, and a review of the main challenges and drivers of change. the review also sets out how the commission intends to pursue further the eu's overall objectives of creating more and better jobs and greater social cohesion within the framework of the lisbon strategy. the commission has announced a comprehensive package of initiatives and actions involving all relevant actors. on your two questions: the commission shall use its right of initiative to propose the specific measures required and announced in the mid-term review. this includes new draft legislation, such as the proposal for a directive on temporary agency workers, or the simplification of legislation, such as in the field of safety and health at work. the commission also exercises its right to consult the social partners and to identify with them those problems which still need to be investigated. the question of the transferability of occupational pension rights is one example. another is the follow-up to the revision of the working time directive. the european employment strategy is the subject of this report, and in particular the proposals for guidelines and recommendations for employment policy. at the same time, the commission also promotes cooperation between member states in modernising social protection and encouraging social inclusion. as regards the second question, i must emphasize that the mid-term review has two priorities for structuring the work for the next few years. the first concerns the further development of the agenda for an enlarged european union. the second priority is to monitor and enforce the proper implementation and application of the acquis communautaire in the employment and social sectors. in order to achieve this objective, particular attention will have to be paid to the involvement of the relevant networks of national representatives, who will also provide appropriate support in the implementation and application of european law, as well as to the other monitoring instruments still available. the commission will not hesitate to take appropriate enforcement measures or, where appropriate, legal action. but this does not only concern the new member states which will join the european union next year, it concerns all member states.
president, commissioner, thank you for your answer. if i look at the social policy agenda and see where we are now - we are mid-term - we are one year before the elections and we are in favour of enlargement. i think we have to take that into account. if we have to choose our priorities, i believe that enlargement should be the top priority. there are a lot of social guidelines. we are encouraging the development of social dialogue. we are encouraging the establishment of administrations, services and institutions capable of providing social security in those countries. we all know that despite the fact that these countries are joining, the implementation of many of these matters is very difficult. if the implementation is not going well, if the social dialogue is not taking place, if there are not enough institutions to apply all of this, then it will cause a delay for everyone. we're in the same boat. of course, this will cause a delay. we cannot start brewing a new set of directives when the countries that will join next year - a global union of 25 countries - cannot follow part of that union. it is of the utmost importance that social policy develops in the enlargement countries. it's developing way too slowly and way too little. i think that is the first priority, because it will prevent the growth of social policy at european level, whether we like it or not. a second priority is employment. i think the commission is making a good choice, also in its priorities. i'm fine with that. the things that have been proposed in lisbon, namely an employment rate of 70% and so on, we all see that this is proceeding very slowly. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we are therefore further away from the lisbon objectives than we are at present. the whole of employment policy must be encouraged and supported. then there is a third point: the commission has also proposed a number of amendments to the directive. i think we can do that, for example, by revising the renault directive, by revising the directive on works councils, even by revising the directive on safety and health at work. we need to update a large number of directives. that is a third priority for me, which we could also introduce. if we are to make new directives, we really need to make a choice and set priorities. we cannot make 40 new directives, not even 10. i think we should limit that and i think one of the priorities of new directives should be to do something for the disabled. we have had a long debate on this. this group in society continues to have difficulty in accessing the labour market. i think we should arrange some things especially for them.
- madam commissioner, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we can only be pleased today to see that two headlines on the front page of the mainstream press are devoted to the positions that the commission and the european parliament want to take to ensure gender equality, both in access to goods and services and in representation in parliament. finally we're talking about it! we can only congratulate our colleague, lone dybkjær, for stressing the importance that changing attitudes towards the equal integration of women in political life can have on the whole social contract and the political contract we have with our fellow citizens. it is recognised that positive action is an essential element in achieving balanced representation between women and men. it is recognised that including both sexes in policy-making improves policy making and makes it more relevant to a diverse population. the obvious under-representation of women in the decision-making process raises the problem of the legitimacy of political structures, since the functioning of a democratic system is in the image of the society it advocates or defends. equality is a european value that member states must respect. we cannot tolerate this inconsistency on the eve of enlargement, when we want to maintain or even improve the representation of women in our parliament, because this parliament must be the spearhead of this new orientation and at the same time the crucible for new behaviour and new strategies. for this to be possible, and not just desirable, we must ensure that the 2004 campaign affirms and realises the principle of effective parity democracy, because the slightest decline in our representation would be a signal that could be misinterpreted on the eve of enlargement.
i would like to point out the greatest power disparity that exists: the commission has already made a number of proposals to improve the situation of women in the labour market. even in this parliament, where we are one third women, that is still far from being achieved and the best performing country, sweden, has gone from 40% to 45% in recent years, still not parity, although we all envy sweden. in belgium we have gone from 11% to 23%. the new countries can look at the examples here, because they have not done so without effort. if we leave it to the good will of, let us say, men, nothing will change. if we leave it to a change of mentality, we can wait until our great-granddaughters become members of parliament. we must make our own efforts, and so must the new countries. none of these countries are getting the 30% we are used to here. only three countries have more than 20% of women. they have only sent 14% of women to this parliament. if there are 100 more members, then - in the worst case, if the same proportions continue - there will be 86 men and 14 women. this is a general setback for women in the european parliament. it is not the case that the acceding countries are exactly helping to make our parliament look like a man again. we therefore want to give firm support from the european parliament to all women and to all governments who want to change things for the better, because without voluntary action it will not be possible. quotas are often criticised as if they were unjust, as if we should leave everything to the spontaneous evolution of democracy. ladies and gentlemen, we are legislators and a legislator knows that we must not leave everything to spontaneous evolution, that action is needed. in the countries where the best results have been achieved, it has been the governments that have adjusted their legislation. i will give you the example of belgium, where the european list cannot have three candidates of the same sex in the top positions. we also want to see an equal number of candidates and i hope that the good recommendations made in this report will be adopted by the governments. i expect the commission to take an initiative, i expect the council to take initiatives, so that equality in rights and in deeds will certainly not be reduced but improved in this parliament after accession.

in writing. - (el) for several years now, the eu has been systematically promoting the entry of large companies into a series of publicly owned and controlled sectors (energy, telecommunications, railways, postal services, etc.). in 2002, a regulatory framework was established for telecommunications, requiring full liberalisation, accompanied by uniform regulation, in order to avoid excessive contrast and to allow new operators to compete more easily with the former dominant public companies, which have become strong monopoly firms and ensure that telecommunications monopolies penetrate the markets of the new member states effectively. the community decisions have not yet been implemented in seven member states, including greece, against which the commission has initiated legal proceedings. this delay illustrates the eu's inability to impose unequivocal solutions between conflicting interests and reactions to the clearly negative results of liberalisation. the report by the committee on industry, foreign trade, research and energy regrets the delays and threatens the countries concerned with proceedings if they do not comply. it even calls for a ban on price reductions by 'historic operators'. not only do the meps of the greek communist party have no reason to complain, but we also condemn once again the selling of public assets and advance our usual position that only public services should be present in crucial sectors of the economy such as telecommunications.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in flanders this week thousands of flemish port workers' families are anxiously awaiting the outcome of the vote on the liberalization of port services on thursday. in flanders this week thousands of people are anxiously waiting to see if they will have a job in a few months' time. that, and nothing else, is the ultimate aim of the report we are discussing again today. for in this case, liberalisation means nothing but unemployment and social misery for thousands of flemish dockworkers. by blindly believing in its own ideology and by ignoring any serious consultation with the parties concerned, the european commission is proving once again that it does not care about the principle of subsidiarity which the prophets of the 'europe of milk and honey' have been teaching us since maastricht. the european commission is in fact intervening in this dossier in the context of traditions which have for decades made flemish ports famous throughout the world. the vote on this report on thursday threatens to destroy decades of social peace in the flemish ports in the name of an ultra-liberal ideology, which is also threatening to create the opposite of what it claims to be seeking. the liberalization of energy and rail transport in particular has shown, in flanders and elsewhere, but also in great britain, that it can also have adverse effects on competition, quality of work and the provision of services. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's ports are not affected by the liberalization of the market. however, it is not too late. i hope that all members of the european parliament, regardless of their political group or party, will be able to see on thursday the precarious situation of dock workers in antwerp, zeebrugge and ghent, as well as in the other european ports. i hope therefore that this report will be voted down en masse.
