mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, our ports are of enormous importance to our prosperity and are the backbone of world trade. unfair competition between ports should therefore be avoided and we can imagine a directive on this, but there is no such thing yet. the fight must not lead to insecurity, social dumping and environmental pollution in ports. the self-treatment proposed here, even in its reduced form, is a serious breach of this. the european parliament therefore wanted to make self-handling subject to a genuine compulsory licence and to exclude pilot services from competition. it is therefore a great pity that the compromise reached in parliament was not followed up by the council and the commission. despite two and a half years of debate, the camps are directly across from each other. we are facing a break in the social tradition of our ports. after the ships under the cheaper flag, are the ports of convenience coming along? this fear is not confined to dock workers, but also to all those concerned about the social peace in our ports. it is being jeopardized by your directive, ma'am. a drive to liberalize, which in our ports now brings more risks than benefits. we shall therefore vote against it with conviction.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i'm rather surprised. commissioner, you have argued that this directive will have a lasting impact on transport policy. i'd like to remind you what sustainability means. gothenburg has defined sustainability as an equilateral triangle of economics, ecology and social. i simply ask you: how have you promoted sustainability when you are putting port safety at risk by this directive? this can lead to greater environmental damage and does not lead to an improvement in environmental protection. how have you strengthened the social dimension by allowing unlicensed self-assembly, which means that the personnel on board the ship, who have been sailing with the ship all along, are now also required to carry out the loading and unloading work? it has nothing to do with reducing working hours or qualifications. the argument of mr hatzidakis is ridiculous! he says new jobs will be created. i would ask you to consider that if the same activities are carried out by those who already have other activities, then it will reduce jobs but not create new jobs. this is also contrary to the lisbon agenda, if we want to reach full employment by 2012 but always move in the opposite direction. i have to be honest with you . i would have expected you as commissioner to have taken these aspects into account properly. that was completely unilateral; that is to say, you said you wanted to move forward with liberalization. if that's your goal, just say so, and it's all right. it is perfectly clear that we will never arrive at a denominator, because liberalization for you means exclusively reducing costs in the economic field. that may be your claim, but it is not mine, and it is not the claim of our group. to the dockers and pilots: in the course of the debate we have had - and you know this, mr jarzembowski - you have met some of the demands and not all. finally, after the conciliation procedure, you have none of the parties involved on your side, none at all! we had the dockers here yesterday once again, 21,000 signatures, which is an extraordinary number in this profession, and that is not taken into account either! in other words, instead of promoting safety, instead of promoting social standards and thus also putting competition on a uniform basis, i believe that this directive is doing just the opposite. that's why you can only reject it!
we want more transport by sea, but not in an unsafe way. this directive is indeed, as many previous speakers have said, a bad thing for dock workers because it does not enforce minimum social standards throughout the union. actually, it's a bad example for the world. we europeans are always trying to outdo others with our social standards. well, if this passes, we're going to be fueling negative globalization and social breakdown. those who think that the text before us is a brake on a downward spiral are mistaken. it will soon become clear that the competition between ports is ruthless. without strict conditions, self-dealing is the death knell for the average dockworker. this text is also bad for the lords. on the contrary, we want more compulsory remuneration. the risks are increasing due to the increased congestion of shipping routes, but also to the lack of training of maritime personnel. the tricolor off the flemish coast has been a chilling example. more than 60 near misses. so we need more qualified pilots to sail along. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full.
i would like to express my full support for those members who have already said in the debate that one of the main reasons why this directive, this compromise, is unacceptable to us is the whole self-regulation. the fact that the compromise leaves the member states free to introduce or not to introduce authorizations for self-handling, this form of subsidiarity, madam commissioner, makes the whole authorization system an empty box. that's obvious to everyone! if a member state is not obliged to introduce a licensing system, it will inevitably create unfair competition, which will be at the expense of working conditions, the social protection of port workers and the safety of ports. the shipowners will very soon be visiting those ports without a permit system. and i am not talking about the italians, because they have a licensing system, i am not talking about the spanish, because they have a licensing system, but ports that do not take too much care with occupational safety and health. i think, commissioner, that if port services are to be opened up to the functioning of the internal market, social and ecological standards must be put in place, which apply to all countries without exception. secondly, the fact that this directive leaves member states free to market pilot services. that's way out of my league. pilot services have an essential safety function. this compromise is organizing unfair competition through social dumping and unsafe working conditions in our ports. it is also a slap in the face to all those dock workers who have protested against this directive on several occasions and who have handed over over this morning to our own president of the european parliament over 20,000 signatures to make it clear that they are very unhappy with this directive. i therefore call on all those who have doubts - dutch, italian, perhaps - to reject this directive, this bad compromise, and to work towards a good regulation, which is good for the ports, but above all good for the port workers. commissioner, president, ladies and gentlemen, the belgian system of port services is functioning to everyone's satisfaction. neither the port authorities, nor the employers, nor the port workers are asking for this system to be changed. it was established by means of social consultation and is, if necessary, adapted by social consultation to the situation of the various ports in belgium. this social consultation provides workers with a guarantee that their requirements in the field of employment, social services, safety and quality are also included in a social agreement. the result of this system, commissioner, is that the port of antwerp, for example, is number one in conventional cargo and this is the most labour-intensive cargo activity. this is the position antwerp has acquired, despite the fact that the work in the port may be a little more expensive than in other ports, but on the other hand the work in the port is very productive, very safe and of excellent quality. the shipowners are not interested in a different system. how do the belgian port authorities, the port workers and the belgian politicians perceive the intervention of europe? first, it does not improve their lot. - no, it's the opposite. they are looking in the directive for ways of preserving the current system as far as possible through belgian legislation. secondly, it is being negotiated far from their own territory. they are used to acting through social consultation. thirdly, the directive is being drawn up in the light of the weakest ports, where there is little organized and high-quality port work. the correct approach, commissioner, would have been to make european port activities subject to a licensing obligation, which would regulate the most important elements in the fields of social protection, safety, training, work organization and the environment. such a compulsory system would not only have allowed self-handling but would have also guaranteed stricter working methods in all european ports. the ports which already have good regulations and a safe system are actually being punished in favour of the much more lax attitude of other ports. it should have been the other way around. for all these reasons, i shall vote against the directive, and i do so out of conviction. i am a convinced european, but in this case european interference is going in the wrong direction. in writing. - the transfer of waste is fundamental in the struggle we developed countries are waging to maintain economic development and protect the environment. i therefore support the line taken by the blokland report. i am, however, opposed to the obstacles to the recovery of waste. this valuation - supported by the court of justice's judgment in case c-228/00 - will penalise the cement works in my country. 36% of the alternative energy used for cement production comes from waste, mainly from neighbouring countries. if this transfer is jeopardized, there will be a double emission of pollutants in belgian cement works, namely those from waste and those from fuel used in cement works. this double emission is costly and harmful to the environment. in addition, cement factories would be exposed to job losses in areas already severely affected (objective 1). that is why i am against the amendments which oppose the valorisation. to use the precautionary principle when you know the answers is an admission of powerlessness to control nuisance. this is the case for intermediate waste sorting centres, which are prohibited because they cannot monitor the exact destination and traceability of the waste in question.
in writing. - i welcome the fact that the european parliament has today, against the opinion of the rapporteur, mr liese, opened the door to funding research on embryonic stem cells. with this vote, the european parliament is sending a real message of hope to all patients (diabetics, parkinson's, alzheimer's, chronic heart failure and other diseases). it also enables researchers to find cures for incurable diseases, perhaps in the future. i voted for three reasons. firstly, it is fully consistent with the position adopted by the strasbourg plenary on 15 may 2002 on the sixth framework programme for research and development. the creation of embryos from donated gametes for the purpose of obtaining stem cells is therefore prohibited. deuzio, the five member states that oppose any funding for embryonic stem cell research will not be able to stop europe from moving forward, as this is in the interests of its citizens waiting for treatment. thirdly, the opponents of this form of regenerative medicine are confronted with their contradictions: so many people who consider the embryo, from the first day, to be a living being in its own right and who, however, are in favour of the destruction of surplus embryos grown for in vitro fertilisation. i can only applaud the development of a neighbourhood policy in the context of next year's enlargement. if progress can be made in the areas of economic development, security and human rights in our new eastern and southern neighbours, then in the end it is to our own advantage. unfortunately, the policy proposed is rather vague on a number of essential, concrete points. first and foremost there is the question of the common external borders and the potential immigration problem. the rapporteur hopes that the new schengen borders will not be a barrier to trade, cultural exchanges and regional cooperation. that is a legitimate concern, but we must first of all ensure that the whole concept does not trigger a new wave of immigration. given the high unemployment and the high social costs of the immigration problem, that is the last thing we need. when we see that commission president prodi declared in the parliament of burkina faso last week that the wider europe is that we are going to share everything from moscow to rabat, i quote, except our institutions, then there is cause for concern, ladies and gentlemen. the second problem is the position of turkey, because it was shown during the debate on the report in the committee on foreign affairs that the european commission does not see turkey as part of the neighbourhood policy, because it is a candidate country for accession to the european union. this is in contradiction with the report, which clearly states that the future eu membership of the participating countries is in no way excluded. it should be clear to everyone that the chances of turkey meeting the criteria for accession by the end of next year are virtually nil. turkey is not a european country, but it is a friendly country and a military ally. it is therefore of the utmost importance that our relations with turkey remain optimal and that the country is part of the new neighbourhood policy from the outset.
- (da) mr president, from a purely formal point of view, the three reports we are debating today deal with the question of the legal basis. therefore, the general view was that it was not the budget and its content that needed to be discussed, but rather the need to clarify the legal basis. it is obvious that this clarification is necessary. for two of the reports, it takes the form of article 308, which in a way shows the importance of the latter. we are obliged to have such an article in case we suddenly need to clarify certain things, for example. on the other hand, this same article should not be violated, as we may be inclined to do. however, in reality, the reports go far beyond the mere formal question of clarifying the legal basis. in fact, it is something extremely political, namely, first of all, a power struggle between the institutions of the union and, secondly, our relationship with the citizens of european democracy in europe and outside europe. a lot has already been said on the first point. but i would like to make a general remark: it is striking that the programme for women is the shortest. i am sorry, but i do not think the commission has given a very good explanation, because everything that has been said about the applicant countries applies rightly to the three programmes. i therefore think that as the work proceeds, we should ask ourselves why it is women, as usual, who are in the most vulnerable position. at least that's how i see it. on the second point, i would like to say that i come from a country which has been a member of the european union since 1972. we have occasionally tested the opinion of the public in the union and held many referendums. we know that unless we involve citizens more actively in the union, both in terms of internal democracy and in relations with other countries, the project will not be successful in the long term. it will be beyond the understanding of the citizens and they may end up taking a negative attitude towards it. this is why it is extremely important that we involve citizens in this work. it is a bit troubling that it is precisely women who have the shortest schedules. i would like to reiterate that the experience of my own country shows that it is women who are most sceptical of the project. there may be various explanations for this, but i will not dwell on them now, because i do not have time. but that is precisely why it is so terribly important that women's organisations can also work with a certain degree of stability, so that they do not have to spend all their time trying to find money for their work. i shall now comment on the various reports. the first, as the rapporteur has pointed out in his report, is the union's relations with third countries, an extremely important subject, and we see that it is becoming more and more important every day for everyone to understand that there are other cultures than the one that is most widespread in europe. in this context, i can only wonder whether this programme will really allow the applicant countries to join us on the basis of these criteria. i would ask the commissioner responsible to look into this, because we know that the candidate countries mainly use their development aid for neighbouring countries rather than for more distant states. this is why there may not be much raw material to work on in the candidate countries as regards relations with third countries. the second report is on citizenship, which i have already mentioned, but i would just like to stress that it must be developed constantly in such a way as to ensure that not only ngos but also citizens in general can participate in this work. in this way, the people will better understand the positive things that the european union can bring to them. in this context, it is extremely important that there are not only traditional organisations but also organisations promoting wider cultural initiatives. i think that's a way of doing things. finally, i would like to turn to the question of the women's programme. i worked constructively with the rapporteur before this report was published, but there is a major disagreement within the committee. that is why several of us will vote for the extension of the programme and for various other amendments which the commission has apparently rejected, but which we hope will nevertheless be supported by a majority.

- mr president, the decision notified at the end of september by the european commission's directorate-general for press and communication to suspend operating subsidies for the info-points and the european carrefours still causes deep disappointment and surprise among the managers and staff of the eips. the 2004 operating subsidy decision also includes european union citizens among its victims. let's be honest with each other. at a time when europe is having enormous difficulties in getting closer to its citizens and making itself known to them, an undeniable gap is widening again. the closure of the european hubs, for example, represents a major loss of information in the more rural areas. decentralised structures are an indispensable asset and, in view of the immense communication work to be carried out, these structures must be maintained or even financed more substantially to play their full role. at a time when europe is at a major turning point in its history, with its next enlargement and its next constitution, it would be unacceptable to put an end to the vital and indispensable help of the info-points. in this context, we must pool all our efforts to find a fair solution, because we are all involved. info-points are and must remain one of the community's communication tools. their activities and efforts have proved their effectiveness. the info-points have always been able to mobilise their efforts in relation to the mission entrusted to them, in this case that of being the interface of a dynamic platform. close to the citizens, they have helped to maintain a european social fabric. can we therefore remain deaf and dumb to the expectations of our citizens, who constantly need to be informed of our interinstitutional activities? conscious of this need and convinced of the important role played by these structures, i call on the commission to reconsider its decision and to enable these european information networks to continue the mission we have entrusted to them, which is more necessary than ever. and to answer the commissioner, whom i thank for being here, i would say: it is all the better if there are other documentation centres and forums which have the support of the commission, but the fact remains that the information gap is very real and is felt badly by the citizen. if a legal basis is needed - and i can understand that - then let it be established. but it takes a minimum of time to solve this kind of problem and not four months, commissioner. that seems very short and the commission's procedures appear very uncourtesy. so let us hope that the proposal for a trilogue will be well received.
- president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i thank the commissioner for his answer, because i too believe that the abolition of the provincial info-points would be a loss to the image of the european union. first of all, these information centres are close to the citizen, and this can be taken literally. the information centres are physically present in the province. the proximity of the centres means that people seeking information do not have to travel long distances, and therefore lose less time and cost less. i think this is an asset for the union and a form of customer friendliness. secondly, the european parliament has already expressed its views on the union's information and communication strategy, expressing its desire to bring communication as close as possible to the citizen. in its motion for a resolution it expressed the wish to invest more in the proper functioning of an information network, including the info-points. so read this: invest, not abolish. i quote: 'the european parliament considers it necessary to take measures for the administrative adjustment, structure and training of staff, aimed at the efficient functioning of external offices and the network of information points, which can also be used by members of the european parliament in their respective constituencies.' if we are talking about bringing the union closer to the citizen and bridging the gap with the citizen - because research shows that there is a gap - then investing more in the info points is already part of the solution. i therefore urge that these info points should not be abolished, but rather that they should be improved, especially now, in the light of the elections and the enlargement in 2004. we must not spare on promoting the union, a union which brings peace, security and prosperity to its member states.

mr president, sri lanka is in a cohabitation situation, with a president and a prime minister belonging to two competing political formations. for the past 20 years, every ruling political party has sought to make peace with the tamil tigers, and each time the opposition has thwarted the peace process. the current prime minister, when in opposition, thwarted the president's plan to give more autonomy to the tamils in the north east, where they are the majority. since becoming prime minister, he has made great strides in negotiating with the ltte, with the help of norwegian mediation, finally achieving a ceasefire that has lasted for several months and has changed the lives of the people while allowing a certain economic restart. for the first time since the movement's inception, tamil separatists had just issued concrete proposals for power-sharing in a region that, while self-ruled by tamils, would remain part of sri lanka. these proposals were negotiable, but subsequently the president took the heavy responsibility of stalling this peace process by taking advantage of the prime minister's trip to the united states and by taking advantage of her constitutional prerogatives. it has dissolved parliament and recalled the ministers of defence, interior and information to sack them. this was the situation until a few days ago. today parliament is back in session and the president is renegotiating with her prime minister, so i think we should seize this opportunity. a conciliation commission was even set up between the two branches of the executive. we in our group really believe that the european parliament must very clearly encourage sri lankan politicians to find a solution to the political crisis, but also and above all a constitutional solution that will allow a lasting agreement with the tamils, and we express our full support for the norwegian mediation in its efforts to reconnect the threads of the peace process.

- mr president, the recent events in vietnam have once again illustrated the repression suffered by the monks of the unified buddhist church of vietnam, the ebuv. the vietnamese authorities have placed eleven ebuv dissidents under house arrest for two years, including the buddhist church patriarch, venerable thich huyen quang, who has already spent twenty-one years in prison. this repression followed the first extraordinary assembly of the ebuv since its ban in 1981 by the communist authorities. lebu, a major religion in vietnam, founded decades ago, is still not allowed to exist. the hanoi government has justified these detentions on the grounds of violations of national security legislation. vietnamese law allows local authorities to place individuals in administrative detention for six to 24 months without trial for harming national security. in reality, the vietnamese authorities are contradicting their great declarations in favour of reforms and democracy. let me remind you that religious freedom is a fundamental right of the universal declaration of human rights and the eu-vietnam cooperation agreement is based precisely on respect for fundamental human rights. this policy of repression, not only against the monks of the unified buddhist church, but also against the buddhists of the hoa hao church, against the catholic church, against the christian mountain men, must stop. all these religions are banned and do not enjoy legal status. vietnam is a multiethnic, multicultural and multi-religious country. this diversity is a treasure not to be squandered. the hanoi regime must show good will and implement a series of concrete reforms, starting with those guaranteeing complete freedom of religion, and thus begin a real process of democratization. then we can trust them again.
- the president, madam commissioner, thank you for this important regulation, rapporteur, thank you for the rapid and flexible work and, above all, for the very good cooperation with the political groups. i should also like to thank the council for its constructive approach to this matter. commissioner, there is a small miracle happening here: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. i do not think that this has been demonstrated before in this house. however, the regulation we are discussing today is very important because it significantly improves the rights of citizens who exercise the right of free movement of persons in two ways. first, everyone has the same rights to essential medical care, and that means a serious improvement for a lot of people. so far, students and pensioners have the right to medical care in the member state where they reside if they need it, while workers and their families can only receive the necessary care in this country. so that discrimination is now gone. secondly, the procedures will also be much simpler for the people. now, people living in another member state must take their e 111 form to the local health insurance fund, where they will then receive a new form and can only go to the doctor or dentist. this is hopelessly complicated! at least now they can go directly to the health care provider, which is also a serious improvement. i am also glad, commissioner, that the council and the commission have agreed to the principle that member states should inform their citizens about their rights to health care, because of course they are not unlimited. finally, i hope, together with my colleagues, that this regulation, madam commissioner, will indeed be a further important step towards the introduction of a general european health insurance card which will provide easy access to health care for all european insured persons exercising their right of residence, everywhere in europe.
- as a member of the committee on employment and social affairs, but also as a former government commissioner for administrative simplification in my country, i can of course only welcome the unanimous approval of our committee of the report by our colleague mrs jensen. the introduction of a european health insurance card, which will replace the current forms for obtaining healthcare in another member state, is a major step forward for europeans. i think we all need to be aware of that. the harmonization of procedures will bring greater well-being, it will avoid having to deal with a multitude of paperwork but above all it will ensure real mobility for european citizens. this step is a great example of how effective reorganisation of procedures can be. the famous e 111 form, which is well known to tourists and is required for short stays, will be the first to be abolished. the european card will simplify the lives of our fellow citizens, without changing existing rights and obligations. this is a point on which we have all insisted. this concrete and visible act will strengthen our sense of belonging to the european union and european citizenship. it is clear that very soon we will have to continue our efforts and extend the functions of the card to include emergency medical data and information on the health of the cardholder. let us now welcome this new administrative culture, which will no doubt be appreciated by european citizens who are too often disappointed by the slowness and burdensome nature of our procedures.

- mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in april 2003, during the debate on the liese report at first reading, i was already speaking on behalf of my group on this important issue. i would remind you of three principles which led me to reject many of the amendments. first principle: the draft directive had a scope which should not be widened, especially to avoid reopening the debate on embryonic stem cell research or even on ivf. second principle: while strict ethical rules are needed, religious or sectarian prohibitions are not needed, no matter where they come from. third principle: the human body is not for sale, not for purchase, not for theft. at the time, the european parliament had largely followed these principles when voting and had placed in the minority those who had proposed amendments that could be described as reactionary in the objective sense of the term. in preparing the second reading, the rapporteur, peter liese, who is true to his ideas and always as combative, has again tried to widen the scope of the draft directive to re-launch further debates and perhaps, one day, win a battle that he has regularly lost since the adoption of the sixth eprd. i was therefore preparing myself for a new task tonight on behalf of the vast majority of our fellow citizens, on behalf of a very large number of researchers, on behalf of human health and, above all, on behalf of patients awaiting treatment and transplants. some amendments, which were voted on in committee, did indeed seem to me to be inappropriate, even dangerous. fortunately - is it a christmas present? - the rapporteur proposes 21 rather satisfactory compromise amendments to us this evening. fortunately, they clarify certain provisions of the text. they clearly set out the principles we hold dear: donations are voluntary and free, but compensation is possible. member states shall guarantee such donations; respect for anonymity is reaffirmed, with the reservations of articles 8 and 16 which refer to a certain subsidiarity and to the laws of the states; traceability is documented and validated. i particularly welcome the disappearance of two amendments: amendment no 35, which dealt with abortion, and amendment no 38, which did not distinguish between therapeutic cloning and reproductive cloning. i am therefore able to support, on behalf of my group, the 21 compromise amendments with the council, accompanied by certain legal assessments which i have heard from commissioner byrne, and i am glad that common sense has largely prevailed. i would therefore like to thank the rapporteur, peter liese, who has undoubtedly played a very positive role in drawing up these compromise amendments.
the proposal for a directive which is now on the table is a very watered-down version of what was unfortunately voted down by one vote by the european parliament a year and a half ago. the current proposal leaves a lot of room for manoeuvre to the member states and to the companies themselves, such as the optional or even temporary authorisation of protective structures when a company is faced with what it considers to be a hostile takeover bid. the question is therefore whether transfers at european level will still be possible in this way and whether this directive will actually change the current situation in practice. i think we should approve this proposal. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. there's a flemish proverb that says: "better one bird in the hand than ten in the air". the acquisition directive is indeed an important part of the lisbon agenda for a strong, competitive european union and is essential for further completion of the european single market. we must therefore ensure that this directive enters into force as soon as possible so that we can immediately examine in practice with the operators whether and how we can further develop this system.
- (da) mr president, it is unacceptable that a directive on environmental liability does not include pollution from the use of genetically modified plants (gmos). parliament's review of the directives on seed wheat, traceability and labelling of gmos clearly shows that there is a risk of unintended pollution, even when gmos are used correctly. the problem is pollen pollution and the lost seeds accumulated in the seed bank. experts disagree on how much lost seed can accumulate in the earth's seed bank and for how long. however, pollution by gmos is not included in the proposed environmental liability directive. this is incredible, especially when we know that the polluter pays principle is enshrined in the treaties. i shall therefore support the proposals on gmos made by mr lannoye and others. the polluter pays principle should, in my view, go beyond words. it is time for the european union to live up to its ideals. the union must therefore oblige the member states to tax genetically modified wheat seed and fodder - a tax that will finance control and surveillance and compensate for damage caused by its correct use.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the report on environmental responsibility has led to extremely complex and even emotionally charged debates in the committees responsible. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval. nevertheless, i think that the legal affairs committee and in particular its rapporteur have done an excellent job in trying to reach a more or less acceptable compromise. i am very pleased that the legal affairs committee has accepted my amendment on development risks and the permit defence. both the development risks which are by definition unknown and impossible to discover on the basis of the state of science and the permit defence are incorporated in the directive in the form of a discharge of the operator's liability. i therefore call on the european commission to accept this amendment. it is absolutely essential if we are to promote the innovation and creativity of european firms, especially in relation to their american competitors, where such a system has been in force for a long time. on the other hand, i regret that the text does not emphasize the principle of 'the polluter pays'. the current wording of the directive is very confusing and i think it can lead to legal uncertainty. i think that there is a real legal loophole here.
mr president, like mr karas, i am a supporter of an environmentally-friendly market economy and i am therefore not afraid of a strict liability regime. it can be assumed that this will have a strong preventive effect and can therefore be justified. i just think we should not forget that in the proposal we are discussing here tonight, we are introducing "flawless liability". a faultless liability is taken for granted by some. i personally think that it is a very radical instrument compared with the classical doctrine of liability in the continental legal tradition. and precisely because it is so radical, we must be careful about it and we must ensure that certain preconditions are met, mr president. the commission has also proposed that the scheme be amended to include a new category of aid, namely the 'cross-border' aid, which is intended to cover the costs of the acquisition of the land and the development of the infrastructure. i shall not list the amendments which would be useful in this respect. anyone who has followed the debate will know that i am talking about, among other things, the grounds for exclusion for licence holders, the state of the art arrangements, the grounds for exclusion for people who adhere to good agricultural codes and the replacement of joint and several liability by proportional liability. the manders report, as we voted on in the legal affairs committee, provides for these preconditions. i therefore congratulate the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteur of our group on their work and on their cooperation. i would ask those of you who are troubled by the limitations introduced in the report, and indeed the commission - because i understand that it is also troubled by some of our amendments - to take a hard look at the concept of full liability, to realise the difficult, heavy consequences that it can have, and to ensure that we finally get a system which shows some leniency for economic and equitable reasons. we must of course ensure, mr president, that we have a viable earth, but i think we must also ensure that we have a viable economy and that we still find people who want to do business with us.

- (it) mr president, this document deals with vat - value added tax - on postage stamps. i had a vision: it was 2013, i was still a member of this parliament and mr schmidt was still presenting a report on stamps. however, at the moment - i speak as if it were 2017 - fewer and fewer european citizens use ordinary stamps and postal services, to the extent that mr schmidt's directive provides for subsidising those who write letters by paying for them. what i mean, mr president, is that stamps and postal services are becoming so rare that letters will become antiquated, like the ancestors of the automobile. that is my final explanation of vote for today and for the year 2003. thank you and enjoy!

- mr president, commissioner liikanen, i would first of all like to congratulate my two colleagues, mrs grossetête and mrs müller, on the excellent work they have done on this revision of the pharmaceutical legislation, which is probably one of the most important public health issues of this parliament. so we have on the table today a package of amendments negotiated between the two rapporteurs, the fictitious rapporteurs and the italian presidency. it is a comprehensive, balanced compromise, accepted by the majority of member states, which offers guarantees and advances to promote the growth of both branded and generic medicines. the agreement with the council includes a number of proposals which are very important to us. i will not repeat what has already been said by previous speakers, particularly on pharmacovigilance or the european medicines agency and the centralised procedure. i would, however, like to emphasise the five-year renewal, which will allow national authorities to remove certain medicines from the market if they are no longer really effective; the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules and will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules. waste collection and the environmental impact of medicines; the braille inscription - it has not yet been said - on the packaging for the visually impaired. last but not least, i would like to highlight the broadening of the definition of generic medicines and the introduction of the concept of 'eurogenerics' which will harmonise and facilitate the launch of generics across europe: a considerable step forward in this review, as our rapporteur has pointed out, and one which has not been said enough so far. i am also pleased with the answer given by commissioner liikanen to mrs grossetête's question concerning the so-called 'frontier' products, the status of which actually still needs to be clarified. i now come to what has been, from the very beginning, the real battle in parliament and between member states: the question of data protection. the '8 + 2 + 1' formula must be supported, that is what we need today: a win-win approach, i would say, which will allow the development of research, which is crucial for europe, and better access for patients to cheaper medicines with the impact that is known on the health budgets of our governments. to my colleagues and to the representatives of the four member states who voted against this fair compromise and who continue to make data protection a prime, if not the only, target in their defence of low-cost health care - as if the debate could be confined to this one issue - i would say that the risk they are taking is enormous. they are taking the risk of europe becoming a mere sales counter and then leaving the united states to become the only territory of innovation. i'll call back a number only. since 1996, the share of new medicines launched by europe has fallen from 55% to 28%, or just half, and i am not talking about turnover here but about a deficit in terms of investment in research and development. in conclusion, we must not mistake either the target or the strategy in this debate. if the essential thing for europe - and we all agree on this - is to continue to care, and to care well, for its patients, then we must have a genuine policy of innovation, stop the leakage of our brains, fight against parallel imports and, as i said, promote, not a head-on collision between industry and generics, which would be sterile, but a win-win approach for the greatest benefit of patients. on behalf of the liberal group, which i represent here, i think that is what we have achieved here after two years of joint work.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i am very happy that this parliament, together with the council, has succeeded in making a thorough change to the commission's proposals, which were originally very far-reaching and liberal. it is a compromise, of course, that we are presented with good and less good things. in any case, it is good that all medicines covered by european regulation will have to meet the same criteria of quality, safety and efficacy. it is also very important that commercial advertising should remain prohibited. i would remind you of the commission's original proposals, which sought to allow advertising of aids, asthma and diabetes medicines as a pilot project. i think that information given to patients must be guaranteed to be reliable and objective. it is therefore logical that the government should initially organise and coordinate this flow of information. i am glad that the commission is now being asked to put forward proposals, on the basis of an overview of the current situation, to improve and objectively inform the public, and i believe that this will also include information and sales via the internet. i am less positive, however, about the duration of data protection. the period of protection of medicinal products in europe is very long compared to, for example, the united states or japan. it is good that from the age of eight the generic industry will be able to start some preparatory work to be able to bring generic medicines to market from the age of ten. but i think there are too many possibilities to add another year of protection to that, including when a new indication can be proven. i think that this was unnecessary, mr president. i therefore sincerely hope that the commission will take great care to ensure that these possibilities are not abused. finally, i also regret the absence of an amendment on doha.
- mr president, in a minute i will be caricaturing the work of the rapporteurs and i apologize in advance to them. i recognize everything they did. i would simply like to say that the compromise of giving medicines data protection for eight years, plus two, plus one, is a nice end-of-year gift from the european parliament to the pharmaceutical industry and a recognition of the effectiveness of these powerful lobbies. but this gift will further increase the gap in social security in the member countries by delaying the marketing of generics. why such generosity? the pharmaceutical industry argues that it must fund its research. however, studies have shown that only one fifth of the new medicines introduced to the market over the past 20 years were genuine innovations. and the new york times reports that two-thirds of the drugs approved between 1989 and 2000 were only slight modifications of existing drugs. and if our social security systems urgently need generic drugs, what about the developing countries where 95% of aids victims and many endemic diseases are concentrated? the absence of generics is simply criminal. amendment no 19 in the grossetête report refers to it. please let's vote for it and keep the spirit of doha. let us at least oppose the pharmaceutical lobbies with our solidarity with the third world.
- mr president, for a long time, ivory coast seemed to be a country with a promising future. it was one of the so-called model countries in africa in terms of stability and economic growth. ivory coast has gradually been drawn into a cycle of violence, especially since the introduction of "ivoirité", a concept that is actually similar to apartheid. it is clear that the ruling political class, including the president, is capable of anything to strengthen its power and does not even hesitate to go down the road of ethnic cleansing. the parallels with what happened 10 years ago on the eve of the genocide in rwanda are disturbing. of course there is no immediate alternative to the marcoussis agreements, but mrs kinnock rightly argues that they should be evaluated and adjusted where necessary. however, the main problem is its enforceability. the international community can no longer be reduced to lip service here. the european union, commissioner, must consider all possible initiatives and instruments, including the suspension of aid to côte d'ivoire until the government is ready to implement the linas-marcoussis agreements fully and consistently. today's silence and passive observation may be interpreted as passive complicity tomorrow. let us avoid a repeat of rwanda.
- mr president, on the night of 11 to 12 december, new deadly clashes struck the ivory coast. these clashes between ivorian soldiers and unknown armed men have raised fears that the peace process in côte d'ivoire could be blocked. these events occurred at a time when the forces of the former rebellion had decided to announce their imminent return to the instances of the government of national reconciliation. unfortunately, we see that the proliferation of militias is a very dangerous consequence of the conflict. many testimonies collected by human rights watch report on multiple abuses committed with impunity by pro-government militias. as the country was about to take a decisive step towards peace, this new outbreak of violence - and in an extremely turbulent context - has renewed the concern of the defenders of the linas-marcoussis agreements. these agreements, it should be recalled, were aimed at bringing an end to hostilities, ensuring a return to peace and stability and preserving the territorial and ethnic integrity of côte d'ivoire. in the light of the linas-marcoussis agreements, the current priority is the need to continue the process of disarmament and reintegration of the forces present in côte d'ivoire. that is why the ivorian government, and especially president gbagbo, must ensure that the rights and freedoms of the citizens are protected and that hostilities are brought to an end as soon as possible, otherwise we may be forced to ask for the suspension of aid to côte d'ivoire, which is, as we know, indispensable to the ivorian people. we are afraid, mr commissioner, that the union's aid is unfortunately largely used to buy arms. so let us be careful, not to say naive or complicit in another genocide.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to take this opportunity of discussing this report to emphasize that there must be a distinction between the harmonization of toll systems and the uniform imposition of tolls as a tax. i am speaking here, on the one hand, of the technical aspects being discussed in the effort to achieve the interoperability of electronic toll systems throughout the union. no sane person will oppose this. i do think that this report avoids some important political questions which arise in the discussion of this dossier. a more comprehensive discussion would therefore have been desirable. i would stress in particular that it is for the member states themselves to decide whether a toll system for lorries and passenger cars is desirable. it seems quite logical to me that in a peripheral or insular member state, such as portugal or malta, economic and political considerations will be different tomorrow after accession than in flanders, for example, which is situated between germany, france, the netherlands and great britain and is, as it were, the central transport hub of the european union. the flemish motorways are threatened with becoming congested tomorrow by the exponentially increasing freight traffic. in order to stimulate rail and sea transport, for example, flanders must therefore be able to apply different criteria than other member states, if it wants to introduce toll charges for freight transport tomorrow - because there are none in my country today. the same applies to tolls on passenger transport, where so many different factors such as tourism, traffic density, air pollution, economic structures, etc. may play a role in determining the desirability of such tolls. each government must retain the right to decide for itself whether the already heavily taxed motorists - and this is about people who work and keep an economy running - should be taxed even more. europe can now harmonize the technical aspects of - and i want to emphasize this - existing toll systems. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
thank you, mr president. i would also like to thank the rapporteur, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, for her work and for the willingness to talk she has always shown on a dossier which is certainly not easy. the temptation is to take the toll as a matter of course, because there are so many aspects to the toll. it can regulate for the environment, it can advocate for safety, it can - mr. president, it was not easy to reach an agreement on the ecopoints. i would like to thank my colleague paolo costa for the effort he has made and also his predecessor, mr caveri, as chairman of the committee on transport, who has also worked very hard on this. it is true that a great deal of effort has been put into finding a compromise and transforming a transitional system, which was granted on the accession of austria, into a more general system. i think it is not possible to make a permanent exception for one member state in this area, however much i understand the specific problems of austria and the way in which it is trying to find an answer to those specific problems. but it is clear that there must be a balance between general environmental problems and the geographical situation in austria on the one hand and the free movement of goods on the other. i think the balance between the two is not easy. we can sell very nice things about that in theory, but in practice sometimes you have to make a tough decision. i think we should also look at the whole thing in the longer term and try to find a solution for traffic across the alps in general. i think this is very important. we must also take this into account in the context of the cocilovo report on the eurovignette. but in any case, for us, the agreement reached through mediation is an acceptable agreement. my group will approve it. what concerns me a little, mr president, are the statements made by the austrian minister after the conciliation. i think that such statements are making things more difficult than they are helping to solve them.
vice-president of the commission. (es) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the commission does not intend to put forward a proposal for a community-wide ban on night flights for a number of reasons. firstly, the noise pollution situation is not the same in all european airports. the commission notes that the airport services agreement concluded between ryanair and ams was signed on the same day as the airport services agreement signed with ryanair on the same day. given that the introduction of a night flight ban has an impact on the economy and employment, the balance between the various elements must be carefully considered, which requires a case-by-case cost-benefit analysis for each airport before such a ban is imposed. secondly, unilateral measures may isolate europe from the international aviation network; the night closure of all airports in the european union may seriously hamper the normal operation of this international network. in many cases, it is inevitable that flight operations take place at night, especially intercontinental flights, due to the existence of different international time zones and types of cargo. however, the commission is monitoring noise problems and their impact on citizens living in the vicinity of airports. the commission therefore consistently insisted in the international context of icao that decisions be taken to accommodate solutions to address the specific needs of the union in the field of airport noise. on the basis of the decisions taken at the 33rd icao assembly in 2001 establishing the framework for introducing restrictions, including those on night flights, the commission presented a proposal for a directive, which was adopted in march 2002. this directive allows, by means of a preliminary examination and consultation procedure, the imposition of night flight bans at community airports where this is justified on the basis of objective criteria.
- madam commissioner, i wonder where you will be staying when you are in brussels. i don't suppose you live in the north rim. i think you live in a place where there are very few night flights, otherwise you would give a different answer. the commissioner, do you understand that if there is no general european ban on night flights, then there will be competition between airports, and downward competition whereby acceptance of noise is exchanged for jobs? do you realize that you are thus catching up with an economy that is a hole-in-the-wall economy, an economy where more and more needs to be done, faster and faster, and where the quality of life is no longer the central issue? the standards of the world health organization are clear: noise at night leads to more irritability, noise at night leads to melancholy. i would ask you to reconsider your intervention and perhaps invite you to spend an evening, a night with me in the northern suburbs of brussels, so that you can see what these people have to go through day after day, night after night.
the rapporteur. - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to say that i think this report is absolutely fundamental. it is fundamental because one of the main objectives of the european union's development policy is to promote the eradication of poverty in developing countries. we also believe that the consolidation of democracy and the rule of law, as well as respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms, are goals to be achieved. it would be an illusion to think that the fight against poverty can be waged without the participation of women. women are the pillars of society, especially of the societies concerned. at the same time, we must recognise that women and children are among the most vulnerable to disease, poverty, illiteracy and war. so all the strategies we implement and all the actions we want to develop must contribute to the empowerment and action of women in all fields and in all countries. women must play a very active role in the development of their countries, because every woman is a potential actor of success, change and development. the report under discussion today is, i hope, one of the fundamental steps in this approach to cooperation, because it contains many measures to promote, develop and support concrete actions in this direction. concrete is the word that matters to us. we want this regulation to be a catalyst, a driver of gender equality initiatives, valuing pilot initiatives, developing and disseminating good practice. it covers 2004-2006. we therefore believe it is imperative that the regulation can be applied as from january and that calls for tenders for projects can be launched. because i think that hundreds of people on the ground are waiting for our decision and that if we delay, the consequences will be damaging for all those on the ground who want to participate and act in a concrete way in favour of equal opportunities. in this report, i found it essential to focus on women of all ages' access to all resources and services such as health, education, training, infrastructure. it should also be noted that women are excluded from participation, access and control in all these sectors. as a woman, but especially as a democrat, i hope to be able to encourage this participation in all the countries concerned. i also hope that the main beneficiaries of our support will be the field actors and women's organisations who work tirelessly to change mentalities in favour of gender equality. but in everything we say, in everything we want, there is the budgetary aspect. the european commission is giving us nine million for this regulation. this may not be enough for us, but it is a real asset for those on the ground to carry out concrete projects. anyway, we wanted to ask for 11 million. eleven million, because we feel that two million more is not much. however, as commissioner nielson has just explained, it seems that this is not possible. i had therefore accepted the report at first reading, with the support of my colleagues, in order to promote action in a pragmatic way, that is to say, to get this regulation adopted as soon as possible, tomorrow in this case, so that it could come into force as from january 2004. it seems that the other political groups do not share this view. it seems that we must fight again, because we might still be able to collect a few cents and a few francs and that counts on the ground. i would, however, ask all those involved in the debate we will have tomorrow in parliament to consider that we are taking on a clear and unequivocal responsibility to start a second reading process for which we do not know the timetable. i also believe that all the organisations involved in the fight have the right to expect us, the council and the commission, to show maturity and a concrete sign of our willingness to see these objectives achieved. as for me, if i still have the power to decide as rapporteur, i will again be advocating that this text should enter into force as from january 2004. in any case, if the negotiations which we will be conducting again tonight and tomorrow force us at some point to reverse our position, i will only regret it, but i will immediately request a meeting with the council and the commission so that we can reach an agreement as soon as possible.
the efficiency of development aid, mr president, remains a problem. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the community. more and more observers and actors on the ground are becoming critical of the way development aid is usually organised. the resources used often do not reach the people for whom they are intended. besides, simply giving money is not always the answer. on the contrary, there are other factors that contribute to success. i am thinking, for example, of the existence of a system of free market economy and the existence of freedom tout court. the report also calls for the establishment of a european network of national and regional authorities responsible for the implementation of the directive. it is rightly said that discrimination against women is an obstacle to sustainable and effective development. the commission has already made a number of proposals to improve the quality of education and training in the member states. the commission shall provide for this, inter alia, 'support for the analysis and improvement of gender and age-related statistics, development and dissemination of methodologies, guidelines, gender impact reports, thematic studies, indicators and other operational tools'. all of this is undoubtedly useful and necessary, but we must be careful not to lose sight of some essential facts. many third world countries are plagued by archaic cultural patterns that are directly responsible for the disadvantage of women. in this respect it is surprising that neither the zrihen report nor the commission report contains even the slightest reference to islam. in the islamic world, women are, as it were, structurally inferior. it is therefore pointless to make a big deal out of this unpleasant fact. as long as this is not acknowledged, this regulation will only be a source of employment for gender experts and other development workers from europe, and nothing more.
mr president, the socialist members of the french community of belgium and other colleagues have asked me to explain why they voted against. parliament is not honouring itself by continuing to blindly defend a draft constitution which does nothing to fill the democratic deficit it has been denouncing for more than ten years, but which is shining through with various flagrant shortcomings, starting with the social field and the non-governance of the euro. parliament is also not honouring itself by concealing, against all evidence, that the convention has proved dramatically incapable of achieving a lasting consensus, nor by concealing the responsibility of the commission and its president, who masked their opposition at the time of the consensus in order to reveal it better later, when they had chosen to remain silent on the treaty of nice. we also believe that parliament would do better to denounce the emergence of a european g6, whose clumsy intervention only complicates an already difficult debate. finally, like others, we have high hopes for the irish presidency, but we feel that a parliament is making a fool of itself by asking for the impossible.
